The target feature is handled in the same perturbative form, starting from \[B(0)= \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[0.2em]1/5&4/5\end{pmatrix}.\] For every \(\upsilon\in\mathbb R^2\), \[25\left(\|B(0)\upsilon\|_2^2-\frac1{25}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\right) = 25\upsilon_1^2+58\upsilon_1\upsilon_2+40\upsilon_2^2 = \frac{159(3\upsilon_1-4\upsilon_2)^2+(187\upsilon_1+236\upsilon_2)^2}{1456}\ge0,\] so \[\sigma_{\min}(B(0))\ge\frac15.\] With \[\chi_B(h):=\frac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h}-\frac45,\] we have \[B(h)=B(0)+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&\chi_B(h)\end{pmatrix}, \qquad \chi_B(h)=\frac{(3/5)h}{3/5-h}.\] The bound \(|h|\le1/100\) gives \(3/5-h\ge59/100\), hence \[|\chi_B(h)| \le \frac{(3/5)(1/100)}{59/100} = \frac{3}{295} \le \frac1{50}.\] Also \[\left\| \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&\chi\end{pmatrix} \right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le|\chi|\] for any real \(\chi\), since its action sends \(\upsilon\) to \((0,\chi\upsilon_2)^\top\). Another application of Weyl’s inequality gives \[\sigma_{\min}(B(h)) \ge \sigma_{\min}(B(0))-\frac1{50} \ge \frac15-\frac1{50} \ge \frac1{10}.\]

  5. Combining the three estimates, \[\min\{\sigma_{\min}(A_0),\sigma_{\min}(A_1),\sigma_{\min}(B)\} = \min\{\sigma_{\min}(A_0(h)),\sigma_{\min}(A_1(h)),\sigma_{\min}(B(h))\} \ge \frac1{10}.\] This is the proxy-rank margin of Assumption 10 with \(\sigma_0=1/10\).

∎

These linear-algebra statements justify the rank thresholding and anchored diagonalization used by the lattice estimator. The condition-number bound provides an explicit finite-dimensional constant for perturbing between feature coordinates and the compressed spectral coordinates.

The Wasserstein comparisons use a one-dimensional dual potential and the coordinate relation between the Euclidean summary metric and \(d_S\).

Lemma 14 [lem:path-model-membership] (Path model membership).

Let \(g,h\in\mathbb R\). Suppose that

  • (Gap range.) \(0\le g\le 1/4\).

  • (Path displacement.) \(|h|\le 1/100\).

Then the path law \(P_{g,h}\) of Definition 51 belongs to the uniformly conditioned model class \(\mathcal M\) of Definition 32 with \[k=d_x=d_z=2,\qquad L=2,\qquad \pi_0=1/10,\qquad \sigma_0=1/10 .\] Equivalently, \(P_{g,h}\) satisfies the core numerical domain for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), together with Assumptions 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, and 10, using latent-arm positivity margin \(1/10\) and proxy-rank margin \(1/10\).

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 14.

  1. Write the two latent classes as \(u=1,2\), and write \(t,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}\) for the binary coordinates in the finite support of the path. Put \[p_1=\frac25+h,\qquad p_2=\frac35-h,\] and \[\begin{array}{c|cc} &u=1&u=2\\ \hline w_1(u)&\frac4{25}+\frac{3h}{5}&\frac9{25}-\frac{3h}{5}\\[0.25em] w_0(u)&\frac6{25}+\frac{2h}{5}&\frac6{25}-\frac{2h}{5}. \end{array}\] For a Bernoulli coordinate \(r\in\{0,1\}\) and success probability \(\theta\), set \[\operatorname{bm}(r;\theta) = \begin{cases} 1-\theta,& r=0,\\ \theta,& r=1. \end{cases}\] The path assigns the atom \((u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1)\) mass \[p_u\, \operatorname{bm}\!\left(t;\frac{w_1(u)}{p_u}\right) \operatorname{bm}(x;\beta_u) \operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u}) \operatorname{bm}\!\left(y_0;\frac14\right) \operatorname{bm}(y_1;\eta_u),\] where \[\beta_1=\frac15,\qquad \beta_2=\frac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h},\] \[\alpha_{1,1}=\frac{225h+28}{20(15h+4)},\quad \alpha_{1,2}=\frac34,\quad \alpha_{0,1}=\frac{35h+9}{10(5h+3)},\quad \alpha_{0,2}=\frac7{10},\] and \[\eta_1=\frac12-\frac g2,\qquad \eta_2=\frac12+\frac g2 .\] Since \(|h|\le 1/100\), \[\frac25+h\ge\frac{39}{100},\qquad \frac35-h\ge\frac{59}{100},\qquad 15h+4\ge\frac{77}{20},\qquad 5h+3\ge\frac{59}{20}.\] A direct check of the two latent classes gives \[0\le \frac{w_1(u)}{p_u}\le1,\qquad 0\le \beta_u\le1,\qquad 0\le \alpha_{t,u}\le1,\] and \(0\le\eta_u\le1\) follows from \(0\le g\le1/4\). Thus every displayed atom mass is nonnegative. Summing first over the Bernoulli success and failure values gives \[\sum_{u,t,x,z,y_0,y_1} p_u\, \operatorname{bm}\!\left(t;\frac{w_1(u)}{p_u}\right) \operatorname{bm}(x;\beta_u) \operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u}) \operatorname{bm}\!\left(y_0;\frac14\right) \operatorname{bm}(y_1;\eta_u) = p_1+p_2=1 .\] Hence \(P_{g,h}\) is a probability law on the full-data record space with \(k=d_x=d_z=2\).

  2. The numerical core domain in Definition 32 is \[2\le2,\qquad 2\le2,\qquad 2\le2,\qquad 1\le2,\qquad 0<\frac1{10}\le\frac1{2\cdot2},\qquad 0<\frac1{10}\le1 .\]

  3. For Assumption 1, fix a latent-arm cell \((U=u,T=t)\). For bounded measurable test functions \(\varphi_Z\) of \(Z\) and \(\psi_{XY}\) of \((X,Y)\), the finite conditional sums over the atom weights factor as \[E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \varphi_Z(Z)\psi_{XY}(X,Y)\mid U=u,T=t \right] = E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \varphi_Z(Z)\mid U=u,T=t \right] E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \psi_{XY}(X,Y)\mid U=u,T=t \right].\] Indeed, conditional on \((U=u,T=t)\), the factor depending on \(z\) is \(\operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u})\), while the factors determining \(X\) and \(Y\) are functions of \(x,y_0,y_1\) and the fixed arm \(t\). The normalizing denominator is the same finite latent-arm mass in all three conditional means, so expanding the four binary nuisance sums gives the displayed product identity.

  4. For Assumption 2, fix \(U=u\). For bounded measurable test functions \(\varphi_X\) of \(X\) and \(\psi_{YT}\) of \((Y,T)\), \[E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \varphi_X(X)\psi_{YT}(Y,T)\mid U=u \right] = E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \varphi_X(X)\mid U=u \right] E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \psi_{YT}(Y,T)\mid U=u \right].\] After conditioning on \(U=u\), summing over \(z\) uses \[\sum_{z=0}^1\operatorname{bm}(z;\alpha_{t,u})=1\] for each arm \(t\). The remaining finite sum separates the \(x\)-factor \(\operatorname{bm}(x;\beta_u)\) from the factors depending on \(t,y_0,y_1\), and hence on \((Y,T)\).

  5. For Assumption 4, fix a treatment state \(r\in\{0,1\}\) and a latent class \(u\). For bounded measurable test functions \(\varphi_r\) of the potential outcome \(Y(r)\) and \(\psi_T\) of \(T\), \[E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \varphi_r(Y(r))\psi_T(T)\mid U=u \right] = E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \varphi_r(Y(r))\mid U=u \right] E_{P_{g,h}}\!\left[ \psi_T(T)\mid U=u \right].\] This is the same finite-product calculation: the Bernoulli factor for \(Y(r)\) depends on \(u\) and \(r\), while the Bernoulli factor for \(T\) is \(\operatorname{bm}(t;w_1(u)/p_u)\).

  6. Every support point of \(P_{g,h}\) records the observed outcome as \(Y=Y(T)\), which gives Assumption 3. Every support point also has \(X=(1,x)^\top\), which gives \(X_1=1\) and hence Assumption 5.

  7. The boundedness conditions hold supportwise. Since \(X=(1,x)^\top\) and \(Z=(1,z)^\top\) with \(x,z\in\{0,1\}\), \[\|X\|_2\le\sqrt2\le2.\] For every vector \(v\), \[\|ZX^\top v\|_2 = \|Z\|_2\,|X^\top v| \le \|Z\|_2\,\|X\|_2\,\|v\|_2 \le 2\|v\|_2,\] so \(\|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2\). Since \(Y\in\{0,1\}\), \[\|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le \|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2 .\] Thus Assumptions 6, 7, and 8 hold with \(L=2\).

  8. The latent-arm cell masses are precisely the four displayed values \(w_t(u)\). From \(|h|\le1/100\), \[\frac4{25}+\frac{3h}{5}\ge\frac4{25}-\frac3{500}\ge\frac1{10},\qquad \frac9{25}-\frac{3h}{5}\ge\frac9{25}-\frac3{500}\ge\frac1{10},\] and \[\frac6{25}+\frac{2h}{5}\ge\frac6{25}-\frac1{250}\ge\frac1{10},\qquad \frac6{25}-\frac{2h}{5}\ge\frac6{25}-\frac1{250}\ge\frac1{10}.\] Therefore \[\min_{u\in\{1,2\},\,t\in\{0,1\}} P_{g,h}(U=u,T=t) \ge \frac1{10},\] which is Assumption 9 with \(\pi_0=1/10\).

  9. By Lemma 13, \[\min\{\sigma_2(A_0),\sigma_2(A_1),\sigma_2(B)\}\ge\frac1{10}\] for the reference- and target-proxy feature matrices formed from \(P_{g,h}\). Hence Assumption 10 holds with \(\sigma_0=1/10\).

  10. Combining the core domain with the model restrictions just verified gives \[P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M\] for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\), as required by Definition 32.

∎
Lemma 15 [lem:path-gap-stratum-membership] (Path stratum membership).

For real numbers \(g\) and \(h\), suppose that

  • (Gap scale.) The local effect-gap scale satisfies \(0<g\le 1/4\).

  • (Path displacement.) The path displacement satisfies \(|h|\le 1/100\).

Then the path law \(P_{g,h}\) of Definition 51 belongs to the gap-local stratum \(\mathcal M(g)\) of Definition 41, with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). Equivalently, \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M\) as specified by Definition 32, Assumption 6, Assumption 9, and Assumption 10, its smallest positive effect gap \(\delta(P_{g,h})\) from Definition 27 satisfies the gap window of Assumption 11, \[\frac{g}{2}\le \delta(P_{g,h})\le 2g,\] and its latent effects from Definition 23 are distinct as in Assumption 12.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 15.

Write \(P=P_{g,h}\). The hypothesis \(0<g\le 1/4\) gives \(0\le g\). Also \(|h|\le 1/100\) gives \[-\frac1{100}\le h\le \frac1{100}, \qquad\text{hence}\qquad -1\le h\le 1 .\] Thus the detailed path construction in Definition 50 applies throughout the tangent-amplitude domain and yields the full-data probability law \(P_{g,h}\).

Under the same bounds \(0\le g\le 1/4\) and \(|h|\le1/100\), Lemma 14 gives \[P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M\] for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). This supplies the model restrictions in Definition 32, including Assumptions 6, 9, and 10.

It remains to check the gap-local conditions. By the explicit construction in Definition 50, the latent-class masses are \[p_1(P)=\frac25+h, \qquad p_2(P)=\frac35-h .\] The displacement bound gives \[p_1(P)\ge \frac25-\frac1{100}=\frac{39}{100}>0, \qquad p_2(P)\ge \frac35-\frac1{100}=\frac{59}{100}>0 .\] These are the positive-mass classes entering Definition 27 and Assumption 12.

Again by Definition 50, the latent conditional potential-outcome means are \[\mu_{01}=\mu_{02}=\frac14, \qquad \mu_{11}=\frac12-\frac g2, \qquad \mu_{12}=\frac12+\frac g2 .\] Using Definition 23, the corresponding latent effects are \[\tau_1(P)=\mu_{11}-\mu_{01} =\frac14-\frac g2, \qquad \tau_2(P)=\mu_{12}-\mu_{02} =\frac14+\frac g2 .\]

Since \(g>0\), these two effects are distinct, and their distance is \[|\tau_1(P)-\tau_2(P)| = \left| \left(\frac14-\frac g2\right) - \left(\frac14+\frac g2\right) \right| = |-g| = g .\] The same value is obtained with the two classes interchanged, while equal-index pairs are excluded by the condition \(\tau_u\ne\tau_v\) in Definition 27. Hence \[\bigl\{|\tau_u(P)-\tau_v(P)|: p_u(P)>0,\ p_v(P)>0,\ \tau_u(P)\ne\tau_v(P)\bigr\} = \{g\},\] and therefore \[\delta(P)=g .\]

Consequently, \[\frac g2\le \delta(P)\le 2g,\] because \(\delta(P)=g\) and \(g>0\). Also, \[\tau_1(P)=\frac14-\frac g2 < \frac14+\frac g2 =\tau_2(P),\] so the positive-mass latent effects take exactly \(2=k\) distinct values. This is Assumption 12.

Combining \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M\), the gap domain \(0<g\le1/4\), the displayed gap window, and distinct effects gives \[P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)\] with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\), as required by Definition 41.

∎
Lemma 16 [lem:path-local-weight-experiment] (Path local weight membership).

Let \(n\in\mathbb N\), let \(c_{\mathrm{loc}}\in\mathbb R\) be the local Kullback–Leibler radius constant, and let \(g,h\in\mathbb R\). Assume:

  • (Local radius.) \(0<c_{\mathrm{loc}}<1\), as in Definition 49.

  • (Gap scale.) The local effect-gap scale \(g\) is in the gap window of Assumption 11, with \(0<g\le 1/4\).

  • (Path displacement.) The path displacement satisfies \(|h|\le 1/100\).

  • (Observed KL calibration.) \[16000\,g^2h^2\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}.\]

Then the path law \(P_{g,h}\) from Definition 51 belongs to the local labeled-weight experiment \(\mathcal L_{n,g}^p\) of Definition 57 with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). Equivalently, \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)\) as in Definition 41, and its observed-data marginal from Definition 20 obeys the neighborhood condition of Assumption 14: \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\,\Vert\,(P_{g,0})_O\right) \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}, \qquad P_{g,0}=P^{\mathrm{wit}}_{g/2}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 16.

The bound \(|h|\le 1/100\) gives \(-1/100\le h\le 1/100\), hence also \(h\in[-1,1]\). On this range the latent masses \[\frac25+h,\qquad \frac35-h\] belong to \([0,1]\). The treatment Bernoulli probabilities are obtained by dividing \[\frac4{25}+\frac35h,\qquad \frac9{25}-\frac35h\] by the corresponding latent masses, and the same bounds on \(h\) make each numerator nonnegative and no larger than its denominator. The target-proxy probabilities \[\frac15,\qquad \frac{12/25-h/5}{3/5-h}\] also lie in \([0,1]\). For the reference-proxy probabilities, \(|h|\le1/100\) gives positive denominators and \[0\le \frac{225h+28}{20(15h+4)} \le 1, \qquad 0\le \frac{35h+9}{10(5h+3)} \le 1,\] while the remaining displayed values \(3/4\) and \(7/10\) already lie in \([0,1]\). Finally \(1/4\) and \(1/2\pm g/2\) are Bernoulli probabilities because \(0\le g\le 1/4\). Thus all finite product weights defining \(P_{g,h}\) in Definition 50 are nonnegative, and summing those product weights over \[u\in\{1,2\},\qquad t,x,z,y_0,y_1\in\{0,1\}\] leaves the two latent masses, whose sum is one. Hence \(P_{g,h}\) is a probability law. The same argument applies at \(h=0\), and Definition 50 gives \[P_{g,0}=P^{\mathrm{wit}}_{g/2}.\]

By Lemma 15, the hypotheses \(0<g\le 1/4\) and \(|h|\le 1/100\) imply \[P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)\] with \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). The local-radius hypothesis supplies \[0<c_{\mathrm{loc}}<1 .\]

It remains to verify the local observed-neighborhood inequality. Let \[\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}:=\{0,1\}^4 .\] For \((\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\in\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}\), define \[\operatorname{vis}_{g,h}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y) = \begin{cases} \displaystyle \sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{\bar y_0\in\{0,1\}} P_{g,h}(U=u,T=1,X_2=\bar x,Z_2=\bar z,Y(0)=\bar y_0,Y(1)=\bar y), & \bar t=1,\\[0.9em] \displaystyle \sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{\bar y_1\in\{0,1\}} P_{g,h}(U=u,T=0,X_2=\bar x,Z_2=\bar z,Y(0)=\bar y,Y(1)=\bar y_1), & \bar t=0 . \end{cases}\] Let \(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\) be the atomic law on \(\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}\) with these singleton masses. The map \[\Psi_{\mathrm{vis}}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y) = \bigl(\bar t,(1,\bar x)^\top,(1,\bar z)^\top,\bar y\bigr)\] pushes \(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\) to the observed margin: \[(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h})\circ\Psi_{\mathrm{vis}}^{-1}=(P_{g,h})_O .\] Therefore data processing gives \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\,\Vert\,(P_{g,0})_O\right) \le D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\,\Vert\,\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\right).\]

At \(h=0\), direct expansion of the sixteen visible cells gives \[\begin{array}{c|cc} \bar t=0,\;(\bar x,\bar z) & \bar y=0 & \bar y=1\\ \hline (0,0) & 279/2500 & 93/2500\\ (0,1) & 171/2500 & 57/2500\\ (1,0) & 171/2500 & 57/2500\\ (1,1) & 279/2500 & 93/2500 \end{array} \qquad \begin{array}{c|cc} \bar t=1,\;(\bar x,\bar z) & \bar y=0 & \bar y=1\\ \hline (0,0) & (253+163g)/5000 & (253-163g)/5000\\ (0,1) & (247-23g)/5000 & (247+23g)/5000\\ (1,0) & (29-16g)/625 & (29+16g)/625\\ (1,1) & (71-64g)/625 & (71+64g)/625 . \end{array}\] Since \(0\le g\le 1/4\), every entry in these two tables is at least \(1/1000\). Hence \[\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\}\ge \frac1{1000} \qquad \text{for every }(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\in\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}},\] and \(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\ll\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\).

For each visible cell, the same finite expansion gives the exact displacement \[\operatorname{vis}_{g,h}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y) - \operatorname{vis}_{g,0}(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y) = \begin{cases} (1-2\bar y)\,\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(\bar x,\bar z)\,g h, & \bar t=1,\\ 0,& \bar t=0, \end{cases}\] where \[\beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(0,0)=\frac3{25},\qquad \beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(0,1)=\frac9{25},\qquad \beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(1,0)=\frac3{100},\qquad \beta_{\mathrm{vis}}(1,1)=\frac9{100}.\] All four coefficients are at most one, and \(g\ge0\), so \[\left| \mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\{(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\} - \mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{(\bar t,\bar x,\bar z,\bar y)\} \right| \le g|h|\] for every visible cell.

For finite probability laws \(\mathfrak p\ll\mathfrak q\) with positive base mass on every cell, \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathfrak p\Vert\mathfrak q) \le \chi^2(\mathfrak p,\mathfrak q), \qquad \chi^2(\mathfrak p,\mathfrak q) = \sum_{\upsilon\in\mathsf{Cell}_{\mathrm{vis}}} \frac{(\mathfrak p\{\upsilon\}-\mathfrak q\{\upsilon\})^2}{\mathfrak q\{\upsilon\}},\] using \(\log r\le r-1\) for the first inequality. Applying this with \(\mathfrak p=\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\) and \(\mathfrak q=\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\), the preceding floor and displacement bound yield, cell by cell, \[\frac{ \left(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\{\upsilon\}-\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{\upsilon\}\right)^2 }{ \mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\{\upsilon\} } \le 1000\,g^2h^2 .\] There are \(16\) visible cells, and therefore \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(\mathsf{Vis}_{g,h}\,\Vert\,\mathsf{Vis}_{g,0}\right) \le 16000\,g^2h^2 .\]

Combining data processing with the last display and the assumed calibration gives \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\,\Vert\,(P_{g,0})_O\right) \le 16000\,g^2h^2 \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}.\] Using \(P_{g,0}=P^{\mathrm{wit}}_{g/2}\), this is exactly the neighborhood condition in Assumption 14. Together with \(P_{g,h}\in\mathcal M(g)\), it proves \[P_{g,h}\in\mathcal L_{n,g}^p\] as defined in Definition 57.

∎

The potential lemma is the dual device behind the \(W_1\) modulus for represented atomic laws. The two summary-metric lemmas place the finite-dimensional summary topology and the selection loss on a common continuous footing.

The next group records the witness and path bookkeeping used in the lower-bound section. The statements give explicit masses, effects, observed arm probabilities, determinant margins, model membership, and local-experiment membership for the two-class laws.

Definition 62 [synth_26] (Ordered aggregate mass map).

For a labelled representative \(\xi=\sum_{i=1}^k w_i\delta_{x_i}\in\mathcal P_{\le k}([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\), define \(\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)\in\mathbb R^k\) as follows. If every labelled weight is positive and the labelled atoms are distinct, then its \(j\)th coordinate is the mass of the atom whose support rank is \(j\): \[\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)_j :=\sum_{i=1}^k w_i\mathbf 1\!\left\{\#\{\ell:x_\ell<x_i\}=j\right\}, \qquad j=0,\ldots,k-1.\] If some labelled weight is zero or two labelled atoms coincide, set \(\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi):=(1/k,\ldots,1/k)\).

Lemma 17 [lem:ordered-masses-measure-invariance] (Ordered masses are representative-invariant).

Fix \(k\in\mathbb N\) and a real support radius. Let \(\nu\) and \(\xi\) be valid labelled atomic representatives with \(k\) slots in the corresponding compact interval, as in Definition 29. If \(\nu\) and \(\xi\) represent the same aggregated probability measure, then their ordered-mass vectors coincide: \[\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)=\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi).\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 17.

Write the two labelled representatives as \[\nu=\bigl((\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}, (\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\bigr), \qquad \xi=\bigl((\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}, (\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak i})_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\bigr), \qquad I_k:=\{0,\ldots,k-1\}.\] Validity means \[0\le \varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i},\quad 0\le \varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i},\quad \sum_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}=1,\quad \sum_{\mathfrak i\in I_k}\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i}=1,\] with all labelled atoms lying in the prescribed compact interval. For any labelled representative \(\zeta=((\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}),(\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}))\), put \[\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta) := \{\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}:\mathfrak i\in I_k,\ 0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}.\] If \(k=0\), there are no vector coordinates to check.

  1. The equality of represented probability measures gives equality of aggregate mass at every real point: \[\sum_{\mathfrak i:\,\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}=\mathfrak x}\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i} = \sum_{\mathfrak j:\,\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\mathfrak x}\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j} \qquad\text{for every }\mathfrak x\in\mathbb R .\] Indeed, by Definitions 29 and 6, the represented measure assigns \(\{\mathfrak x\}\) the aggregate of the positive parts of the labelled weights at \(\mathfrak x\); validity gives \(\max\{\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i},0\}=\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\) and \(\max\{\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j},0\}=\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}\). Evaluating the common represented measure on \(\{\mathfrak x\}\) gives the displayed identity.

  2. Consequently, \[\operatorname{supp}_+(\nu)=\operatorname{supp}_+(\xi).\] If \(\mathfrak x=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\) and \(0<\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\), then the aggregate mass of \(\nu\) at \(\mathfrak x\) is positive. By [pf:ordered-masses:aggregate], the aggregate mass of \(\xi\) at \(\mathfrak x\) is positive, and nonnegativity of the labelled weights yields some \(\mathfrak j\in I_k\) with \[\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\mathfrak x, \qquad 0<\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.\] This proves one inclusion, and the reverse inclusion follows by interchanging \(\nu\) and \(\xi\).

  3. For a representative \(\zeta\), define the rank of a labelled slot by \[\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i) := \#\{\mathfrak q\in I_k:\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak q}<\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}.\] The counted set excludes \(\mathfrak i\), so \[\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)<k .\] Moreover, if \(\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}<\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak j}\), then \[\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i) < \operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak j),\] because the first counted set is contained in the second and \(\mathfrak i\) belongs to the second but not the first. Hence, whenever \(\mathfrak i\mapsto\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\) is injective, the map \(\mathfrak i\mapsto\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)\) is injective, and therefore bijective on the finite set \(I_k\).

  4. The following full-support criterion holds: \[\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta)=k \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \left[ (\forall \mathfrak i\in I_k,\ 0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}) \ \text{and}\ \mathfrak i\mapsto\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\ \text{is injective} \right].\] For the forward implication, \(\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta)\) is the image of \(\{\mathfrak i\in I_k:0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\}\) under \(\mathfrak i\mapsto\chi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\), so \[\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\zeta) \le \#\{\mathfrak i\in I_k:0<\varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i}\} \le k .\] If the left side equals \(k\), both inequalities are equalities; thus every labelled weight is positive, and equality in the image-cardinality bound gives injectivity of the labelled locations. The reverse implication follows because all \(k\) labelled slots are then positive and distinct.

  5. Suppose first that \[\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\nu)=k .\] By [pf:ordered-masses:support], \[\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\xi)=k .\] Then [pf:ordered-masses:full-support] gives \[0<\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}, \quad 0<\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak i} \qquad(\mathfrak i\in I_k),\] and both labelled-location maps are injective. For either such full-support representative \(\zeta\), Definition 62 gives \[\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\zeta)_{\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)} = \sum_{\mathfrak q:\, \operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak q)=\operatorname{rank}_{\zeta}(\mathfrak i)} \varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak q} = \varpi^\zeta_{\mathfrak i},\] where the last equality uses the rank injectivity from [pf:ordered-masses:rank].

  6. Fix a coordinate \(\mathfrak r\in I_k\). Since the rank map for \(\nu\) is bijective by [pf:ordered-masses:rank], choose \(\mathfrak i\in I_k\) such that \[\mathfrak r=\operatorname{rank}_{\nu}(\mathfrak i).\] The support equality in [pf:ordered-masses:support] gives \(\mathfrak j\in I_k\) with \[\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}, \qquad 0<\varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.\] Using [pf:ordered-masses:aggregate] at \(\mathfrak x=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\), and using injectivity of both labelled-location maps to collapse the two sums, yields \[\varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i} = \sum_{\mathfrak q:\,\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}} \varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak q} = \sum_{\mathfrak q:\,\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak q}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}} \varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak q} = \varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.\]

  7. For each \(\mathfrak q\in I_k\), [pf:ordered-masses:support] supplies a slot \(\psi(\mathfrak q)\in I_k\) such that \[\chi^\xi_{\psi(\mathfrak q)}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}.\] The map \(\psi\) is injective: if \(\psi(\mathfrak q_1)=\psi(\mathfrak q_2)\), then \(\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q_1}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q_2}\), and injectivity of the locations of \(\nu\) gives \(\mathfrak q_1=\mathfrak q_2\). Thus \(\psi\) is bijective. Since also \(\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}=\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i}\), for every \(\mathfrak q\in I_k\), \[\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak q}<\chi^\nu_{\mathfrak i} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \chi^\xi_{\psi(\mathfrak q)}<\chi^\xi_{\mathfrak j}.\] The bijection \(\psi\) therefore identifies the two sets counted by the ranks, so \[\operatorname{rank}_{\nu}(\mathfrak i) = \operatorname{rank}_{\xi}(\mathfrak j).\] Combining this rank equality with [pf:ordered-masses:apply-rank,pf:ordered-masses:weight-match] gives \[\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)_{\mathfrak r} = \varpi^\nu_{\mathfrak i} = \varpi^\xi_{\mathfrak j} = \operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)_{\mathfrak r}.\] Since \(\mathfrak r\) was arbitrary, the ordered-mass vectors are equal in the full-support case.

  8. It remains to consider \[\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\nu)\ne k .\] By [pf:ordered-masses:support], \[\#\operatorname{supp}_+(\xi)\ne k .\] Using [pf:ordered-masses:full-support], the positivity-and-injectivity condition in Definition 62 fails for both representatives. Hence both ordered-mass vectors take the barycentric fallback value: \[\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)_{\mathfrak r} = k^{-1} = \operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi)_{\mathfrak r} \qquad\text{for every }\mathfrak r\in I_k .\] Thus the ordered-mass vectors are equal in the complementary case as well.

The two cases prove \[\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\nu)=\operatorname{orderedMasses}(\xi),\] as required.

∎
Lemma 18 [lem:observed-outcome-proxy-moment-factorization] (Outcome proxy moment factorization).

Let \(P\), a full-data probability law carrying \((U,T,X,Z,Y(0),Y(1),Y)\), belong to the model class \(\mathcal M\) in Definition 32. Suppose that

  • (Latent classes.) \(2\le k\).

  • (Positivity margin.) The latent-arm positivity margin satisfies \(\pi_0>0\).

  • (Model conditions.) The reference- and target-proxy separation, consistency, latent ignorability, latent-arm positivity, bounded outcome-proxy product, and bounded target-proxy conditions in Assumptions 1, 2, 3, 4, 9, 8, and 6 hold.

Then, for each treatment arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), \[N_t(P) = A_t\,\Gamma_t\,\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}:1\le u\le k\}\,B^\top, \qquad \Gamma_t = \operatorname{diag}\left\{\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}:1\le u\le k\right\}.\] Here \(N_t(P)\) is the observable armwise \(YZX^\top\) population moment in Definition 28, \(A_t\) is the reference-proxy feature matrix in Definition 24, \(B\) is the target-proxy feature matrix in Definition 25, and \(\mu_{tu}\) is the latent conditional potential-outcome mean in Definition 22.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 18.

Step 1. Fix \(t\in\{0,1\}\). For \(1\le u\le k\), put \[\mathsf U_u:=\{U=u\},\qquad \mathsf R_t:=\{T=t\},\qquad \mathsf H_{u,t}:=\mathsf U_u\cap\mathsf R_t .\] For a full-data event \(\mathfrak e\) and a real-valued measurable function \(\varphi\), write \[\mathbb E_{\mathfrak e}^{P}[\varphi] := \{P(\mathfrak e)\}^{-1}\int_{\mathfrak e}\varphi\,dP,\] with the total convention \(0^{-1}=0\). The latent-arm positivity assumption gives, for every \(u\), \[0<\pi_0\le P(\mathsf H_{u,t})\le P(\mathsf U_u).\] Also, Lemma 3 gives \[k\pi_0\le P(\mathsf R_t),\] and since \(2\le k\) and \(0<\pi_0\), \[0<k\pi_0\le P(\mathsf R_t).\] Thus all normalizations used below have positive denominators on each latent class, each latent-treatment cell, and the treatment arm.

Step 2. Let \(P_u^\circ\) be the normalized restriction of \(P\) to \(\mathsf U_u\), equivalently the probability law with density \(P(\mathsf U_u)^{-1}\mathbf 1_{\mathsf U_u}\) with respect to \(P\). Latent ignorability in Assumption 4 gives independence of \(Y(t)\) and \(T\) under \(P_u^\circ\). Therefore \[\int_{\mathsf R_t}Y(t)\,dP_u^\circ = P_u^\circ(\mathsf R_t)\int Y(t)\,dP_u^\circ, \qquad P_u^\circ(\mathsf R_t)=\frac{P(\mathsf H_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf U_u)} .\] Using the normalized-restriction formula on both sides, \[\frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}\int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}Y(t)\,dP = \frac{P(\mathsf H_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf U_u)} \frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)}\int_{\mathsf U_u}Y(t)\,dP .\] The positive denominators from Step 1 allow cancellation, giving \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y(t)] = \mathbb E_{\mathsf U_u}^{P}[Y(t)] = \mu_{tu},\] where the last equality is Definition 22. On \(\mathsf R_t\), consistency in Assumption 3 gives \(Y=Y(t)\) almost surely, hence \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y] = \mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y(t)] = \mu_{tu}.\]

Step 3. Fix coordinates \(1\le \iota_z\le d_z\) and \(1\le \iota_x\le d_x\). Target-proxy separation in Assumption 2, applied under \(P_u^\circ\), gives independence of \(X\) and \((Y,T)\) conditional on \(U=u\). Define \[\psi_t(y,s):=y\,\mathbf 1\{s=t\}, \qquad (y,s)\in\mathbb R\times\{0,1\}.\] The product identity under \(P_u^\circ\) gives \[\int X_{\iota_x}\psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ = \left(\int X_{\iota_x}\,dP_u^\circ\right) \left(\int \psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ\right).\] The three terms are \[\int X_{\iota_x}\psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ = \frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)} \int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}X_{\iota_x}Y\,dP,\] \[\int X_{\iota_x}\,dP_u^\circ = \frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)} \int_{\mathsf U_u}X_{\iota_x}\,dP, \qquad \int \psi_t(Y,T)\,dP_u^\circ = \frac{1}{P(\mathsf U_u)} \int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}Y\,dP .\] Multiplying by \(P(\mathsf U_u)/P(\mathsf H_{u,t})\) and using Step 1 yields \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}Y] = \mathbb E_{\mathsf U_u}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}]\, \mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Y].\] By Definition 25 and Step 2, \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}Y] = B(\iota_x,u)\,\mu_{tu}.\]

Step 4. Let \(P_{u,t}^\circ\) be the normalized restriction of \(P\) to \(\mathsf H_{u,t}\). Reference-proxy separation in Assumption 1, applied under \(P_{u,t}^\circ\), gives independence of \(Z\) and \((X,Y)\) on the latent-treatment cell. Applying the product identity to \(Z_{\iota_z}\) and \(X_{\iota_x}Y\), \[\int Z_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}Y\,dP_{u,t}^\circ = \left(\int Z_{\iota_z}\,dP_{u,t}^\circ\right) \left(\int X_{\iota_x}Y\,dP_{u,t}^\circ\right).\] Equivalently, \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Z_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}Y] = \mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[Z_{\iota_z}]\, \mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[X_{\iota_x}Y].\] Combining this identity with Step 3 and Definition 24 gives the cellwise factorization \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}] = A_t(\iota_z,u)\,\mu_{tu}\,B(\iota_x,u).\]

Step 5. Set \[\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}:=YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}.\] The bounded outcome-proxy product condition in Assumption 8 implies \[|\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}| = |YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}| \le \|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le L\] almost surely, since every matrix entry is bounded by the operator norm. Thus \(\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}\) is integrable on every latent-treatment cell. The treatment arm decomposes as the disjoint finite union \[\mathsf R_t=\bigsqcup_{u=1}^{k}\mathsf H_{u,t},\] so \[\int_{\mathsf R_t}\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}\,dP = \sum_{u=1}^{k}\int_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}\,dP .\] Dividing by the positive arm mass from Step 1 and rewriting each cell integral as a conditional mean gives \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf R_t}^{P}[\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}] = \sum_{u=1}^{k} \frac{P(\mathsf H_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf R_t)} \mathbb E_{\mathsf H_{u,t}}^{P}[\varphi_{\iota_z,\iota_x}].\] Substituting Step 4, \[\mathbb E_{\mathsf R_t}^{P}[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}] = \sum_{u=1}^{k} A_t(\iota_z,u)\, \frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}\, \mu_{tu}\, B(\iota_x,u).\]

Step 6. By Definition 20, \(P_O\) is the pushforward of \(P\) by \(O=(T,X,Z,Y)\). Hence the observed-arm conditional mean agrees with the corresponding full-data arm conditional mean: \[E_{P_O}\!\left[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}\mid T=t\right] = \mathbb E_{\mathsf R_t}^{P}[YZ_{\iota_z}X_{\iota_x}].\] The armwise \(YZX^\top\) moment notation in Definition 28 identifies the \((\iota_z,\iota_x)\) entry of \(N_t(P)\) with this same full-data conditional moment. Therefore \[N_t(P)(\iota_z,\iota_x) = \sum_{u=1}^{k} A_t(\iota_z,u)\, \frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}\, \mu_{tu}\, B(\iota_x,u).\] With \[\Gamma_t = \operatorname{diag}\left\{ \frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}:1\le u\le k \right\},\] the \((\iota_z,\iota_x)\) entry of \[A_t\,\Gamma_t\,\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}:1\le u\le k\}\,B^\top\] is exactly the displayed sum. Since \(\iota_z\) and \(\iota_x\) were arbitrary, \[N_t(P) = A_t\,\Gamma_t\,\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}:1\le u\le k\}\,B^\top .\]

∎
Lemma 19 [lem:latent-arm-weight-invertibility] (Latent-arm weight injectivity).

Let \(P\) be a full-data probability law carrying \((U,T,X,Z,Y(0),Y(1),Y)\), and let \(\pi_0>0\). Suppose \(P\) satisfies latent-arm positivity with margin \(\pi_0\), as in Assumption 9. Then, for each treatment arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), the diagonal matrix \[\Gamma_t = \operatorname{diag}\left\{ \frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)} : u\in\{1,\ldots,k\} \right\}\] defines an injective linear map on \(\mathbb R^k\).

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 19.

Fix \(t\in\{0,1\}\). For a latent coordinate \(u\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\), set \[\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}:=\{U=u,\ T=t\}, \qquad \mathsf{arm}_{t}:=\{T=t\}, \qquad \gamma_{u,t}:=\frac{P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf{arm}_t)} .\] By the definition of \(\Gamma_t\), this matrix is diagonal and its \(u\)th diagonal entry is \(\gamma_{u,t}\).

We first show that every diagonal entry is bounded below by the positivity margin. By Assumption 9, \[\pi_0\le P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}).\] Moreover \(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}\subseteq \mathsf{arm}_t\), so monotonicity gives \[P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t})\le P(\mathsf{arm}_t).\] Together with \(\pi_0>0\), this implies \[0<P(\mathsf{arm}_t).\] Since \(P\) is a probability law, \[P(\mathsf{arm}_t)\le 1.\] Hence, using \(\pi_0>0\), \[\pi_0 P(\mathsf{arm}_t) \le \pi_0 \le P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t}).\] Dividing by the positive number \(P(\mathsf{arm}_t)\) yields \[\pi_0 \le \frac{P(\mathsf{cell}_{u,t})}{P(\mathsf{arm}_t)} = \gamma_{u,t}.\] In particular \(\gamma_{u,t}>0\), so \(\gamma_{u,t}\ne0\).

Now let \(x,y\in\mathbb R^k\) and suppose \(\Gamma_t x=\Gamma_t y\). Taking the \(u\)th coordinate and using diagonality gives \[\gamma_{u,t}x_u=\gamma_{u,t}y_u .\] Since \(\gamma_{u,t}\ne0\), cancellation gives \[x_u=y_u .\] This holds for every latent coordinate \(u\), and therefore \(x=y\). Thus the linear map induced by \(\Gamma_t\) is injective.

∎
Lemma 20 [lem:moore-penrose-product-cancellation] (Moore--Penrose product cancellation).

Let \(C\in\mathbb R^{\mathrm{rows}\times r}\), \(B\in\mathbb R^{\mathrm{cols}\times r}\), and \(\mu\in\mathbb R^r\). Suppose that the linear maps represented by \(C\) and by \(B\), each with domain \(\mathbb R^r\), are injective. Then \[(CB^\top)^\dagger\bigl(C\operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top\bigr) = (B^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 20.

For any real rectangular matrix \(A\), let \(A^\dagger\) denote its Moore–Penrose inverse, characterized by \[AA^\dagger A=A,\qquad A^\dagger A A^\dagger=A^\dagger,\qquad (AA^\dagger)^\top=AA^\dagger,\qquad (A^\dagger A)^\top=A^\dagger A .\] If the matrix map \(A\) is injective, then \[A^\dagger A=I .\] Indeed, the first Moore–Penrose identity gives \[A(A^\dagger A)=AA^\dagger A=A.\] Thus, for every vector \(x\), \[A\{(A^\dagger A)x\}=Ax,\] and injectivity of \(A\) gives \((A^\dagger A)x=x\). Hence \(A^\dagger A=I\).

Apply this observation to \(C\) and \(B\). Then \[C^\dagger C=I_r,\qquad B^\dagger B=I_r,\] and transposing the second identity gives \[B^\top (B^\dagger)^\top=I_r .\] Set \[G:=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger .\] We verify that \(G\) is the Moore–Penrose inverse of \(CB^\top\). First, \[(CB^\top)G(CB^\top) = CB^\top (B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C B^\top = C I_r I_r B^\top = CB^\top ,\] and \[G(CB^\top)G = (B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C B^\top (B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger = (B^\dagger)^\top I_r I_r C^\dagger = G .\] For the symmetry equations, the same cancellations give \[(CB^\top)G=CB^\top(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger=CC^\dagger,\] so \(((CB^\top)G)^\top=(CB^\top)G\) by the Moore–Penrose symmetry of \(CC^\dagger\). Also \[G(CB^\top)=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C B^\top=(B^\dagger)^\top B^\top=(BB^\dagger)^\top=BB^\dagger,\] so \((G(CB^\top))^\top=G(CB^\top)\) by the Moore–Penrose symmetry of \(BB^\dagger\). Thus \(G\) satisfies all four Moore–Penrose equations for \(CB^\top\), and uniqueness yields \[(CB^\top)^\dagger=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger .\]

Therefore, using associativity and \(C^\dagger C=I_r\), \[\begin{aligned} (CB^\top)^\dagger\bigl(C\operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top\bigr) &=(B^\dagger)^\top C^\dagger C\operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top \\ &=(B^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}(\mu)B^\top . \end{aligned}\] This is the claimed cancellation identity.

∎
Lemma 21 [lem:kantorovich-rubinstein-attaining-potential] (Attaining KR potential).

Let \(\nu\) and \(\xi\) be valid represented atomic laws with finite labelled slot sets: their weights are nonnegative and sum to one. Let \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\) be the cumulative-distribution-gap sign Kantorovich–Rubinstein potential selected for the pair \((\nu,\xi)\). Then \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\) is one-Lipschitz, is normalized by \[\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(0)=0,\] and attains the Wasserstein distance of Definition 19: \[W_1(\nu,\xi) = \left| \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu - \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi \right|.\] Moreover, for every \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), if every one-Lipschitz function \(\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) satisfying \(\varphi(0)=0\) obeys \[\left| \int \varphi\,d\nu - \int \varphi\,d\xi \right| \le \epsilon,\] then \[W_1(\nu,\xi)\le \epsilon.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 21.

Write \[\nu=\sum_i p_i\delta_{x_i}, \qquad \xi=\sum_j q_j\delta_{y_j},\] with \(p_i,q_j\ge0\), \(\sum_i p_i=\sum_jq_j=1\). Define \[F_\nu(t):=\sum_i p_i{\bf 1}\{x_i\le t\}, \qquad F_\xi(t):=\sum_j q_j{\bf 1}\{y_j\le t\}, \qquad \Delta(t):=F_\nu(t)-F_\xi(t),\] and let \[s(t):= \begin{cases} 1,&\Delta(t)>0,\\ -1,&\Delta(t)<0,\\ 0,&\Delta(t)=0. \end{cases}\] The selected potential is \[\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(x):=\int_0^x s(t)\,dt .\] Since \(|s(t)|\le1\), for all \(x,y\in\mathbb R\), \[|\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(x)-\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(y)| = \left|\int_y^x s(t)\,dt\right| \le |x-y|,\] so \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\) is one-Lipschitz. Also \[\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(0)=\int_0^0 s(t)\,dt=0 .\]

We next record the finite one-dimensional transport identity used by the potential. For any finite transport plan \(\gamma=(\gamma_{ij})\) from \(\nu\) to \(\xi\), put \[C_\gamma(t) := \sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij} \bigl( {\bf 1}\{x_i\le t<y_j\} - {\bf 1}\{y_j\le t<x_i\} \bigr).\] Using the two marginal constraints of the plan gives \[\Delta(t)=C_\gamma(t) \qquad\text{for every }t\in\mathbb R .\] Hence, with \[E_\gamma(t):= \sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij} {\bf 1}\{\min(x_i,y_j)\le t<\max(x_i,y_j)\},\] we have \[|\Delta(t)|\le E_\gamma(t).\] Integrating and using \[\int_{\mathbb R}{\bf 1}\{\min(x_i,y_j)\le t<\max(x_i,y_j)\}\,dt = |x_i-y_j|\] yields \[\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt \le \sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}|x_i-y_j|.\]

Conversely, the finite transport polytope is compact and the quadratic cost \[\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}(x_i-y_j)^2\] therefore has a minimizer. If such a minimizer sent positive mass across some cut in both directions, shifting \(\varepsilon=\min\{\gamma_{ij},\gamma_{kl}\}\) from the crossing pairs \((i,j),(k,l)\) to \((i,l),(k,j)\) would preserve the two marginals and decrease the quadratic cost by \[2\varepsilon(x_k-x_i)(y_j-y_l)>0,\] a contradiction. Thus the minimizer has no counterflow across any cut, and for that plan \(|C_\gamma(t)|=E_\gamma(t)\) for every \(t\). Consequently \[\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}|x_i-y_j| = \int_{\mathbb R}E_\gamma(t)\,dt = \int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt .\] Together with the preceding inequality and Definition 19, this gives \[W_1(\nu,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt .\]

It remains to evaluate the potential. For an atom \(a\), set \[\chi_a(t):= {\bf 1}\{0\le t<a\}-{\bf 1}\{a\le t<0\}.\] Then \[\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(a)=\int_{\mathbb R}s(t)\chi_a(t)\,dt .\] Interchanging the finite sums with the integral gives \[\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu-\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi = \int_{\mathbb R}s(t) \left\{ \sum_i p_i\chi_{x_i}(t)-\sum_j q_j\chi_{y_j}(t) \right\}\,dt .\] For \(t\ge0\), the expression in braces equals \((1-F_\nu(t))-(1-F_\xi(t))=-\Delta(t)\); for \(t<0\), it equals \(-F_\nu(t)+F_\xi(t)=-\Delta(t)\). Therefore \[\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu-\int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi = -\int_{\mathbb R}s(t)\Delta(t)\,dt = -\int_{\mathbb R}|\Delta(t)|\,dt .\] Taking absolute values and using the preceding display for \(W_1\) gives \[W_1(\nu,\xi) = \left| \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu - \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi \right|.\]

Finally, suppose \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\) and every normalized one-Lipschitz test function satisfies the displayed bound. Applying that bound to the just-constructed \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}\), which is one-Lipschitz and satisfies \(\varphi_{\nu,\xi}(0)=0\), gives \[W_1(\nu,\xi) = \left| \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\nu - \int \varphi_{\nu,\xi}\,d\xi \right| \le \epsilon .\]

∎
Lemma 22 [lem:summary-metric-coordinate-comparison] (Coordinate metric comparison).

Let \(d_x\) and \(d_z\) be fixed target- and reference-proxy dimensions, and let \(s,s'\) be elements of the five-block observable summary space in Definition 33. Equip this space with its coordinate Euclidean distance \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\). Then \[d_S(s,s') \le \bigl(4\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}+d_x\bigr)\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(s,s'),\] where \[\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c} := c\,r\,\|J_c\|_{\mathrm{op}},\] and \(J_c:\mathbb R^c\to(\{1,\ldots,c\}\to\mathbb R)\) is the canonical coordinate identification.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 22.

Write \[\varrho:=\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(s,s').\] For \(d\in\mathbb N\), put \[[d]_0:=\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\};\] when \(d=0\), this set is empty and the finite sums below have value zero. Write \[s=(s_1,s_2,s_3,s_4,s_5), \qquad s'=(s'_1,s'_2,s'_3,s'_4,s'_5),\] where \(s_1,\ldots,s_4\) and \(s'_1,\ldots,s'_4\) are \(d_z\times d_x\) matrix blocks and \(s_5,s'_5\in\mathbb R^{d_x}\), as in Definition 33.

  1. Since \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\) is the Euclidean distance on the full coordinate vector, each coordinate-block projection is nonexpansive. Hence, for each \(\ell=1,2,3,4\), \[\left( \sum_{\mathfrak i\in[d_z]_0,\ \mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0} \bigl((s_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j} -(s'_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}\bigr)^2 \right)^{1/2} \le \varrho,\] and \[\left( \sum_{\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0} \bigl((s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr)^2 \right)^{1/2} \le \varrho .\] In particular, \[\bigl|(s_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j} -(s'_\ell)_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}\bigr| \le \varrho \quad (\ell=1,2,3,4;\ \mathfrak i\in[d_z]_0,\ \mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0),\] and \[\bigl|(s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr| \le \varrho \quad(\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0).\]

  2. We use the following finite-dimensional entrywise estimate. Let \(\Psi\in\mathbb R^{r\times c}\), let \(\zeta\in\mathbb R\) satisfy \(0\le\zeta\), and suppose \[|\Psi_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}|\le \zeta \quad (\mathfrak i\in[r]_0,\ \mathfrak j\in[c]_0).\] For the canonical vector \(e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}\in\mathbb R^c\), \[\|\Psi e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}\|_2 \le \sum_{\mathfrak i\in[r]_0}|\Psi_{\mathfrak i\mathfrak j}| \le r\,\zeta .\] Thus, for every \(\upsilon\in\mathbb R^c\) with \(\|\upsilon\|_2\le1\), \[\begin{aligned} \|\Psi\upsilon\|_2 &= \left\| \sum_{\mathfrak j\in[c]_0} (J_c\upsilon)_{\mathfrak j}\,\Psi e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j} \right\|_2 \\ &\le \sum_{\mathfrak j\in[c]_0} |(J_c\upsilon)_{\mathfrak j}|\, \|\Psi e^{(c)}_{\mathfrak j}\|_2 \\ &\le c\,r\,\|J_c\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\zeta . \end{aligned}\] Using the ordinary matrix action and operator-norm convention in Definitions 8 and 11, taking the supremum over the Euclidean unit ball gives \[\|\Psi\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}\,\zeta, \qquad \operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}=c\,r\,\|J_c\|_{\mathrm{op}} .\]

  3. Apply the preceding estimate with \[r=d_z,\qquad c=d_x,\qquad \zeta=\varrho\] to the four matrix differences \(s_\ell-s'_\ell\), \(\ell=1,2,3,4\). The coordinate bounds from the first step yield \[\|s_\ell-s'_\ell\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}\,\varrho, \qquad \ell=1,2,3,4 .\]

  4. For the mean block, expand in the canonical basis of \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\): \[s_5-s'_5 = \sum_{\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0} \bigl((s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr)e^{(d_x)}_{\mathfrak j}.\] The coordinate bounds and the triangle inequality give \[\|s_5-s'_5\|_2 \le \sum_{\mathfrak j\in[d_x]_0} \bigl|(s_5)_{\mathfrak j}-(s'_5)_{\mathfrak j}\bigr| \le d_x\,\varrho .\]

  5. Substituting the four matrix-block estimates and the mean-block estimate into the definition of \(d_S\) in Definition 33 gives \[\begin{aligned} d_S(s,s') &= \sum_{\ell=1}^{4}\|s_\ell-s'_\ell\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|s_5-s'_5\|_2 \\ &\le 4\,\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}\,\varrho+d_x\,\varrho \\ &= \bigl(4\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}+d_x\bigr) \rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(s,s') . \end{aligned}\]

∎
Lemma 23 [lem:summary-metric-continuity] (Summary metric continuity).

For fixed target- and reference-proxy dimensions \(d_x,d_z\), let \(d_S\) be the summary metric on observable moment summaries from Definition 33. The map \[(s,q)\longmapsto d_S(s,q)\] from the product of the five-block summary space with itself to \(\mathbb R\) is continuous.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 23.

By Definition 46, for the fixed dimensions \(d_x\) and \(d_z\) the ambient summary space is \[\mathscr S_{\mathrm{all}} = (\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x})^4\times \mathbb R^{d_x}.\] Write \[s=\bigl(M_0(s),M_1(s),N_0(s),N_1(s),m_X(s)\bigr), \qquad q=\bigl(M_0(q),M_1(q),N_0(q),N_1(q),m_X(q)\bigr).\] The coordinate map \[\iota(s):=\bigl(M_0(s),M_1(s),N_0(s),N_1(s),m_X(s)\bigr)\] is continuous for the product topology, and therefore each coordinate projection \[s\mapsto M_0(s),\quad s\mapsto M_1(s),\quad s\mapsto N_0(s),\quad s\mapsto N_1(s),\quad s\mapsto m_X(s)\] is continuous. The same finite-product argument covers the zero-dimensional cases, where the relevant products and sums have their usual singleton or empty interpretation.

For a pair \((s,q)\), the four matrix-valued difference maps \[(s,q)\longmapsto M_0(s)-M_0(q),\quad (s,q)\longmapsto M_1(s)-M_1(q),\] \[(s,q)\longmapsto N_0(s)-N_0(q),\quad (s,q)\longmapsto N_1(s)-N_1(q)\] are continuous by continuity of the coordinate projections and of subtraction. If \(\Upsilon\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}\), the associated Euclidean linear operator \[\mathbb R^{d_x}\to\mathbb R^{d_z}, \qquad x\longmapsto \Upsilon x,\] depends continuously on \(\Upsilon\), because this matrix-to-operator assignment is linear between finite-dimensional normed spaces. Composing with the continuous operator norm gives continuity of \[(s,q)\longmapsto \|M_0(s)-M_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}},\quad (s,q)\longmapsto \|M_1(s)-M_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}},\] \[(s,q)\longmapsto \|N_0(s)-N_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}},\quad (s,q)\longmapsto \|N_1(s)-N_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} .\]

The mean-coordinate difference \[(s,q)\longmapsto m_X(s)-m_X(q)\] is continuous as a map into \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\). Its Euclidean norm is \[\|m_X(s)-m_X(q)\|_2 = \left(\sum_{i<d_x}\bigl(m_X(s)_i-m_X(q)_i\bigr)^2\right)^{1/2}.\] The summand coordinates are continuous, the index set is finite, and the square-root is continuous on the nonnegative range of the sum of squares. Hence \[(s,q)\longmapsto \|m_X(s)-m_X(q)\|_2\] is continuous.

Using Definition 33, \[d_S(s,q) = \|M_0(s)-M_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|M_1(s)-M_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|N_0(s)-N_0(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|N_1(s)-N_1(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|m_X(s)-m_X(q)\|_2 .\] This is a finite sum of continuous real-valued functions on \(\mathscr S_{\mathrm{all}}\times\mathscr S_{\mathrm{all}}\). Therefore \[(s,q)\longmapsto d_S(s,q)\] is continuous.

∎
Lemma 24 [lem:stacked-proxy-moment-signal-margin] (Stacked proxy signal margin).

Let \(P\), a full-data probability law carrying \((U,T,X,Z,Y(0),Y(1),Y)\), belong to the uniformly conditioned proxy causal model class \(\mathcal M\) in Definition 32. Suppose:

  • (Dimensions.) \(2\le k\le d_x\), where \(k\) is the number of latent classes and \(d_x\) is the target-proxy dimension.

  • (Radius.) \(L\ge 1\), with the bounded target-proxy condition in Assumption 6.

  • (Margins.) \(\pi_0>0\) and \(\sigma_0>0\), with the latent-arm positivity and proxy-rank margin conditions in Assumptions 9 and 10.

Then the vertically stacked observable proxy moment satisfies \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2 \le \sigma_{k-1}\!\left\{\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(P),M_1(P)\bigr)\right\},\] where \(\sigma_{k-1}\) uses zero-based singular-value indexing and \(M_0(P),M_1(P)\) are the armwise \(ZX^\top\) population moments from Definition 28.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 24.

Set \[A_0:=\bigl(E_P[Z_i\mid U=u,T=0]\bigr)_{i,u},\qquad B:=\bigl(E_P[X_i\mid U=u]\bigr)_{i,u},\] and \[\Gamma_0:= \operatorname{diag}\left\{ \frac{P(U=u,T=0)}{P(T=0)}:1\le u\le k \right\}.\] Let \(S=\operatorname{range}(B)\subseteq\mathbb R^{d_x}\). The proxy-rank margin in Assumption 10 gives \[\sigma_{k-1}(A_0)\ge \sigma_0,\qquad \sigma_{k-1}(B)\ge \sigma_0 .\] Since \(\sigma_0>0\), the maps induced by \(A_0\) and \(B\) are injective. Hence \[\dim S=k .\]

The latent-arm positivity condition gives \(P(U=u,T=0)\ge \pi_0\) for every \(u\). Since \(0<P(T=0)\le1\), each diagonal entry of \(\Gamma_0\) is at least \(\pi_0\). Therefore, for every \(y\in\mathbb R^k\), \[\pi_0\|y\|_2\le \|\Gamma_0y\|_2 .\]

Now fix \(x\in S\). The least-singular-value bound for \(B\), restricted to its range, gives \[\sigma_0\|x\|_2\le \|B^\top x\|_2 .\]

Applying the preceding diagonal bound to \(y=B^\top x\) gives \[\pi_0\|B^\top x\|_2\le \|\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 .\]

The least-singular-value bound for \(A_0\) gives \[\sigma_0\|\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 \le \|A_0\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 .\]

Combining these three inequalities, \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2\|x\|_2 \le \|A_0\Gamma_0B^\top x\|_2 .\]

By the observable proxy moment factorization in Lemma 4, \[M_0(P)=A_0\Gamma_0B^\top .\] Thus \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2\|x\|_2 \le \|M_0(P)x\|_2 .\]

For \[G:=\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(P),M_1(P)\bigr),\] vertical stacking gives the Pythagorean identity \[\|Gx\|_2^2=\|M_0(P)x\|_2^2+\|M_1(P)x\|_2^2,\] and hence \[\|M_0(P)x\|_2\le \|Gx\|_2 .\]

Consequently, \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2\|x\|_2\le \|Gx\|_2 \qquad\text{for every }x\in S .\]

Since \(S\) is a \(k\)-dimensional subspace and \(\pi_0\sigma_0^2\ge0\), the variational subspace characterization of zero-indexed singular values yields \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2 \le \sigma_{k-1}(G) = \sigma_{k-1}\!\left\{\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(P),M_1(P)\bigr)\right\}.\]

∎

These witness calculations locate the explicit two-class comparisons inside the same model restrictions used for the upper bounds. The path-local statement calibrates the observed Kullback–Leibler radius to the product \(g^2h^2\), which is the scale used by Theorem 6.

The final two auxiliary statements concern ordered masses as functions of represented atomic laws. They ensure that, when the support is separated, the ordered-weight target depends on the aggregate measure and is invariant across labelled coordinate representations.

Lemma 25 [lem:model-compressed-spectral-certificate] (Compressed spectral certificate).

Let \(k,d_x,d_z\in\mathbb N\), let \(L,\pi_0,\sigma_0\in\mathbb R\), and let \(Q\) be a full-data probability law in the uniformly conditioned model class \(\mathcal M\) of Definition 32, with observable summary \(S(Q)\) as in Definition 33. Assume that \(Q\) satisfies Assumptions 5, 10, and 9. Let \(M_t(S(Q))\) be the \(t\)-arm \(ZX^\top\) moment block from Definition 28, and set \[G:=\operatorname{stack}(M_0(S(Q)),M_1(S(Q))).\] Then there exist a signal basis \(V\) spanning the signal row space of \(S(Q)\) and a real diagonalization of the compressed effect operator \(\Delta Q(Q)\) from Definition 39 such that:

  1. for each arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), the compressed map \[x\longmapsto M_t(S(Q))Vx\] is injective;

  2. the stacked proxy moment has rank \[\operatorname{rank}(G)=k;\]

  3. for every index \(j\), \[\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}\le \sigma_j(G) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad j<k;\]

  4. the eigenvalue vector of the diagonalization is the latent-effect vector \((\tau_u(Q))_u\) from Definition 23;

  5. writing \(R\) for the diagonalizing basis matrix, the left and right spectral anchors satisfy \[a(Q)=R^{-\top}p(Q), \qquad R^{-1}c(Q)=\mathbf 1_k,\] where \(p(Q)=(p_u(Q))_u\) is the latent-mass vector from Definition 21.

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 25.

  1. Write \[B:=B(Q)\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times k}\] for the target-proxy feature matrix from Definition 25. The parameter domain in Definition 32 gives \(2\le k\), \(k\le d_x\), \(1\le L\), \(\pi_0>0\), and \(\sigma_0>0\). With zero-based singular-value indexing, Assumption 10 gives \[\sigma_{k-1}(B)\ge \sigma_0>0 .\] Hence the first \(k\) singular values of \(B\) are positive. Choose the thin singular-value factorization \[B=V\Upsilon ,\] where \(V\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times k}\) has orthonormal columns and \(\Upsilon\in\mathbb R^{k\times k}\) is invertible. Put \[\Theta:=B^\top V=\Upsilon^\top, \qquad \Theta^{-1}:=\Upsilon^{-T}.\] Then \[\Theta\Theta^{-1}=\Theta^{-1}\Theta=I_k .\]

  2. For each arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), define \[\Gamma_t := \operatorname{diag}\left\{ \frac{Q(U=u,T=t)}{Q(T=t)}:1\le u\le k \right\}, \qquad \Xi_t:=A_t\Gamma_t ,\] where \(A_t=A_t(Q)\) is the reference-proxy feature matrix from Definition 24. At the model-generated summary \(S(Q)\), Lemmas 4 and 18 give \[M_t(S(Q))=A_t\Gamma_tB^\top=\Xi_tB^\top\] and \[N_t(S(Q)) = A_t\Gamma_t\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}B^\top = \Xi_t\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}B^\top .\] Multiplying by \(V\) gives \[M_t(S(Q))V=\Xi_t\Theta, \qquad N_t(S(Q))V = \Xi_t\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta .\]

    Also, \[M_t(S(Q))^\top = B\Xi_t^\top = V\Upsilon\Xi_t^\top ,\] so the row space of each \(M_t(S(Q))\) is contained in \(\operatorname{range}(V)\). For \(t=0\), latent-arm positivity in Assumption 9 gives \[\Gamma_0(u,u)=\frac{Q(U=u,T=0)}{Q(T=0)}\ge \pi_0\] for every \(u\), since \(Q(U=u,T=0)\ge\pi_0\) and \(Q(T=0)\le1\). Combining this with Assumption 10 and the standard product lower bound for the least signal singular value, \[\begin{aligned} \sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_0(S(Q))\right) &= \sigma_{k-1}\!\left(A_0\Gamma_0B^\top\right) \\ &\ge \sigma_{k-1}(A_0)\,\sigma_{k-1}(\Gamma_0)\,\sigma_{k-1}(B) \ge \pi_0\sigma_0^2 . \end{aligned}\] Thus \(M_0(S(Q))\) has row rank \(k\). Its row space is contained in the \(k\)-dimensional space \(\operatorname{range}(V)\), hence \[\operatorname{range}\!\left(M_0(S(Q))^\top\right)=\operatorname{range}(V).\] Since the row space of \(M_1(S(Q))\) is contained in the same space, the signal row space of \(S(Q)\) equals \[\operatorname{range}\!\left(M_0(S(Q))^\top\right) + \operatorname{range}\!\left(M_1(S(Q))^\top\right) = \operatorname{range}(V).\] Thus \(V\) spans the signal row space of \(S(Q)\).

  3. The same product-margin argument for each arm \(t\) gives \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2 \le \sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))\right).\] Because \(V\) is an orthonormal basis for the signal row space, compression by \(V\) preserves the \(k\) nonzero signal singular values: \[\sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))V\right) = \sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))\right).\] Consequently \[0<\pi_0\sigma_0^2 \le \sigma_{k-1}\!\left(M_t(S(Q))V\right),\] and the linear map \[x\longmapsto M_t(S(Q))Vx,\qquad x\in\mathbb R^k,\] is injective for each \(t\in\{0,1\}\).

  4. Let \[G:=\operatorname{stack}\bigl(M_0(S(Q)),M_1(S(Q))\bigr).\] By Lemma 24, \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2\le \sigma_{k-1}(G),\] so \(\operatorname{rank}(G)\ge k\). On the other hand, if \[\Xi_{\mathrm{st}}:=\operatorname{stack}(\Xi_0,\Xi_1)\in\mathbb R^{2d_z\times k},\] then the proxy factorizations give \[G=\Xi_{\mathrm{st}}B^\top .\] Hence \(\operatorname{rank}(G)\le k\), and therefore \[\operatorname{rank}(G)=k .\] The threshold characterization follows immediately. If \(j<k\), monotonicity of singular values gives \[\sigma_j(G)\ge\sigma_{k-1}(G)\ge\pi_0\sigma_0^2 \ge\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}.\] If \(k\le j\), the rank identity gives \(\sigma_j(G)=0\), while \[0<\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}.\] Thus, for every singular-value index \(j\), \[\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}\le \sigma_j(G) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad j<k .\]

  5. Since \(M_t(S(Q))V\) is injective, its Moore–Penrose inverse is a left inverse on its range: \[\{M_t(S(Q))V\}^{\dagger}M_t(S(Q))V=I_k .\] Using the displayed factorizations, \[N_t(S(Q))V = \{M_t(S(Q))V\}\, \Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta .\] Therefore \[\{M_t(S(Q))V\}^{\dagger}N_t(S(Q))V = \Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta .\] By the compressed-operator formula in Definition 39, \[\begin{aligned} \Delta Q(Q) &= \Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{1u}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta - \Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{0u}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta \\ &= \Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{1u}(Q)-\mu_{0u}(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta \\ &= \Theta^{-1}\operatorname{diag}\{\tau_u(Q):1\le u\le k\}\Theta , \end{aligned}\] where the last equality is Definition 23. Thus \(R:=\Theta^{-1}\) is a real diagonalizing basis for \(\Delta Q(Q)\), its inverse is \(R^{-1}=\Theta\), and the eigenvalue vector is \((\tau_u(Q))_u\).

  6. It remains to identify the spectral anchors. The target-proxy mean factors through the latent masses: \[m_X(Q)=B\,p(Q).\] Indeed, for each target-proxy coordinate \(i\), \[m_X(Q)_i = E_Q[X_i] = \sum_{u=1}^k E_Q[X_i\mid U=u]\,Q(U=u) = \sum_{u=1}^k B(i,u)\,p_u(Q),\] using Definitions 25 and 21. The anchor condition in Assumption 5, together with Definitions 36 and 25, gives \[B^\top e_1=\mathbf 1_k,\] because for each latent class \(u\), \[(B^\top e_1)_u=B(1,u)=E_Q[X_1\mid U=u]=1 .\] By Definition 39, \[a(Q)=V^\top m_X(Q), \qquad c(Q)=V^\top e_1 .\] Using \(B=V\Upsilon\), \(V^\top V=I_k\), and \(R^{-1}=\Theta=\Upsilon^\top\), \[a(Q)=V^\top B\,p(Q)=\Upsilon p(Q),\] and hence \[a(Q)^\top=p(Q)^\top\Upsilon^\top=p(Q)^\top R^{-1}.\] Similarly, \[R^{-1}c(Q) = \Theta V^\top e_1 = \Upsilon^\top V^\top e_1 = B^\top e_1 = \mathbf 1_k .\] These are the claimed left and right spectral-anchor identities.

∎
Lemma 26 [lem:diagonalization-condition-number-bound] (Diagonalization condition bound).

Let \(d_x\ge 1\) and \(k\ge 1\). Let \(B\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times k}\), and let \(F\) be a thin signal factorization of \(B\), written \(B=V\Upsilon\), with coordinate inverse \(\Upsilon^{-1}\). Let \(\tau\in\mathbb R^k\). Suppose that

  • (Feature bound.) For a constant \(L\ge 0\) as in Assumption 6, \(|B_{iu}|\le L\) for every row \(i\) and latent index \(u\).

  • (Coordinate-inverse bound.) For a constant \(\sigma_0>0\) as in Assumption 10, \(|(\Upsilon^{-1})_{ru}|\le \sigma_0^{-1}\) for every pair of coordinate indices \(r,u\).

Define \[K_{\mathrm{forw}} := \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}\sigma_0^{-1} + \bigl(\operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,d_x} + \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}\bigr),\] \[K_{\mathrm{back}} := \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}(d_xL) + \bigl(\operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,d_x} + \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}\bigr),\] and \[\kappa_\star := \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,d_x}^{\,2}K_{\mathrm{forw}}K_{\mathrm{back}},\] where \(\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}\) is the entrywise-to-operator norm constant of Lemma 22. Then the real diagonalization associated with \(F\) and \(\tau\) has condition number at most \(\kappa_\star\).

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 26.

  1. Write \[E_{r,c}:=\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}.\] For these constants we use the following finite-dimensional entrywise comparison. If \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{ent}}\in\mathbb R^{r\times c}\), \(0\le M_{\mathrm{ent}}\), and \(|(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{ent}})_{ab}|\le M_{\mathrm{ent}}\) for all entries, then \[\|\mathsf A_{\mathrm{ent}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le E_{r,c}M_{\mathrm{ent}} .\] Indeed, writing \(e_j^{(c)}\) for the standard coordinate vectors and \(J_c\) for the coordinate identification in the definition of \(\operatorname{Ent}_{r,c}\), each column satisfies \[\|\mathsf A_{\mathrm{ent}}e_j^{(c)}\|_2\le rM_{\mathrm{ent}} .\] Thus, for every \(x\in\mathbb R^c\), \[\begin{aligned} \|\mathsf A_{\mathrm{ent}}x\|_2 &\le \sum_{j=1}^c |(J_cx)_j|\,\|\mathsf A_{\mathrm{ent}}e_j^{(c)}\|_2 \\ &\le c\,r\,M_{\mathrm{ent}}\|J_cx\|_2 \\ &\le E_{r,c}M_{\mathrm{ent}}\|x\|_2, \end{aligned}\] which gives the displayed operator-norm bound. In particular \(E_{r,c}\ge0\). Since the columns of \(V\) are orthonormal, every coordinate of \(V\) has absolute value at most one, and therefore \[\|V\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le E_{d_x,k}, \qquad \|V^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le E_{k,d_x}.\] The same comparison gives \[\|I_{d_x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le E_{d_x,d_x}.\]

  2. Let \(\Upsilon\) and \(\Upsilon^{-1}\) be the coordinate matrix and coordinate inverse from the thin factorization \(B=V\Upsilon\). Since \(V^\top V=I_k\), \[\Upsilon=V^\top B .\] Thus, for coordinate indices \(r,u\), \[|\Upsilon_{ru}| = \left|\sum_{i=1}^{d_x}V_{ir}B_{iu}\right| \le \sum_{i=1}^{d_x}|V_{ir}|\,|B_{iu}| \le \sum_{i=1}^{d_x}L = d_xL .\] Consequently, \[\|\Upsilon^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le E_{k,k}(d_xL), \qquad \|(\Upsilon^{-1})^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le E_{k,k}\sigma_0^{-1}.\]

  3. Define the forward and backward ambient maps \[\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}} := V(\Upsilon^{-1})^\top V^\top+(I_{d_x}-VV^\top), \qquad \Phi_{\mathrm{back}} := V\Upsilon^\top V^\top+(I_{d_x}-VV^\top).\] Using submultiplicativity, the triangle inequality, and the bounds above, \[\begin{aligned} \|\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} &\le \|V(\Upsilon^{-1})^\top V^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}} + \|I_{d_x}-VV^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\\ &\le E_{d_x,k}E_{k,k}E_{k,d_x}\sigma_0^{-1} + \bigl(E_{d_x,d_x}+E_{d_x,k}E_{k,d_x}\bigr) = K_{\mathrm{forw}}, \end{aligned}\] and similarly \[\begin{aligned} \|\Phi_{\mathrm{back}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} &\le \|V\Upsilon^\top V^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}} + \|I_{d_x}-VV^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\\ &\le E_{d_x,k}E_{k,k}E_{k,d_x}(d_xL) + \bigl(E_{d_x,d_x}+E_{d_x,k}E_{k,d_x}\bigr) = K_{\mathrm{back}} . \end{aligned}\] Both constants are nonnegative because \(L\ge0\), \(\sigma_0>0\), and \(E_{r,c}\ge0\).

  4. Let \((q_j)_{j=1}^{d_x}\) be the ambient orthonormal extension of the signal columns used in the associated real diagonalization, and let the diagonalizing basis vectors be \[s_j:=\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}}q_j,\qquad j=1,\ldots,d_x .\] The backward map is the inverse of the forward map. To see this, put \(P_\perp:=I_{d_x}-VV^\top\). Then \[V^\top P_\perp=0,\qquad P_\perp V=0,\qquad P_\perp^2=P_\perp,\] and the identities \(\Upsilon\Upsilon^{-1}=I_k\) and \(\Upsilon^{-1}\Upsilon=I_k\) give \[\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}}\Phi_{\mathrm{back}} = VV^\top+P_\perp = I_{d_x}, \qquad \Phi_{\mathrm{back}}\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}} = VV^\top+P_\perp = I_{d_x}.\] Hence the inverse-coordinate matrix of the basis \((s_j)_j\) is computed by first applying \(\Phi_{\mathrm{back}}\) and then taking coordinates in the orthonormal basis \((q_j)_j\).

  5. Let \(\mathsf{Basis}_{\mathrm{diag}}\) be the matrix with columns \(s_j\), and let \(\mathsf{Basis}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{-1}\) be its coordinate inverse. For every pair of ambient indices \(i,j\), \[|(\mathsf{Basis}_{\mathrm{diag}})_{ij}| = |(s_j)_i| \le \|s_j\|_2 = \|\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}}q_j\|_2 \le \|\Phi_{\mathrm{forw}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|q_j\|_2 \le K_{\mathrm{forw}},\] because \(\|q_j\|_2=1\). Therefore \[\|\mathsf{Basis}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le E_{d_x,d_x}K_{\mathrm{forw}} .\] Likewise, for the inverse-coordinate entries, \[|(\mathsf{Basis}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{-1})_{ij}| = |\langle q_i,\Phi_{\mathrm{back}}e_j\rangle| \le \|q_i\|_2\|\Phi_{\mathrm{back}}e_j\|_2 \le K_{\mathrm{back}},\] so \[\|\mathsf{Basis}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le E_{d_x,d_x}K_{\mathrm{back}} .\] The condition number of the associated real diagonalization is the product of these two operator norms, hence \[\operatorname{cond} \le \bigl(E_{d_x,d_x}K_{\mathrm{forw}}\bigr) \bigl(E_{d_x,d_x}K_{\mathrm{back}}\bigr) = E_{d_x,d_x}^{\,2}K_{\mathrm{forw}}K_{\mathrm{back}} = \kappa_\star .\]

∎

The definition matches the ordering convention in Definition 30 and Algorithm 5. The invariance lemma records the compatibility needed to read ordered masses from any valid representative of the same aggregated atomic law.

Spectral repair and finite-net benchmark

This appendix collects two auxiliary constructions used to connect the population modulus with sample-level procedures. The first is a spectral repair handle that turns empirical spectral information into a positive real atomic law even when eigenvalues are locally coalescent. The second is a class-advised finite-net benchmark that selects a stored admissible summary and then runs the exact spectral construction at that representative. Both constructions use finite-dimensional perturbation ideas familiar from matrix spectral analysis (Bauer et al., 1960; Kato, 1995; Davis et al., 1970; Stewart et al., 1990) and the moment-recovery perspective behind Wasserstein estimation from moments (Wu et al., 2020).

The repair handle records the finite moment target and the positive-law replacement used in the modulus-to-estimation route.

Algorithm 6 [def:constructive-repair-handle] (Constructive repair handle \(m\)).

The constructive repair handle is defined for laws in Definition 32 as follows.

  1. At the realized empirical summary, define the first \(2k\) spectral moments by \[m=(m_j)_{0\le j<2k}.\]

  2. Among all valid positive \(k\)-atomic laws \(\sum_i w_i\delta_{x_i}\), choose a minimizer of \[\sum_{j=0}^{2k-1}\left|m_j-\sum_i w_i x_i^j\right|.\]

  3. Extract the repaired real probability measure from the minimizing positive \(k\)-atomic law, transporting aggregate projector mass across each cluster diameter after replacing individual eigenvectors inside overlapping localization discs by Kato contour projectors.

  4. For the labeled-weight converse, differentiate the Bernoulli factorization of \(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\) and solve the linear score-cancellation equations for the proxy and treatment primitives so that a weight displacement \(h\) preserves the factorization and the observed score has order \(g h\).

⊢ Lean

Algorithm 6 isolates the spectral information needed for positive real-law repair. The moment vector \(m\) contains the first \(2k\) empirical spectral moments, and the minimizing positive atomic law supplies a real probability measure compatible with those moments. The contour-projector clause expresses the standard perturbation treatment of clustered spectra: inside overlapping localization discs, aggregate eigenspaces carry the stable law-level mass.

The finite-net benchmark makes this repair logic an exact-real finite search after a class-dependent representative library has been fixed.

Algorithm 7 [def:finite-net-program] (Finite-net program \(\operatorname{NetProgram}(A,\omega)\)).

The inputs are a stored representative library \(A=(A_i)_i\) and an observed sample \(\omega\).

  1. For each representative \(A_i\), let \(S_i=(M_{0,i},M_{1,i},N_{0,i},N_{1,i},m_{X,i})\) be its stored five-block summary, and let \[\operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(i)\] be its position in the fixed lexicographic library order.

  2. Define the spectral law attached to representative \(A_i\) by \[\lambda^{\mathrm{spec}}(A_i),\] the real atomic effect law returned by the thresholded exact-real spectral run for \(A_i\).

  3. For the empirical five-block summary \[\widehat S_n(\omega) = (\widehat M_{0,n},\widehat M_{1,n},\widehat N_{0,n},\widehat N_{1,n},\widehat m_{X,n}),\] define the comparison criterion \[\Delta_i(\omega) := \lVert M_{0,i}-\widehat M_{0,n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}} + \lVert M_{1,i}-\widehat M_{1,n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}} + \lVert N_{0,i}-\widehat N_{0,n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}} + \lVert N_{1,i}-\widehat N_{1,n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}} + \lVert m_{X,i}-\widehat m_{X,n}\rVert_2.\]

  4. If the library is nonempty, define the selected representative index by \[\widehat i(\omega) \in \operatorname*{arg\,min}_{i} \bigl(\Delta_i(\omega),\operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(i)\bigr),\] with the minimum taken in lexicographic order.

  5. The finite-library spectral program returns the selected representative and its spectral law, \[\operatorname{NetProgram}(A,\omega) := \bigl(A_{\widehat i(\omega)},\lambda^{\mathrm{spec}}(A_{\widehat i(\omega)})\bigr),\] when the library is nonempty, and returns the canonical zero law when the library is empty. Its returned effect-law component is \[\operatorname{law}(\operatorname{NetProgram}(A,\omega)).\]

⊢ Lean

Algorithm 7 defines the library selector at the level of stored summaries. The criterion is the same five-block \(d_S\)-type comparison used throughout the paper, and lexicographic tie-breaking makes the selected representative a total deterministic function of the sample and the library.

The estimator below specifies how the representative library is built from an \(\epsilon_n\)-net of admissible summaries and how the selected representative is converted into an atomic law.

Algorithm 8 [def:finite-net-law-estimator] (Finite-net estimator \(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}\)).

The input is the empirical summary \(\widehat S_n\); the tuning constants are \[D:=4d_zd_x+d_x,\qquad b:=4\sqrt{d_zd_x}+\sqrt{d_x},\qquad \epsilon_n:=n^{-1/2}.\]

  1. Partition the coordinate box \([-L,L]^D\) into a deterministic lexicographically ordered grid of half-open cubes whose side length is at most \[\epsilon_n/b.\]

  2. If \(\mathscr S=\varnothing\), set \[\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}:=\delta_0\] and stop.

  3. For each grid cube that intersects \(\mathscr S\), choose one representative \[q_{n,\ell}\in\mathscr S.\] Let \(\widehat q_n\) be the smallest-index minimizer \[\widehat q_n \in \arg\min_{\ell} d_S(q_{n,\ell},\widehat S_n).\]

  4. At \(\widehat q_n\), form the vertically stacked proxy moment and retain the \(k\) right singular directions whose singular values exceed \(\pi_0\sigma_0^2/2\). Denote the resulting orthonormal basis by \[V.\] Using this basis, form the compressed effect operator, left anchor, and right anchor \[\Delta,\qquad a,\qquad c\] from the compressed-operator formulas.

  5. For each distinct eigenvalue \(\lambda\) of \(\Delta\), define the spectral projector \[E_\lambda := \prod_{\gamma\in\operatorname{spec}(\Delta):\,\gamma\ne\lambda} \frac{\Delta-\gamma I_k}{\lambda-\gamma},\] with the empty product equal to \(I_k\).

  6. Output the atomic law \[\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}} := \sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}(\Delta)} a^\top E_\lambda c\,\delta_\lambda .\]

⊢ Lean

The estimator \(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}\) is class-advised through the choice of admissible representatives \(q_{n,\ell}\). Once the nearest representative is selected, the construction returns to the compressed-operator formula from Definition 39: thresholding fixes the rank-\(k\) signal space, and the spectral projectors aggregate the anchor weights attached to each distinct eigenvalue.

Theorem 8 [thm:polynomial-net-law-estimator] (Polynomial finite-net estimator).

Fix integers \(k,d_x,d_z\) and real constants \(L,\pi_0,\sigma_0\). Suppose

  • (Dimensions.) \(2\le k\), \(k\le d_x\), and \(k\le d_z\).

  • (Bounds and margins.) \(1\le L\), \(0<\pi_0\le (2k)^{-1}\), and \(0<\sigma_0\le 1\).

Then there are constants \(C_{\mathrm{mod}}>0\) and \(C>0\) such that, for every integer \(n\ge 1\), there is a class-dependent finite library \(A=(A_i)_i\) of representative summaries as in Algorithm 8 with the following properties. For every uniform provider of the fixed-parameter exact-real primitives on the admissible core parameter domain, the finite-net estimator \(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}\) is Borel measurable, and \[|A|\le C n^{(4d_zd_x+d_x)/2}.\] For every observed sample \(\omega=(O_1,\ldots,O_n)\in O_{d_x,d_z}^n\), the operation count of the same exact-real execution that returns \(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}(\omega)\) is at most \[C\{n+n^{(4d_zd_x+d_x)/2}\}.\] Its trace is exactly the empirical-summary trace, followed by the exhaustive finite-library search trace, and followed by the selected spectral-run trace when a representative is selected; if no representative is selected, the final spectral-run trace is empty.

If the program selects index \(i\) on sample \(\omega\), then \(S_i\) is a nearest library summary to the empirical summary \(\widehat S_n(\omega)\) of Definition 35: \[d_S(S_i,\widehat S_n(\omega))\le d_S(S_j,\widehat S_n(\omega)) \qquad\text{for every }j.\] Among all tied nearest summaries, \(i\) has minimal lexicographic rank: \[d_S(S_i,\widehat S_n(\omega))=d_S(S_j,\widehat S_n(\omega)) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(i)\le \operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(j).\] For the exact spectral run at \(S_i\), the estimator equals the run’s returned effect law. For each treatment value \(t\), the observed proxy moment at \(S_i\), multiplied by the run’s basis matrix, defines an injective Euclidean linear map. The stacked proxy moment at \(S_i\) satisfies the exact threshold characterization \[\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}\le \sigma_j(\operatorname{stack}(M_0(S_i),M_1(S_i))) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad j<k,\] for every singular-value index \(j\). Every eigenvalue of the compressed operator formed at \(S_i\) with the run’s basis and spans appears among the eigenvalues returned by that run.

For every stored representative \(S_i\) and every rectangular perturbation \(H\in\mathbb R^{2d_z\times d_x}\), \[\|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2}\] implies that singular-value thresholding at \(\pi_0\sigma_0^2/2\) recovers exactly the \(k\)-dimensional matrix signal space for \[\operatorname{stack}(M_0(S_i),M_1(S_i))+H.\] For every index \(i\), there is a model law \(Q\in\mathcal M\) from Definition 32 whose observable summary is \(S_i\), and the exact spectral run at \(S_i\) returns the quotient law \(\nu_Q\). Each returned representative effect law is a valid atomic law.

For every \(P\in\mathcal M\), every observed sample \(\omega\in O_{d_x,d_z}^n\) satisfies \[W_1\!\left(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}(\omega),\nu_P\right) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\left\{2d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))+n^{-1/2}\right\}.\] Finally, for every tail level \(\eta\) with \(0<\eta<1/2\) and every \(P\in\mathcal M\), \[Q_P^{(n)}\!\left( W_1\!\left(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}},\nu_P\right) > C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} \right) \le \eta .\]

⊢ Lean

Theorem 8 establishes the finite-net benchmark. For fixed dimensions and fixed conditioning constants, the representative library has polynomial size in \(n\), the online exact-real operation count is polynomial in the same sense, and the selected summary is the lexicographically first nearest library representative. The deterministic \(W_1\) inequality converts empirical summary error plus the \(n^{-1/2}\) mesh scale into quotient-law error, and the final tail bound applies the same concentration scale used for the main law estimator.

The intuition is that the admissible summary image is compact and finite-dimensional, so an \(n^{-1/2}\)-mesh supplies a representative close enough for the gap-free modulus to absorb the discretization error. At the chosen representative, the proxy-rank margin makes singular-value thresholding recover the correct \(k\)-dimensional signal space under perturbations below \(\pi_0\sigma_0^2/2\). The exact spectral run then returns the quotient law for a model law with that stored summary, and the modulus transfers the distance from the empirical summary to \(W_1\) distance between effect laws.

Proofs and verification note

This appendix collects the proof layer for the paper’s formal results. The arguments establish the population stability statement in Theorem 1, the measurable repair and estimation claims in Proposition 3, Proposition 4, and Theorem 2, the confidence and reporting guarantees in Theorems 3, 4, and 5, and the local converse statements in Theorem 6, Proposition 6, Proposition 7, and Theorem 7.

The proof of Theorem 1 uses the compact summary closure from Proposition 2 and the quotient moment representation in Definition 43. The finite-dimensional moment map gives a Lipschitz modulus from the observable summary coordinates to the \(W_1\) law target on the compact domain. The extension clause follows by completing the admissible image under \(d_S\), with Algorithm 1 and Proposition 3 supplying the measurable nearest-summary selection needed to evaluate the extended map at empirical summaries.

The estimation proofs combine this deterministic modulus with concentration for the empirical five-block summary in Definition 35. For the repaired estimator, nearest-summary projection keeps the evaluated summary within the same concentration scale as the population summary. For the structured-lattice estimator, Algorithm 2 and Proposition 4 give a duplicate-free finite search whose deterministic error is bounded by the empirical summary error and the \(n^{-1/2}\) lattice scale. These two routes yield the joint root-\(n\) tail statement in Theorem 2.

The confidence-set proofs use the same concentration event with the objects in Algorithm 3. The summary-inversion set inherits its diameter from the modulus on \(\mathcal K\), while the algorithmic outer set inherits its radius from the deterministic lattice bound. The transport-plan formulation in Definition 2 gives the finite representation used in Theorem 3. The cluster-report argument then applies the atom floor and \(W_1\) transport budget in Algorithm 4 to associate true atoms with empirical components, yielding the support containment, mass-coverage, and interval-length conclusions in Theorem 4.

For ordered weights, the proof of Theorem 5 works on the gap-local stratum in Definition 41. The positive effect gap converts law-level transport error into an \(\ell_1\) error bound for the effect-ordered mass vector defined in Definition 30 and estimated by Algorithm 5. This gives the clipped inverse-gap upper rate stated in the theorem.

The lower-bound proofs use the explicit two-class witness in Definition 55, its validity certificate in Proposition 5, and the local experiments in Definitions 56 and 57. Standard two-point testing inequalities (Cam, 1986; Polyanskiy et al., 2019) convert the displayed Kullback–Leibler bounds and target separations into the local risk lower bounds in Theorem 6. The same comparisons give the same-class minimax rates in Propositions 6 and 7. The transfer statement in Theorem 7 expresses the same witness-pair logic for comparator classes stated in the published VMW vocabulary (Virk et al., 2026).

Proofs of the main results

Proof of Proposition 1.

Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\) satisfying the displayed hypotheses, and set \[L_\tau:=\frac{4L\sqrt{d_z}}{\sigma_0}.\] All model restrictions used below are the components of \(\mathcal M\) in Definition 32.

  1. For each treatment arm \(t\in\{0,1\}\), Lemma 3 gives \[P(T=t)\ge k\pi_0 .\] Since \(k\ge2\) and \(\pi_0>0\), this arm probability is positive. Define \[\Gamma_t := \operatorname{diag}\left\{ \frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}:1\le u\le k \right\}.\] The diagonal entries are \[\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)} = P(U=u\mid T=t),\] so Lemma 4 yields \[M_t(P)=A_t\Gamma_tB^\top = A_t\operatorname{diag}\{P(U=u\mid T=t):1\le u\le k\}B^\top .\]
