  2. Latent-arm positivity gives, for every latent class \(u\), \[\frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)} \ge P(U=u,T=t) \ge \pi_0,\] because \(P(T=t)\le1\). Hence, for every \(\beta\in\mathbb R^k\), \[\|\Gamma_t\beta\|_2^2 = \sum_{u=1}^k \Gamma_t(u,u)^2\beta_u^2 \ge \pi_0^2\sum_{u=1}^k\beta_u^2 = \pi_0^2\|\beta\|_2^2,\] and therefore \[\sigma_k(\Gamma_t)\ge\pi_0 .\] The proxy-rank margin in Assumption 10 gives \[\sigma_k(A_t)\ge\sigma_0, \qquad \sigma_k(B)\ge\sigma_0 .\] Using the product lower bound for full-column-rank factors in the factorization above, \[\sigma_k\{M_t(P)\} = \sigma_k(A_t\Gamma_tB^\top) \ge \sigma_k(A_t)\sigma_k(\Gamma_t)\sigma_k(B) \ge \pi_0\sigma_0^2 .\]

  3. Let \[\mathcal H(P):= \operatorname{row}\!\left(\operatorname{stack}(M_0(P),M_1(P))\right) \subseteq\mathbb R^{d_x},\] and let \(V\in\mathbb R^{d_x\times k}\) have orthonormal columns spanning \(\mathcal H(P)\). For the fixed arm \(t\), the row space of \(M_t(P)\) is contained in \(\mathcal H(P)\): \[\operatorname{range}\{M_t(P)^\top\}\subseteq \mathcal H(P)=\operatorname{range}(V).\] The lower bound from the previous step gives \(\sigma_k\{M_t(P)\}>0\), so this row space has dimension at least \(k\). Since \(\operatorname{range}(V)\) has dimension \(k\), the two spaces are equal. The map \(r\mapsto Vr\) is an isometry from \(\mathbb R^k\) onto this row space, and composing \(M_t(P)\) with that isometry preserves its positive singular values. Thus \[\sigma_k\{M_t(P)V\} = \sigma_k\{M_t(P)\}, \qquad \pi_0\sigma_0^2\le \sigma_k\{M_t(P)V\}.\]

  4. The stacked proxy signal margin is supplied by Lemma 24. Applied with the present model membership and numerical hypotheses, it gives \[\pi_0\sigma_0^2 \le \sigma_k\!\left(\operatorname{stack}(M_0(P),M_1(P))\right),\] with the displayed one-based singular-value indexing of the statement.

  5. By Definition 28, \[M_t(P)=E_P[ZX^\top\mid T=t], \qquad N_t(P)=E_P[YZX^\top\mid T=t], \qquad m_X(P)=E_P[X].\] The envelope components of \(\mathcal M\) give \[\|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L, \qquad \|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L, \qquad \|X\|_2\le L \quad P\text{-a.s.}\] Since \(P(T=t)>0\), Jensen’s inequality for the two conditional matrix means and for the unconditional vector mean yields \[\|M_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L, \qquad \|N_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L, \qquad \|m_X(P)\|_2\le L .\]

  6. Fix \(u\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\) and \(t\in\{0,1\}\), and put \[\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}:=\{U=u,T=t\}, \qquad \mathsf{Class}_u:=\{U=u\}.\] Latent-arm positivity gives \[P(\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t})\ge\pi_0>0.\] Let \(e_u^{(k)}\) be the \(u\)th canonical vector in \(\mathbb R^k\). The proxy-rank margin implies \[\|A_te_u^{(k)}\|_2 \ge \sigma_k(A_t)\|e_u^{(k)}\|_2 \ge \sigma_0 .\] Since \(d_z\ge k\ge2\), some coordinate \(\jmath_{u,t}\in\{1,\ldots,d_z\}\) satisfies \[\left|E_P[Z_{\jmath_{u,t}}\mid \mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}]\right| = |A_t(\jmath_{u,t},u)| \ge \frac{\sigma_0}{\sqrt{d_z}} .\] Define \[\zeta_{u,t}:=\frac{\sigma_0}{2\sqrt{d_z}}, \qquad \mathsf{Large}_{u,t}:=\{|Z_{\jmath_{u,t}}|\ge \zeta_{u,t}\}.\] If \(P(\mathsf{Large}_{u,t}\mid\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t})=0\), then \[\left|E_P[Z_{\jmath_{u,t}}\mid \mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}]\right| \le E_P[|Z_{\jmath_{u,t}}|\mid \mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}] \le \zeta_{u,t} < \frac{\sigma_0}{\sqrt{d_z}},\] a contradiction. Hence \[P(\mathsf{Large}_{u,t}\mid\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t})>0 .\] By Lemma 5, \[|YZ_{\jmath_{u,t}}|\le L \quad P\text{-a.s. on }\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}.\] The reference-proxy separation component in Assumption 1 gives \(Z_{\jmath_{u,t}}\perp Y\mid \mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}\). Therefore positive conditional probability for \[\mathsf{Bad}_{u,t}:= \left\{|Y|>\frac{L}{\zeta_{u,t}}\right\}\] would give \[P(\mathsf{Large}_{u,t}\cap\mathsf{Bad}_{u,t}\mid\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}) = P(\mathsf{Large}_{u,t}\mid\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}) P(\mathsf{Bad}_{u,t}\mid\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}) >0.\] On this intersection, \[L<|Y|\,|Z_{\jmath_{u,t}}|=|YZ_{\jmath_{u,t}}|,\] contradicting the product envelope. Thus \[|Y| \le \frac{L}{\zeta_{u,t}} = \frac{2L\sqrt{d_z}}{\sigma_0} = \frac{L_\tau}{2} \quad P\text{-a.s. conditional on }\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}.\] On \(\{T=t\}\), the consistency component in Assumption 3 gives \(Y=Y(t)\) almost surely. Inside the conditional law given \(\mathsf{Class}_u\), the event \(\{T=t\}\) has positive probability, and the latent-ignorability component in Assumption 4 gives \(Y(t)\perp T\mid U\). If \[\left\{|Y(t)|>\frac{L_\tau}{2}\right\}\] had positive conditional probability given \(\mathsf{Class}_u\), independence would give a positive-probability violation on \(\mathsf{Cell}_{u,t}\), contradicting the displayed cell bound. Hence \[|Y(t)|\le\frac{L_\tau}{2} \quad P\text{-a.s. conditional on }U=u .\] Finally, Lemmas 6 and 7 give \[|\mu_{tu}|\le \frac{L_\tau}{2}, \qquad |\tau_u|\le L_\tau .\]

  7. It remains to identify membership in the published VMW parameter class. The scope in Definition 52, together with the cited VMW correspondence recorded in Definition 60 and (Virk et al., 2026), identifies \(\mathsf{Model}_{\mathrm{VMW}}(k,d_x,d_z,P)\) with the qualitative structural conditions summarized there. Those conditions are supplied as follows. The proxy independences, consistency, and armwise latent ignorability are components of \(\mathcal M\). The proxy-rank margin and \(\sigma_0>0\) give full column rank: \[\sigma_k(A_0)\ge\sigma_0>0,\qquad \sigma_k(A_1)\ge\sigma_0>0,\qquad \sigma_k(B)\ge\sigma_0>0.\] Latent-arm positivity gives \[P(U=u,T=t)\ge\pi_0>0\] for every \(u\) and \(t\), and hence the corresponding latent treatment probabilities are strictly positive. Together with \(2\le k\), \(k\le d_x\), and \(k\le d_z\), these are exactly the qualitative conditions for \(P\in\mathsf{Model}_{\mathrm{VMW}}(k,d_x,d_z)\).

∎
Proof of Proposition 2.

  1. First record the coordinate estimate used to pass from operator envelopes to coordinate boxes. For any \(H\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}\), any \(\iota_z\in\{1,\ldots,d_z\}\), and any \(\iota_x\in\{1,\ldots,d_x\}\), \[|H_{\iota_z\iota_x}| = \|(H e_{\iota_x})_{\iota_z}\| \le \|H e_{\iota_x}\|_2 \le \|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|e_{\iota_x}\|_2 = \|H\|_{\mathrm{op}},\] where \(e_{\iota_x}\) is the \(\iota_x\)th canonical basis vector of \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\).

  2. Define the finite coordinate boxes \[\mathcal H_{\mathrm{mat}}(L) = \Bigl\{H\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}: -L\le H_{\iota_z\iota_x}\le L \text{ for all }\iota_z,\iota_x\Bigr\},\] \[\mathcal H_X(L) = \Bigl\{h\in\mathbb R^{d_x}: -L\le h_{\iota_x}\le L \text{ for all }\iota_x\Bigr\},\] and \[\mathcal H_S(L) = \mathcal H_{\mathrm{mat}}(L)^4\times \mathcal H_X(L).\] Each factor is a finite product of closed intervals, hence compact, and therefore \(\mathcal H_S(L)\) is compact. For a five-block summary \(s=(M_0,M_1,N_0,N_1,m_X)\), define the coordinate map \[\Phi(s)=(M_0,M_1,N_0,N_1,m_X).\] This map is a homeomorphism from the space of five-block summaries onto this finite product coordinate space. Thus \[\Phi^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal H_S(L)\bigr)\] is compact in the summary topology.

  3. Let \(P\in\mathcal M\). For \(t\in\{0,1\}\), put \[\mathsf{Arm}_t(P)=\{T=t\}.\] By Assumption 9, together with \(2\le k\) and \(0<\pi_0\), the arm \(\mathsf{Arm}_t(P)\) has positive mass. Let \(P^{\mathrm{arm}}_t\) be the probability law obtained by restricting \(P\) to \(\mathsf{Arm}_t(P)\) and normalizing by this positive mass. By Definition 28, the armwise blocks in \(S(P)\) are conditional means; on this positive arm, equivalently, \[M_t(P)=\int ZX^\top\,dP^{\mathrm{arm}}_t, \qquad N_t(P)=\int YZX^\top\,dP^{\mathrm{arm}}_t,\] and \(m_X(P)=\int X\,dP\). By Assumption 7, the proxy-product envelope restricts to \(P^{\mathrm{arm}}_t\): \(\|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L\) \(P^{\mathrm{arm}}_t\)-almost surely. Since \(1\le L\), the matrix-valued integral is integrable and \[\begin{aligned} \|M_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} &=\left\|\int ZX^\top\,dP^{\mathrm{arm}}_t\right\|_{\mathrm{op}} \\ &\le \int \|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,dP^{\mathrm{arm}}_t \le \int L\,dP^{\mathrm{arm}}_t = L . \end{aligned}\] The same normalized-restriction argument with Assumption 8 gives \[\|N_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L .\] Likewise, Assumption 6 gives \[\|m_X(P)\|_2 =\left\|\int X\,dP\right\|_2 \le \int \|X\|_2\,dP \le L .\] Hence every coordinate of \(m_X(P)\) has absolute value at most \(L\). Combining these inequalities with the coordinate estimate from the first step and the summary tuple in Definition 33 gives \[S(P)\in \Phi^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal H_S(L)\bigr).\] Since \(P\in\mathcal M\) was arbitrary, \[\mathscr S\subseteq \Phi^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal H_S(L)\bigr).\]

  4. The same block envelopes give the stated uniform \(d_S\)-bound. For \(s=S(P)\in\mathscr S\), \[\begin{aligned} d_S(s,0) &= \|M_0(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|M_1(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|N_0(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\|N_1(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +\Bigl(\sum_{\iota_x} m_X(P)_{\iota_x}^2\Bigr)^{1/2}. \end{aligned}\] For the final term, \[\Bigl(\sum_{\iota_x} m_X(P)_{\iota_x}^2\Bigr)^{1/2} = \Bigl\|\sum_{\iota_x} m_X(P)_{\iota_x} e_{\iota_x}\Bigr\|_2 \le \sum_{\iota_x}|m_X(P)_{\iota_x}| \le d_x L.\] Therefore \[d_S(S(P),0)\le 4L+d_xL=(4+d_x)L.\] Taking the scalar bound \[B_{\mathrm{sum}}=(4+d_x)L\] and then instantiating the existential bound with \(B_{\mathrm{sum}}\) proves the boundedness claim.

  5. Since \(\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal H_S(L))\) is compact and closed and contains \(\mathscr S\), it also contains \[\mathcal K=\overline{\mathscr S}.\] The set \(\mathcal K\) is closed by definition as a closure, so it is a closed subset of the compact set \(\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal H_S(L))\). Hence \(\mathcal K\) is compact.

  6. Finally, \[\mathcal K\neq\varnothing \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \mathscr S\neq\varnothing,\] because a closure has the same nonemptiness as the set it closes. By the definition \[\mathscr S=\{S(P):P\in\mathcal M\},\] the condition \(\mathscr S\neq\varnothing\) is equivalent to \(\mathcal M\neq\varnothing\): a law \(P\in\mathcal M\) gives \(S(P)\in\mathscr S\), and any element of \(\mathscr S\) is \(S(P)\) for some \(P\in\mathcal M\). Thus \(\mathcal K\) is nonempty if and only if \(\mathcal M\) is nonempty.

∎
Proof of Lemma 1.

Fix \(k,d_x,d_z,\pi_0,\sigma_0\) satisfying the displayed hypotheses. Let \(\mathcal I_{d_x,d_z}\) be the finite disjoint union of the following scalar summary coordinates: \[\{\operatorname{prox},\operatorname{out}\}\times\{0,1\} \times\{1,\ldots,d_z\}\times\{1,\ldots,d_x\}, \qquad \operatorname{xcoord}\times\{1,\ldots,d_x\}, \qquad \operatorname{arm}\times\{0,1\}.\] For \(O=(T,X,Z,Y)\in O_{d_x,d_z}\) from Definition 18, define \[\varphi_{\operatorname{prox},t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}(O) := \mathbf 1\{T=t\}Z_{\mathfrak z}X_{\mathfrak x}, \qquad \varphi_{\operatorname{out},t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}(O) := \mathbf 1\{T=t\}YZ_{\mathfrak z}X_{\mathfrak x},\] \[\varphi_{\operatorname{xcoord},\mathfrak x}(O):=X_{\mathfrak x}, \qquad \varphi_{\operatorname{arm},t}(O):=\mathbf 1\{T=t\}.\] Set \[\varsigma_{\iota}(L):= \begin{cases} L, & \iota\text{ is a proxy, outcome, or target-proxy coordinate},\\ 1, & \iota\text{ is an arm coordinate}. \end{cases}\] The bounded target-proxy, bounded proxy-product, and bounded outcome-proxy components of Definition 32, namely Assumptions 6, 7, and 8, give, for every \(\iota\in\mathcal I_{d_x,d_z}\), \[|\varphi_\iota(O)|\le \varsigma_\iota(L) \quad\text{for \(P_O\)-almost every }O,\] where \(P_O\) is the observed-data marginal of Definition 20.

Let \(\kappa_{d_z,d_x}\ge0\) be a finite coordinate-to-operator-norm constant such that, for every \(d_z\times d_x\) real matrix \(H\) and every \(u_{\mathrm{coord}}\ge0\), \[\max_{\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}|H_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x}| \le u_{\mathrm{coord}} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le \kappa_{d_z,d_x}u_{\mathrm{coord}}.\] Such a constant exists because, if each coordinate of a vector in \(\mathbb R^r\) has absolute value at most \(u_{\mathrm{coord}}\), then its Euclidean norm is at most \(r u_{\mathrm{coord}}\); applying this to each column and then taking the supremum over unit input vectors gives a finite constant depending only on \(d_z,d_x\). We shall also use the corresponding vector estimate \[\max_{\mathfrak x}|v_{\mathfrak x}|\le u_{\mathrm{coord}} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \|v\|_2\le d_xu_{\mathrm{coord}} .\]

Put \[\chi_{\mathrm{sum}}:=|\mathcal I_{d_x,d_z}|=4d_zd_x+d_x+2,\] \[K_{\mathrm{sm}}:=\frac{16\kappa_{d_z,d_x}}{k\pi_0}+d_x, \qquad K_{\mathrm{lg}}:=4(\kappa_{d_z,d_x}+1)+d_x+1,\] and choose \[C_0:= \max\left\{\chi_{\mathrm{sum}}+1,\ 2K_{\mathrm{sm}},\ \frac{4K_{\mathrm{lg}}}{k\pi_0}\right\}.\] Then \(1\le C_0\). Since \(2\le k\) and \(0<\pi_0\), the product \(k\pi_0\) is positive, so all denominators in this definition are positive.

Now fix \(L\ge1\), \(n\ge1\), \(P\in\mathcal M\), and \(0<\eta<1/2\). Define \[\zeta_{\eta,n}:= \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}}, \qquad \beta_{\eta,n}:=2\zeta_{\eta,n}.\] For each \(\iota\in\mathcal I_{d_x,d_z}\), Hoeffding’s inequality under \(Q_P^{(n)}=P_O^{\otimes n}\) gives \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ \varsigma_\iota(L)\beta_{\eta,n} \le \left| \frac1n\sum_{\mathfrak r=1}^n\varphi_\iota(O_{\mathfrak r}) - \int \varphi_\iota\,dP_O \right| \right\} \le 2\left(\frac{\eta}{C_0}\right)^2 .\] Indeed, \(\varphi_\iota\in[-\varsigma_\iota(L),\varsigma_\iota(L)]\) \(P_O\)-almost surely, the interval length is \(2\varsigma_\iota(L)\), and the choice \(\beta_{\eta,n}=2\sqrt{\log(C_0/\eta)/n}\) reduces the exponential factor to \(\exp\{-2\log(C_0/\eta)\}\).

Let \[\mathcal B_{\mathrm{dev}} := \left\{\omega:\exists\iota\in\mathcal I_{d_x,d_z},\ \varsigma_\iota(L)\beta_{\eta,n} \le \left| \frac1n\sum_{\mathfrak r=1}^n\varphi_\iota(O_{\mathfrak r}) - \int \varphi_\iota\,dP_O \right|\right\}.\] By the union bound and \(\chi_{\mathrm{sum}}\le C_0\), \[Q_P^{(n)}(\mathcal B_{\mathrm{dev}}) \le \chi_{\mathrm{sum}}\,2\left(\frac{\eta}{C_0}\right)^2 \le \frac{2\eta^2}{C_0} \le 2\eta^2 \le \eta .\] Also set \[\mathcal B_{\mathrm{supp}} := \left\{\omega:\exists \mathfrak r,\exists\iota\in\mathcal I_{d_x,d_z},\ |\varphi_\iota(O_{\mathfrak r})|>\varsigma_\iota(L)\right\}.\] The \(P_O\)-almost-sure coordinate envelope transported to the product experiment gives \[Q_P^{(n)}(\mathcal B_{\mathrm{supp}})=0 .\]

The population summary blocks obey \[\|M_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L,\qquad \|N_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L,\qquad \|m_X(P)\|_2\le L .\] For the armwise blocks, Lemma 3 gives positive arm probabilities, and Jensen’s inequality applied to the conditional moments in Definition 28 transfers the almost-sure bounds on \(ZX^\top\) and \(YZX^\top\). For \(m_X(P)\), Jensen’s inequality applies directly to the unconditional target-proxy mean.

It remains to prove a deterministic implication off the two bad events. Write \[\theta_t:=P_O\{O:T=t\}, \qquad \widehat\theta_t(\omega):=\frac{N_{t,n}(\omega)}{n}.\] By Lemma 3, transported through Definition 20, \[k\pi_0\le \theta_t \qquad(t\in\{0,1\}).\] First suppose that \[\beta_{\eta,n}<\frac{k\pi_0}{2}.\] On \(\mathcal B_{\mathrm{dev}}^c\), \[|\widehat\theta_t-\theta_t|<\beta_{\eta,n}, \qquad \widehat\theta_t>\frac{k\pi_0}{2}.\] For \(\bullet\in\{\operatorname{prox},\operatorname{out}\}\), put \[\overline\Phi^\bullet_{t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}(\omega) := \frac1n\sum_{\mathfrak r=1}^n \varphi_{\bullet,t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}(O_{\mathfrak r}).\] The coordinate integral identities are \[\int\varphi_{\operatorname{prox},t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}\,dP_O = \theta_t\,M_t(P)_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x}, \qquad \int\varphi_{\operatorname{out},t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x}\,dP_O = \theta_t\,N_t(P)_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x}.\] Because \(\widehat\theta_t>0\), the total empty-arm convention in Definition 34 gives \[(\widehat M_{t,n}-M_t(P))_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x} = \frac{ \{\overline\Phi^{\operatorname{prox}}_{t,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x} -\theta_tM_t(P)_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x}\} + (\theta_t-\widehat\theta_t)M_t(P)_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x} }{\widehat\theta_t},\] and the same identity with \(\widehat N_{t,n},N_t(P)\) and \(\overline\Phi^{\operatorname{out}}\). Since each population entry is bounded by \(L\), the preceding displays imply, for both matrix types, \[\left| (\widehat M_{t,n}-M_t(P))_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x} \right| \le \frac{4L\beta_{\eta,n}}{k\pi_0}, \qquad \left| (\widehat N_{t,n}-N_t(P))_{\mathfrak z\mathfrak x} \right| \le \frac{4L\beta_{\eta,n}}{k\pi_0}.\] The matrix norm conversion and the mean-coordinate bounds \[|(\widehat m_{X,n}-m_X(P))_{\mathfrak x}|<L\beta_{\eta,n}\] therefore yield \[d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) \le \left(\frac{16\kappa_{d_z,d_x}}{k\pi_0}+d_x\right)L\beta_{\eta,n} = 2K_{\mathrm{sm}}L\zeta_{\eta,n} \le C_0L\zeta_{\eta,n}.\]

Now suppose that \[\frac{k\pi_0}{2}\le \beta_{\eta,n}.\] On \(\mathcal B_{\mathrm{supp}}^c\), every empirical arm matrix has entries bounded by \(L\): if \(N_{t,n}=0\), the convention in Definition 34 gives the zero matrix, and if \(N_{t,n}>0\), the matrix is an average of entries whose absolute values are at most \(L\). Hence each empirical matrix block has operator norm at most \(\kappa_{d_z,d_x}L\), and \[\|\widehat m_{X,n}\|_2\le d_xL .\] Combining these empirical bounds with the population envelopes gives \[d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) \le \{4(\kappa_{d_z,d_x}+1)+d_x+1\}L = K_{\mathrm{lg}}L .\] Since \(k\pi_0/2\le\beta_{\eta,n}=2\zeta_{\eta,n}\), \[\frac{k\pi_0}{4}\le \zeta_{\eta,n},\] and therefore \[K_{\mathrm{lg}}L \le \frac{4K_{\mathrm{lg}}}{k\pi_0}L\zeta_{\eta,n} \le C_0L\zeta_{\eta,n}.\]

The two cases imply \[\left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P))> C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \subseteq \mathcal B_{\mathrm{dev}}\cup\mathcal B_{\mathrm{supp}}.\] Consequently, \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P))> C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \le Q_P^{(n)}(\mathcal B_{\mathrm{dev}}) + Q_P^{(n)}(\mathcal B_{\mathrm{supp}}) \le \eta .\]

Finally fix \(t\in\{0,1\}\). The event \(N_{t,n}=0\) is the event that all \(n\) observed records have \(T\ne t\). Since \(Q_P^{(n)}=P_O^{\otimes n}\), \[Q_P^{(n)}\{N_{t,n}=0\} = \bigl(P_O\{O:T\ne t\}\bigr)^n = \bigl(1-P_O\{O:T=t\}\bigr)^n .\] The lower bound from Lemma 3, again through Definition 20, gives \[k\pi_0\le P_O\{O:T=t\}\le 1.\] Thus \[0\le 1-P_O\{O:T=t\}\le 1-k\pi_0,\] and monotonicity of \(x\mapsto x^n\) on \([0,\infty)\) gives \[\bigl(1-P_O\{O:T=t\}\bigr)^n \le (1-k\pi_0)^n .\] This proves both asserted conclusions.

∎
Proof of Theorem 1.

  1. Put \[s_\star:=\pi_0\sigma_0^2, \qquad L_\tau:=\frac{4L\sqrt{d_z}}{\sigma_0}.\] For matrix sizes \(r_{\mathrm{row}},r_{\mathrm{col}}\), write \[\operatorname{Ent}_{r_{\mathrm{row}},r_{\mathrm{col}}} := r_{\mathrm{col}}r_{\mathrm{row}}\, \|J_{r_{\mathrm{col}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}},\] with \(J_{r_{\mathrm{col}}}\) as in Lemma 22. Define \[\begin{aligned} K_{\mathrm{forw}} &:= \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,k} \operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}\,\sigma_0^{-1} +\operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,d_x} +\operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x},\\ K_{\mathrm{back}} &:= \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,k} \operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}\,(d_xL) +\operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,d_x} +\operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,k}\operatorname{Ent}_{k,d_x}, \end{aligned}\] and \[\kappa_\star := \operatorname{Ent}_{d_x,d_x}^{\,2}K_{\mathrm{forw}}K_{\mathrm{back}} .\] The assumptions \(L\ge1\) and \(\sigma_0>0\), together with nonnegativity of the entry-norm constants, give \[0\le \kappa_\star .\] Set \[C_{\mathrm{base}} := \kappa_\star L_\tau + L\,d_x^2\kappa_\star^2 \{3s_\star^{-2}L+s_\star^{-1}\}, \qquad C_{\mathrm{mod}}:=C_{\mathrm{base}}+1 .\] Since \(s_\star>0\), \(L_\tau\ge0\), and \(\kappa_\star\ge0\), we have \[0\le C_{\mathrm{base}}, \qquad 0<C_{\mathrm{mod}} .\]

  2. Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\). Let \(B(P)\) be the target-proxy feature matrix of Definition 25. The proxy-rank margin gives \[\sigma_0\le \sigma_k(B(P)).\] Choose a thin singular-value factorization \[B(P)=V_P\Upsilon_P, \qquad V_P^\top V_P=I_k, \qquad \Upsilon_P^{-1}\Upsilon_P=\Upsilon_P\Upsilon_P^{-1}=I_k .\] The singular-value lower bound implies \[|(\Upsilon_P^{-1})_{ab}|\le \sigma_0^{-1} \qquad(1\le a,b\le k).\] For every coordinate \(i\) and latent class \(u\), latent-arm positivity gives \[0<P(U=u,T=0)\le P(U=u).\] Under the normalized restriction of \(P\) to \(\{U=u\}\), Lemma 5 gives \[|X_i|\le L\quad\text{almost surely}.\] Therefore \[\begin{aligned} |B(P)_{iu}| &=\left|E_P[X_i\mid U=u]\right|\\ &=\left|\int X_i\,dP(\,\cdot\mid U=u)\right|\\ &\le \int |X_i|\,dP(\,\cdot\mid U=u) \le L . \end{aligned}\] Thus Lemma 26 applies to \(B(P)=V_P\Upsilon_P\).

    Define the ambient contrast \[\mathfrak D_P := M_1(P)^\dagger N_1(P)-M_0(P)^\dagger N_0(P).\] By Lemmas 4 and 18, for each \(t\in\{0,1\}\), \[M_t(P)=A_t(P)\Gamma_t(P)B(P)^\top, \qquad N_t(P)=A_t(P)\Gamma_t(P) \operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(P)\}_{u=1}^kB(P)^\top ,\] where \[\Gamma_t(P)= \operatorname{diag}\left\{ \frac{P(U=u,T=t)}{P(T=t)}:1\le u\le k \right\}.\] The proxy-rank margin and Lemma 19 make \(A_t(P)\Gamma_t(P)\) injective, and the proxy-rank margin makes \(B(P)\) injective. Hence Lemma 20 yields \[M_t(P)^\dagger N_t(P) = (B(P)^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}\{\mu_{tu}(P)\}_{u=1}^kB(P)^\top .\] Subtracting the two arms gives \[\mathfrak D_P = (B(P)^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}\{\tau_u(P)\}_{u=1}^kB(P)^\top .\]

    Let \[\mathsf{Forw}_P := V_P(\Upsilon_P^{-1})^\top V_P^\top+(I_{d_x}-V_PV_P^\top), \qquad \mathsf{Back}_P := V_P\Upsilon_P^\top V_P^\top+(I_{d_x}-V_PV_P^\top).\] Then \[\mathsf{Forw}_P\mathsf{Back}_P = \mathsf{Back}_P\mathsf{Forw}_P = I_{d_x}.\] Extending the columns of \(V_P\) to an orthonormal basis of \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\), the columns obtained by applying \(\mathsf{Forw}_P\) form a real eigenbasis for \(\mathfrak D_P\). The signal eigenvalues are \(\tau_u(P)\), and the remaining eigenvalues are \(0\). Consequently \[\mathfrak D_P = \mathcal S_P\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_{P,1},\ldots,\alpha_{P,d_x}) \mathcal S_P^{-1},\] with \[\|\mathcal S_P\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathcal S_P^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \kappa_\star, \qquad |\alpha_{P,j}|\le L_\tau .\] The eigenvalue bound uses Lemma 7 on the signal coordinates and \(0\in[-L_\tau,L_\tau]\) on the remaining coordinates.

    It remains to record the two anchor identities used by the spectral representation. For each coordinate \(j\), \[\begin{aligned} m_X(P)_j &=\int X_j\,dP\\ &=\sum_{u=1}^k\int_{\{U=u\}}X_j\,dP\\ &=\sum_{u=1}^k E_P[X_j\mid U=u]\,P(U=u)\\ &=\sum_{u=1}^k B(P)_{ju}p_u(P), \end{aligned}\] where the latent classes form a finite measurable partition and \(X_j\) is integrable by Lemma 5. Hence \[m_X(P)=B(P)p(P).\] Also, since \(X_1=1\) almost surely and \(P(U=u)>0\), \[B(P)_{1u}=E_P[X_1\mid U=u]=1 \qquad(1\le u\le k),\] so the first canonical basis vector \(e_1\in\mathbb R^{d_x}\) satisfies \[B(P)^\top e_1=\mathbf 1_k .\] Therefore, for every one-Lipschitz \(\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) satisfying \(\varphi(0)=0\), \[\varphi(\mathfrak D_P) = (B(P)^\dagger)^\top \operatorname{diag}\{\varphi(\tau_u(P))\}_{u=1}^kB(P)^\top\] and \[\int \varphi\,d\nu_P = \sum_{u=1}^kp_u(P)\varphi(\tau_u(P)) = \left\langle m_X(P),\varphi(\mathfrak D_P)e_1\right\rangle .\]

  3. Fix \(P,Q\in\mathcal M\). For each arm \(t\), Proposition 1 supplies \[s_\star\le\sigma_k(M_t(P)), \qquad s_\star\le\sigma_k(M_t(Q)), \qquad \|N_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L .\] Together with the proxy factorization, these inequalities give \[\operatorname{rank}M_t(P)=\operatorname{rank}M_t(Q)=k .\] We first record the armwise perturbation estimate. Let \(\mathsf H,\mathsf H'\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}\) have the same rank \(k\), and suppose \[s_\star\le \sigma_k(\mathsf H), \qquad s_\star\le \sigma_k(\mathsf H') .\] Then \[\|\mathsf H^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le s_\star^{-1}, \qquad \|\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le s_\star^{-1},\] because the operator norm of the Moore–Penrose inverse is the reciprocal of the smallest nonzero singular value. The Penrose equations, applied with the common rank condition, give the exact moving-space identity \[\begin{aligned} \mathsf H^\dagger-\mathsf H'{}^\dagger ={}&-\mathsf H^\dagger(\mathsf H-\mathsf H')\mathsf H'{}^\dagger +\mathsf H^\dagger(\mathsf H^\dagger)^\top (\mathsf H-\mathsf H')^\top(I-\mathsf H'\mathsf H'{}^\dagger)\\ &+(I-\mathsf H^\dagger\mathsf H)(\mathsf H-\mathsf H')^\top (\mathsf H'{}^\dagger)^\top\mathsf H'{}^\dagger . \end{aligned}\] Here \(I-\mathsf H'\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\) and \(I-\mathsf H^\dagger\mathsf H\) are orthogonal projection residuals, so their operator norms are at most one. Taking norms term by term gives \[\begin{aligned} \|\mathsf H^\dagger(\mathsf H-\mathsf H')\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}} &\le s_\star^{-2}\|\mathsf H-\mathsf H'\|_{\mathrm{op}},\\ \|\mathsf H^\dagger(\mathsf H^\dagger)^\top (\mathsf H-\mathsf H')^\top(I-\mathsf H'\mathsf H'{}^\dagger)\|_{\mathrm{op}} &\le s_\star^{-2}\|\mathsf H-\mathsf H'\|_{\mathrm{op}},\\ \|(I-\mathsf H^\dagger\mathsf H)(\mathsf H-\mathsf H')^\top (\mathsf H'{}^\dagger)^\top\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}} &\le s_\star^{-2}\|\mathsf H-\mathsf H'\|_{\mathrm{op}}. \end{aligned}\] Hence \[\|\mathsf H^\dagger-\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le 3s_\star^{-2}\|\mathsf H-\mathsf H'\|_{\mathrm{op}} .\] For matrices \(\mathsf N,\mathsf N'\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}\), the algebraic split \[\mathsf H^\dagger\mathsf N-\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\mathsf N' = (\mathsf H^\dagger-\mathsf H'{}^\dagger)\mathsf N +\mathsf H'{}^\dagger(\mathsf N-\mathsf N')\] therefore implies, whenever \(\|\mathsf N\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L\), \[\|\mathsf H^\dagger\mathsf N-\mathsf H'{}^\dagger\mathsf N'\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le 3s_\star^{-2}L\,\|\mathsf H-\mathsf H'\|_{\mathrm{op}} +s_\star^{-1}\|\mathsf N-\mathsf N'\|_{\mathrm{op}} .\] Applying this estimate with \[\mathsf H=M_t(P),\quad \mathsf H'=M_t(Q),\quad \mathsf N=N_t(P),\quad \mathsf N'=N_t(Q)\] gives \[\begin{aligned} &\|M_t(P)^\dagger N_t(P)-M_t(Q)^\dagger N_t(Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\\ &\qquad\le 3s_\star^{-2}L\,\|M_t(P)-M_t(Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} +s_\star^{-1}\|N_t(P)-N_t(Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} . \end{aligned}\] Summing over \(t=0,1\) and using the definition of \(d_S\) yields \[\|\mathfrak D_P-\mathfrak D_Q\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \{3s_\star^{-2}L+s_\star^{-1}\}\, d_S(S(P),S(Q)).\] Moreover, \[\|m_X(P)-m_X(Q)\|_2\le d_S(S(P),S(Q)), \qquad \|m_X(Q)\|_2\le L, \qquad \|e_1\|_2=1 .\]

  4. Let \(\varphi\) be the normalized attaining Kantorovich–Rubinstein potential for \((\nu_P,\nu_Q)\) from Lemma 21. Then \[\varphi(0)=0, \qquad \operatorname{Lip}(\varphi)\le1, \qquad W_1(\nu_P,\nu_Q) = \left| \int\varphi\,d\nu_P-\int\varphi\,d\nu_Q \right|.\] Use the diagonalizations in Equation 1 for \(P\) and \(Q\), written \[\mathfrak D_P = \mathcal S_P\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_{P,1},\ldots,\alpha_{P,d_x})\mathcal S_P^{-1}, \qquad \mathfrak D_Q = \mathcal S_Q\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_{Q,1},\ldots,\alpha_{Q,d_x})\mathcal S_Q^{-1}.\] Let \[\Omega_P:=\{\alpha_{P,j}:1\le j\le d_x\}, \qquad \Omega_Q:=\{\alpha_{Q,j}:1\le j\le d_x\}.\] For any real number \(\zeta\), define the aggregate projectors \[\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta) := \mathcal S_P\operatorname{diag}\{\mathbf 1(\alpha_{P,j}=\zeta):1\le j\le d_x\}\mathcal S_P^{-1},\] and \[\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta) := \mathcal S_Q\operatorname{diag}\{\mathbf 1(\alpha_{Q,j}=\zeta):1\le j\le d_x\}\mathcal S_Q^{-1}.\] The diagonal \(0\)-\(1\) matrices have operator norm at most one, so for \(\zeta_P\in\Omega_P\) and \(\zeta_Q\in\Omega_Q\), \[\|\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\kappa_\star, \qquad \|\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\kappa_\star.\] The projectors extract their spectral values on the appropriate side: \[\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P)\mathfrak D_P = \zeta_P\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P), \qquad \mathfrak D_Q\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q) = \zeta_Q\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q).\] Hence \[\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P)(\mathfrak D_P-\mathfrak D_Q)\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q)\\ &\qquad= (\zeta_P-\zeta_Q)\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P)\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q). \end{aligned}\] In particular, when \(\zeta_P=\zeta_Q\), \[\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P)(\mathfrak D_P-\mathfrak D_Q)\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q)=0.\] Functional calculus with the two diagonalizations gives \[\varphi(\mathfrak D_P) = \sum_{\zeta_P\in\Omega_P}\varphi(\zeta_P)\mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P), \qquad \varphi(\mathfrak D_Q) = \sum_{\zeta_Q\in\Omega_Q}\varphi(\zeta_Q)\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q).\] Since the aggregate projectors sum to the identity for each diagonalization, subtraction and expansion over \(\Omega_P\times\Omega_Q\) yield \[\varphi(\mathfrak D_P)-\varphi(\mathfrak D_Q) = \sum_{\zeta_P\in\Omega_P}\sum_{\zeta_Q\in\Omega_Q} \Xi(\zeta_P,\zeta_Q),\] where \[\Xi(\zeta_P,\zeta_Q) := \begin{cases} 0, &\zeta_P=\zeta_Q,\\[2mm] \dfrac{\varphi(\zeta_P)-\varphi(\zeta_Q)}{\zeta_P-\zeta_Q}\, \mathsf{Proj}_P(\zeta_P)(\mathfrak D_P-\mathfrak D_Q)\mathsf{Proj}_Q(\zeta_Q), &\zeta_P\ne\zeta_Q . \end{cases}\] The equal-spectral-value branch is zero by the preceding vanishing identity. In the unequal branch, the one-Lipschitz property gives \[\left| \frac{\varphi(\zeta_P)-\varphi(\zeta_Q)}{\zeta_P-\zeta_Q} \right|\le1,\] and therefore \[\|\Xi(\zeta_P,\zeta_Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \kappa_\star^2\|\mathfrak D_P-\mathfrak D_Q\|_{\mathrm{op}}.\] Because \(|\Omega_P|\le d_x\) and \(|\Omega_Q|\le d_x\), the triangle inequality gives \[\|\varphi(\mathfrak D_P)-\varphi(\mathfrak D_Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le d_x^2\kappa_\star^2 \|\mathfrak D_P-\mathfrak D_Q\|_{\mathrm{op}}.\] Also, \[\|\varphi(\mathfrak D_P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \kappa_\star L_\tau,\] because \(\varphi(0)=0\), \(\varphi\) is one-Lipschitz, and every eigenvalue of \(\mathfrak D_P\) lies in \([-L_\tau,L_\tau]\).

    Using the representation identity for \(P\) and \(Q\), \[\begin{aligned} W_1(\nu_P,\nu_Q) &\le \|m_X(P)-m_X(Q)\|_2\,\|\varphi(\mathfrak D_P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|e_1\|_2\\ &\quad+ \|m_X(Q)\|_2\, \|\varphi(\mathfrak D_P)-\varphi(\mathfrak D_Q)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|e_1\|_2\\ &\le C_{\mathrm{base}}\,d_S(S(P),S(Q)) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\,d_S(S(P),S(Q)). \end{aligned}\] This proves the asserted modulus on model summaries: \[W_1(\nu_P,\nu_Q) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}d_S(S(P),S(Q)).\]

  5. Let \[\mathscr S:=\{S(P):P\in\mathcal M\}.\] For \(q\in\mathscr S\), choose a representative law \(P_q\in\mathcal M\) with \[S(P_q)=q,\] and define \[F_{\mathscr S}(q):=\nu_{P_q}.\] This definition is independent of the representative. Indeed, if another \(Q\in\mathcal M\) has \(S(Q)=q\), then the modulus just proved gives \[0\le W_1(\nu_{P_q},\nu_Q) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\,d_S(q,q)=0.\] A zero finite transport cost between quotient atomic laws forces the same aggregated mass at each support point, so the two quotient laws are equal. Hence \[F_{\mathscr S}(S(Q))=\nu_Q \qquad(Q\in\mathcal M),\] and, for all \(q,q'\in\mathscr S\), \[W_1(F_{\mathscr S}(q),F_{\mathscr S}(q')) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\,d_S(q,q').\]

  6. Let \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\) be the ambient product metric on the five summary blocks: each matrix block has its Frobenius norm, the mean block has its Euclidean norm, and the finite product uses the maximum of the five block distances. Set \[\Lambda_{\mathrm{sum}}:=4\operatorname{Ent}_{d_z,d_x}+d_x .\] By Lemma 22, \[d_S(q,q') \le \Lambda_{\mathrm{sum}}\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(q,q') \qquad\text{for all summaries }q,q'.\] For a matrix block \(A\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times d_x}\), each entry obeys \[|A_{ab}|\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}},\] so summing the squared entries gives \[\|A\|_{\mathrm F}\le\sqrt{d_zd_x}\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}.\] Since \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\) is the maximum of the four Frobenius block distances and the Euclidean mean-block distance, while \(d_S\) is the sum of the four operator-norm distances and that same mean-block distance, \[\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(q,q') \le \max\{1,\sqrt{d_zd_x}\}\,d_S(q,q') \qquad\text{for all summaries }q,q'.\] (The assumptions \(2\le k\le d_x,d_z\) cover the positive-dimensional case used here; in a zero-dimensional matrix block the unique empty matrix contributes zero.) Thus \(d_S\) and \(\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}\) induce the same topology and the same closures.

    The first comparison and the \(d_S\)-control on \(F_{\mathscr S}\) give \[W_1(F_{\mathscr S}(q),F_{\mathscr S}(q')) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{sum}}\rho_{\mathrm{sum}}(q,q') \qquad(q,q'\in\mathscr S).\] Hence \(F_{\mathscr S}\) is Lipschitz on the summary image in the ambient metric.

    Inside \(\mathcal K=\overline{\mathscr S}\), put \[\mathscr S_{\mathcal K}:=\{q\in\mathcal K:q\in\mathscr S\}.\] The equality of closures just proved makes \(\mathscr S_{\mathcal K}\) dense in \(\mathcal K\). The quotient atomic-law space \(\mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\) is complete for \(W_1\): in labelled coordinates it is the metric quotient of the compact set \[\Delta_{k-1}\times[-L_\tau,L_\tau]^k.\] Apply the dense-set extension theorem to the ambient-Lipschitz map \[\widetilde F_{\mathscr S}(q):=F_{\mathscr S}(q), \qquad q\in\mathscr S_{\mathcal K}.\] The dense-set extension construction gives a Lipschitz, hence continuous, map \[\overline F:\mathcal K\to\mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\] that agrees with \(F_{\mathscr S}\) on \(\mathscr S\), and the uniqueness property of this construction makes it the unique continuous extension with that agreement.

    It remains to transfer the sharper \(d_S\)-control. Fix \(q,q'\in\mathcal K\). Choose sequences \(q_m,q'_m\in\mathscr S\) converging in the ambient metric to \(q,q'\), respectively. Continuity of \(\overline F\) and Lemma 23 give \[W_1(\overline F(q_m),\overline F(q'_m)) \longrightarrow W_1(\overline F(q),\overline F(q')), \qquad d_S(q_m,q'_m)\longrightarrow d_S(q,q').\] For every \(m\), agreement on \(\mathscr S\) and the model-summary control in Equation 2 yield \[W_1(\overline F(q_m),\overline F(q'_m)) = W_1(F_{\mathscr S}(q_m),F_{\mathscr S}(q'_m)) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}d_S(q_m,q'_m).\] Passing to the limit proves \[W_1(\overline F(q),\overline F(q')) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}d_S(q,q') \qquad(q,q'\in\mathcal K).\] Continuity of \(\overline F\), together with the Borel structure on the quotient atomic-law space, makes \(\overline F\) Borel measurable.

  7. For every \(Q\in\mathcal M\), the summary \(S(Q)\) belongs to \(\mathscr S\), and hence \[\overline F(S(Q)) = F_{\mathscr S}(S(Q)) = \nu_Q .\] Let \[G:\mathcal K\to\mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\] be continuous, satisfy the stated \(C_{\mathrm{mod}}\)-Lipschitz bound, and agree with \(Q\mapsto\nu_Q\) on all model summaries. For any \(q\in\mathcal K\), choose \(q_m\in\mathscr S\) with \[q_m\to q .\] Continuity and agreement on \(\mathscr S\) imply \[G(q) = \lim_m G(q_m) = \lim_m F_{\mathscr S}(q_m) = \overline F(q).\] Thus \(G(q)=\overline F(q)\) for all \(q\in\mathcal K\). Combining the model-summary modulus, the continuous extension, the Borel measurability, and this uniqueness proves the theorem.

∎
Proof of Proposition 3.

  1. Write \[\mathcal K=\overline{\mathscr S}^{\,d_S} =\operatorname{cl}_{d_S}\{S(P):P\in\mathcal M\}.\] By Proposition 2, \(\mathcal K\) is compact. Also \(2\le k\) gives \(0<k\), and Definition 26 with \(L\ge 1\) and \(0<\sigma_0\le 1\) gives \(L_\tau\ge 0\). These two inequalities supply the positivity data needed for the atomic-law fallback \(\delta_0\). From Theorem 1 we take a continuous, hence Borel, extension \[\overline F:\mathcal K\longrightarrow \mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\] satisfying, for every admissible model law \(P\), \[\overline F(S(P))=\nu_P .\]

  2. First suppose \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\). Define the selector on the full five-block summary space by \[\Pi(s)=0\qquad\text{for every summary }s .\] This map is Borel. The extension condition over model summaries is vacuous, because any admissible \(P\) would give \[S(P)\in\mathscr S\subseteq \mathcal K,\] contradicting \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\). The nonempty-\(\mathcal K\) nearest-point clause in Algorithm 1 likewise has a false premise. Thus these choices define admissible repair data \(R\). For every observed sample \(\mathsf o_{1:n}\in O_{d_x,d_z}^n\), Algorithm 1 gives \[\widehat\nu_n(\mathsf o_{1:n}) =\delta_0,\] so \(\widehat\nu_n\) is the constant Borel map. In the same case, Algorithm 3 gives the retained set \[\{\delta_0\},\] which is nonempty.

  3. Now suppose \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\). Let \[d_{\mathrm{sum}}=4d_zd_x+d_x\] be the number of real coordinates in a five-block summary, and let \[\Psi_{\mathrm{sum}}(s) =\bigl(s.M_0,s.M_1,s.N_0,s.N_1,s.m_X\bigr)\in\mathbb R^{d_{\mathrm{sum}}}\] be the coordinate homeomorphism from the summary space to Euclidean space. Set \[\mathcal K^\Psi=\Psi_{\mathrm{sum}}(\mathcal K)\] and, for \(x_{\mathrm{Euc}},y_{\mathrm{Euc}}\in\mathbb R^{d_{\mathrm{sum}}}\), define the transported loss \[\ell_\Psi(x_{\mathrm{Euc}},y_{\mathrm{Euc}}) = d_S\!\left(\Psi_{\mathrm{sum}}^{-1}(y_{\mathrm{Euc}}), \Psi_{\mathrm{sum}}^{-1}(x_{\mathrm{Euc}})\right).\] The set \(\mathcal K^\Psi\) is nonempty and compact, and \(\ell_\Psi\) is continuous because \(\Psi_{\mathrm{sum}}\) is a homeomorphism and Lemma 23 gives continuity of \(d_S\). Applying Lemma 2 to \((\mathcal K^\Psi,\ell_\Psi)\), then transporting the selector back through \(\Psi_{\mathrm{sum}}\), gives a Borel map \[\Pi:\text{summary space}\longrightarrow \text{summary space}\] such that, for every summary \(s\), \[\Pi(s)\in\mathcal K \quad\text{and}\quad \forall q_{\mathrm{sum}}\in\mathcal K,\qquad d_S(\Pi(s),s)\le d_S(q_{\mathrm{sum}},s).\] Together with the \(\overline F\) from the first step, this defines repair data \(R\) as in Algorithm 1; the fallback clause has premise \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\), which is false in the present case.

  4. For \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\), the repaired estimator is \[\widehat\nu_n(\mathsf o_{1:n}) = \overline F\!\left(\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o_{1:n}))\right), \qquad \mathsf o_{1:n}\in O_{d_x,d_z}^n.\] The empirical summary map \(\widehat S_n\) is Borel by hypothesis, \(\Pi\) is Borel by the preceding step, and \(\overline F\) is Borel because it is continuous. Hence \(\widehat\nu_n\) is Borel measurable.

  5. It remains in the nonempty case to verify nonemptiness of the retained summary-inversion set. By Definition 45, \[r_{n,\alpha} = C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\alpha)}{n}} .\] Since \(n\ge 1\), \(0<\alpha<1/2\), \(C_0\ge 1\), and \(L\ge 1\), one has \(C_0/\alpha>1\), hence \(\log(C_0/\alpha)>0\), and therefore \[0<r_{n,\alpha} \qquad\text{and}\qquad 0\le 2r_{n,\alpha}.\] For a fixed sample \(\mathsf o_{1:n}\), put \[q_{\mathrm{ret}}=\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o_{1:n})) .\] The selector property gives \(q_{\mathrm{ret}}\in\mathcal K\), and the defining formula for \(d_S\) gives \[d_S(q_{\mathrm{ret}},q_{\mathrm{ret}})=0\le 2r_{n,\alpha}.\] Moreover, \[\widehat\nu_n(\mathsf o_{1:n}) = \overline F(q_{\mathrm{ret}}).\] Thus \(q_{\mathrm{ret}}\) witnesses membership of \(\widehat\nu_n(\mathsf o_{1:n})\) in the displayed retained set, so that set is nonempty for every sample.

  6. The two cases \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\) and \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\) cover all possibilities. In each case the constructed repair data \(R\) has Borel selector \(\Pi\), continuous extension \(\overline F\), Borel repaired estimator \(\widehat\nu_n\), the asserted \(\delta_0\) fallback when \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\), and a nonempty retained summary-inversion set for every sample.

∎
Proof of Proposition 4.

Set \[s_0:=\pi_0\sigma_0^2,\qquad L_\tau:=\frac{4L\sqrt{d_z}}{\sigma_0},\] and order singular values nonincreasingly.

  1. Let \(H_n,q_n,\ell_n\) and \[\mathcal A_n=\mathcal V_n\times\mathcal R_n\times\mathcal P_n\times\mathcal T_n\] be the structured lattice of Definition 13. Its four factors have cardinalities bounded by fixed-parameter constants times \[H_n^{d_xk},\qquad H_n^{k^2},\qquad H_n^{k-1},\qquad H_n^k .\] Since \(n\ge1\), the lattice height satisfies \[H_n\le C_H\sqrt n\] for a finite \(C_H=C_H(k,d_x,\pi_0,\sigma_0)\). Hence there is a constant \(C_\#>0\), depending only on the displayed parameters, such that \[|\mathcal A_n| \le C_\# n^{(d_xk+k^2+2k-1)/2}.\] In the fixed-dimensional exact-real model of Definition 48, the work is the empirical-summary pass plus one fixed-size objective evaluation for each candidate, so \[\operatorname{Work}_n \le C_\#\left\{n+n^{(d_xk+k^2+2k-1)/2}\right\}.\]

  2. Choose \(C_0\ge1\) from Lemma 1. Thus, for every \(P\in\mathcal M\), \(n\ge1\), and \(0<\eta<1/2\), \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) > C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \le\eta .\] The assumptions imply \(d_z>0\), \(L_\tau>0\), and \(s_0>0\). Therefore \[0<\frac{8L_\tau}{s_0} \le C_{\mathrm{lat}} = \max\!\left\{\frac{8L_\tau}{s_0}, (L_\tau+K_D+LK_f)B_{\mathrm{lat}}\right\}.\] Define \[C_{\mathrm{tail}} := \max\!\left\{C_0,\mathrm e,C_{\mathrm{lat}}(C_0L+1)\right\}, \qquad C:=\max\{C_\#,C_{\mathrm{tail}}\}.\] Then \(C>0\).

  3. Fix \(n\ge1\). The lattice factors are finite and nonempty: canonical coordinate columns furnish an element of \(\mathcal V_n\), a rounded scalar multiple of \(I_k\) furnishes an element of \(\mathcal R_n\), the inequality \(k\ell_n\le H_n\) furnishes an element of \(\mathcal P_n\), and \(0\in\mathcal T_n\). Enumerate \(\mathcal A_n\) in duplicate-free lexicographic order as \[\vartheta_i=(V_i,R_i,p_i,\tau_i), \qquad i=1,\ldots,|\mathcal A_n|.\] For a summary \(s\), put \[i_n(s):= \min_{\mathrm{lex}}\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{1\le i\le|\mathcal A_n|} J_n(\vartheta_i;s), \qquad \Lambda_n(s):=\lambda_{\vartheta_{i_n(s)}} ,\] where \(J_n\) is the criterion in Definition 16. Thresholded singular-value pseudoinversion is Borel, each score \(s\mapsto J_n(\vartheta_i;s)\) is Borel, and a finite first-minimizer rule over Borel scores is Borel. Hence \[\widehat\lambda_n(\omega):=\Lambda_n(\widehat S_n(\omega))\] is a total Borel empirical-summary rule, with \(\widehat S_n\) as in Definition 35, and it is the first minimizer prescribed by Algorithm 2. Every coordinate of every \(p_i\in\mathcal P_n\) is at least \(\pi_0\), so aggregating coordinates at repeated effect locations gives \[\operatorname{AtomFloor}(\pi_0,\widehat\lambda_n(\omega))\] for every sample \(\omega\).

  4. Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\), and put \[G_P:=\operatorname{stack}(M_0(P),M_1(P)).\] Lemma 25 gives \(\operatorname{rank}(G_P)=k\) and the threshold characterization at \(S(P)\). In addition, Lemma 24 gives the population signal margin; since the singular values are nonincreasing, \[\sigma_j(G_P)\ge s_0\quad (1\le j\le k), \qquad \sigma_j(G_P)=0\quad (j>k).\] In particular, \(G_P\) has exactly its first \(k\) singular values at least \(s_0/2\). Now let \(s\) satisfy \[d_S(s,S(P))<s_0/2,\] and set \(G_s:=\operatorname{stack}(M_0(s),M_1(s))\). The stacked-block comparison gives \[\|G_s-G_P\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le d_S(s,S(P))<s_0/2.\] Weyl’s inequality yields \[|\sigma_j(G_s)-\sigma_j(G_P)|<s_0/2 \qquad\text{for every }j.\] Thus, for \(1\le j\le k\), \[\sigma_j(G_s)>s_0/2,\] while, for \(j>k\), \[\sigma_j(G_s)<s_0/2.\] This proves that \(G_s\) also has exactly its first \(k\) singular values at least \(s_0/2\) and all remaining singular values below \(s_0/2\).

  5. Fix a sample \(\omega\), and write \[s_\omega:=\widehat S_n(\omega), \qquad \varepsilon_{\omega,P}:=d_S(s_\omega,S(P)).\] Choose the population signal tuple \[B(P)=V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top,\qquad p_{\mathrm{pop}}=(P(U=u))_{u=1}^k,\qquad \tau_{\mathrm{pop}}=(\tau_u(P))_{u=1}^k .\] By Definitions 25 and 21, and because the latent classes partition the record space with positive class masses, \[m_X(P) =E_P[X] =\sum_{u=1}^k P(U=u)E_P[X\mid U=u] =B(P)p_{\mathrm{pop}}.\] The chosen signal factorization \(B(P)=V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top\) therefore gives \[m_X(P)=V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top p_{\mathrm{pop}}.\] Using Assumption 5, the first target-proxy coordinate has conditional mean one in every latent class: \[B(P)^\top e_1 = \bigl(E_P[X_1\mid U=u]\bigr)_{u=1}^k =\mathbf1_k.\] Since \(B(P)^\top=R_{\mathrm{pop}}V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top\), this yields \[R_{\mathrm{pop}}V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top e_1=\mathbf1_k.\] The proxy and outcome moment factorizations from Lemmas 4 and 18 give, for each arm \(t\), \[M_t(P)=A_t\Gamma_tB(P)^\top, \qquad N_t(P)=A_t\Gamma_t\operatorname{diag}(\mu_{tu})B(P)^\top .\] The proxy-rank margin in Assumption 10 supplies injectivity of the linear maps represented by \(A_t\) and \(B(P)\). By Lemma 19, \(\Gamma_t\) is injective, so \(A_t\Gamma_t\) is injective. Applying Lemma 20 with \(C=A_t\Gamma_t\), \(B=B(P)\), and \(\mu=(\mu_{tu})_u\) yields \[M_t(P)^\dagger N_t(P) = \{B(P)^\dagger\}^\top\operatorname{diag}(\mu_{tu})B(P)^\top .\] Subtracting the two arms and using \(\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}=\mu_{1u}-\mu_{0u}\), together with \(B(P)=V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top\), gives \[\mathcal D_P:= M_1(P)^\dagger N_1(P)-M_0(P)^\dagger N_0(P) = V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1} \operatorname{diag}(\tau_{\mathrm{pop}}) R_{\mathrm{pop}}V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top .\] Moreover, \[\sigma_k(R_{\mathrm{pop}})\ge\sigma_0,\qquad \|R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{k}L,\] \[p_{\mathrm{pop},u}\ge2\pi_0,\qquad \sum_{u=1}^k p_{\mathrm{pop},u}=1,\qquad |\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}|\le L_\tau ,\] where the effect envelope is supplied by Lemma 7. The associated quotient law is \[\nu_P=\sum_{u=1}^k p_{\mathrm{pop},u}\delta_{\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}} .\]

  6. Recall that \(q_n=H_n^{-1}\). The definition of \(H_n\) gives the four mesh inequalities \[\sqrt{d_xk}\,q_n\le\frac14, \qquad kq_n\le\frac{\sigma_0}{2}, \qquad kq_n\le\sqrt{k}L, \qquad \lceil\pi_0^{-1}\rceil\le H_n .\] For the signal basis \(V_{\mathrm{pop}}\), each entry has absolute value at most one because its columns are orthonormal. Coordinatewise clipped rounding therefore gives a grid matrix \(V_{n,\mathrm{raw}}\) with \[(V_{n,\mathrm{raw}})_{ij}=q_n z_{ij}\quad (z_{ij}\in\mathbb Z), \qquad |(V_{n,\mathrm{raw}})_{ij}|\le1, \qquad \|V_{n,\mathrm{raw}}-V_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \sqrt{d_xk}\,q_n .\] Weyl’s inequality and the first mesh bound give \[\sigma_k(V_{n,\mathrm{raw}})\ge1-\sqrt{d_xk}\,q_n\ge\frac34\ge\frac12 .\] Let \(V_n^\circ\) be the polar factor \(V_{n,\mathrm{raw}}(V_{n,\mathrm{raw}}^\top V_{n,\mathrm{raw}})^{-1/2}\). The polar-factor perturbation estimate at an orthonormal basis yields \[\|V_n^\circ-V_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le4\sqrt{d_xk}\,q_n=c_Vq_n .\] Next, since \(\|R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{k}L\), coordinatewise clipped rounding in the radius \(2\sqrt{k}L\) gives \(R_n^\circ\) with grid entries and \[\|R_n^\circ-R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le kq_n .\] Using \(kq_n\le\sigma_0/2\) and Weyl’s inequality, \[\sigma_k(R_n^\circ)\ge\sigma_k(R_{\mathrm{pop}})-kq_n \ge\frac{\sigma_0}{2},\] and using \(kq_n\le\sqrt{k}L\), \[\|R_n^\circ\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \|R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|R_n^\circ-R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le2\sqrt{k}L .\] For the weight vector, set \(b_u:=\lfloor H_np_{\mathrm{pop},u}\rfloor\). Since \(p_{\mathrm{pop},u}\ge2\pi_0\) and \(\lceil\pi_0^{-1}\rceil\le H_n\), the quantities \(x:=\pi_0H_n\) and \(y_u:=H_np_{\mathrm{pop},u}\) satisfy \(1\le x\) and \(2x\le y_u\). Because \(y_u< b_u+1\), this implies \(x\le b_u\), hence \[\lceil\pi_0H_n\rceil\le b_u .\] The floor vector satisfies \(\sum_u b_u\le H_n\) and \(H_n\le\sum_u b_u+k\). Choose a subset of indices of size \(H_n-\sum_u b_u\) and add one to \(b_u\) on that subset, obtaining integers \(a_u\) with \[\sum_{u=1}^k a_u=H_n, \qquad \lceil\pi_0H_n\rceil\le a_u, \qquad \left|\frac{a_u}{H_n}-p_{\mathrm{pop},u}\right|\le q_n .\] Thus \(p_n^\circ:=(a_u/H_n)_{u=1}^k\) belongs to \(\mathcal P_n\), and summing the squared coordinate errors gives \[\|p_n^\circ-p_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_2 =\left\{\sum_{u=1}^k\left(\frac{a_u}{H_n}-p_{\mathrm{pop},u}\right)^2\right\}^{1/2} \le\sqrt{k}q_n .\] Finally, since \(|\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}|\le L_\tau\), coordinatewise clipped rounding on the effect grid gives \(\tau_n^\circ\in\mathcal T_n\) with \[|\tau_{n,u}^\circ-\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}|\le q_n \qquad(1\le u\le k).\] Combining these four rounded components gives a well-formed lattice comparator \[\vartheta_n^\circ=(V_n^\circ,R_n^\circ,p_n^\circ,\tau_n^\circ)\in\mathcal A_n\] satisfying \[\|V_n^\circ-V_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le c_Vq_n,\qquad \|R_n^\circ-R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le kq_n,\] \[\|p_n^\circ-p_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_2\le\sqrt{k}q_n,\qquad |\tau_{n,u}^\circ-\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}|\le q_n .\]

  7. Assume first that \[\varepsilon_{\omega,P}<s_0/4 .\] For each treatment arm, \[\|M_t(s_\omega)-M_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\varepsilon_{\omega,P}, \qquad \|N_t(s_\omega)-N_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\varepsilon_{\omega,P}.\] The population armwise proxy block has rank \(k\), kth singular value at least \(s_0\), and \(\|N_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L\). If \(\widetilde M_t(s_\omega)\) denotes the rank-\(k\) truncation retained at threshold \(s_0/2\), singular-value perturbation gives \[(M_t(s_\omega))^\dagger_{\ge s_0/2}=\widetilde M_t(s_\omega)^\dagger, \quad \sigma_k(\widetilde M_t(s_\omega))\ge3s_0/4, \quad \|\widetilde M_t(s_\omega)-M_t(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le2\varepsilon_{\omega,P}.\] Define the thresholded empirical compressed operator, in the notation of Definition 16, by \[\widehat D(s_\omega):= M_1(s_\omega)^\dagger{}_{\ge s_0/2}N_1(s_\omega) - M_0(s_\omega)^\dagger{}_{\ge s_0/2}N_0(s_\omega).\] The equal-rank Moore–Penrose perturbation identity gives \[\|\mathcal U^\dagger-\mathcal V^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le 3\max\{\|\mathcal U^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2,\|\mathcal V^\dagger\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2\} \|\mathcal U-\mathcal V\|_{\mathrm{op}},\] and therefore \[\|\widehat D(s_\omega)-\mathcal D_P\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le A_D\varepsilon_{\omega,P}, \qquad \|m_X(s_\omega)-m_X(P)\|_2\le\varepsilon_{\omega,P}.\] For the comparator \(\vartheta_n^\circ\), the mean residual decomposes as \[V_n^\circ(R_n^\circ)^\top p_n^\circ - V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top p_{\mathrm{pop}} = (V_n^\circ-V_{\mathrm{pop}})(R_n^\circ)^\top p_n^\circ + V_{\mathrm{pop}}(R_n^\circ-R_{\mathrm{pop}})^\top p_n^\circ + V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top(p_n^\circ-p_{\mathrm{pop}}),\] so the bounds \(\|V_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le1\), \(\|p_n^\circ\|_2\le1\), \(\|R_n^\circ\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2\sqrt{k}L\), and \(\|R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{k}L\) give \[\|V_n^\circ(R_n^\circ)^\top p_n^\circ - V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top p_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_2 \le c_mq_n .\] Similarly, \[R_n^\circ(V_n^\circ)^\top e_1 - R_{\mathrm{pop}}V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top e_1 = (R_n^\circ-R_{\mathrm{pop}})V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top e_1 + R_n^\circ(V_n^\circ-V_{\mathrm{pop}})^\top e_1,\] and \(\|e_1\|_2=1\), \(\|V_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le1\), and \(\|R_n^\circ\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2\sqrt{k}L\) yield \[\|R_n^\circ(V_n^\circ)^\top e_1-\mathbf 1_k\|_2\le c_bq_n .\] For the operator residual, write \[\begin{aligned} &D_{\vartheta_n^\circ}-\mathcal D_P \\ &=(V_n^\circ-V_{\mathrm{pop}})(R_n^\circ)^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(\tau_n^\circ)R_n^\circ(V_n^\circ)^\top\\ &\quad+V_{\mathrm{pop}}\{(R_n^\circ)^{-1}-R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\}\operatorname{diag}(\tau_n^\circ)R_n^\circ(V_n^\circ)^\top\\ &\quad+V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(\tau_n^\circ-\tau_{\mathrm{pop}})R_n^\circ(V_n^\circ)^\top\\ &\quad+V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(\tau_{\mathrm{pop}})(R_n^\circ-R_{\mathrm{pop}})(V_n^\circ)^\top\\ &\quad+V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(\tau_{\mathrm{pop}})R_{\mathrm{pop}}(V_n^\circ-V_{\mathrm{pop}})^\top . \end{aligned}\] The inverse identity \[(R_n^\circ)^{-1}-R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1} = (R_n^\circ)^{-1}(R_{\mathrm{pop}}-R_n^\circ)R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\] and the singular-value bounds give \[\|(R_n^\circ)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\frac{2}{\sigma_0}, \qquad \|R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\frac1{\sigma_0}, \qquad \|(R_n^\circ)^{-1}-R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le\frac{2kq_n}{\sigma_0^2}.\] Bounding the five displayed summands with \(\|\operatorname{diag}(\tau_n^\circ)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L_\tau\), \(\|\operatorname{diag}(\tau_{\mathrm{pop}})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le L_\tau\), and \(\|\operatorname{diag}(\tau_n^\circ-\tau_{\mathrm{pop}})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le q_n\) gives \[\|D_{\vartheta_n^\circ}-\mathcal D_P\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le c_Dq_n.\] Consequently \[J_n(\vartheta_n^\circ;s_\omega) \le (A_D+1)\varepsilon_{\omega,P}+c_{\mathrm{grid}}q_n .\] Let \[\widehat\vartheta_n(\omega) =(\widehat V_n,\widehat R_n,\widehat p_n,\widehat\tau_n)\] be the first minimizer. Minimality and the nonnegativity of the three terms in \(J_n\) imply \[\|D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}-\mathcal D_P\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le(2A_D+1)\varepsilon_{\omega,P}+c_{\mathrm{grid}}q_n,\] \[\|\widehat V_n\widehat R_n^\top\widehat p_n-m_X(P)\|_2 \le(A_D+2)\varepsilon_{\omega,P}+c_{\mathrm{grid}}q_n,\] and \[\|\widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top e_1-\mathbf1_k\|_2 \le(A_D+1)\varepsilon_{\omega,P}+c_{\mathrm{grid}}q_n.\] Since \[B_{\mathrm{lat}}=2(A_D+1)+c_{\mathrm{grid}},\] all three residuals are bounded by \[B_{\mathrm{lat}}(\varepsilon_{\omega,P}+q_n).\]

  8. Let \(\varphi_\omega\) be the attaining Kantorovich–Rubinstein potential for \((\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P)\) supplied by Lemma 21. Thus \[\varphi_\omega(0)=0,\qquad \operatorname{Lip}(\varphi_\omega)\le1,\] and \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) = \left| \int\varphi_\omega\,d\widehat\lambda_n(\omega) - \int\varphi_\omega\,d\nu_P \right|.\] The selected structured operator and \(\mathcal D_P\), extended by zero on the orthogonal complement of their signal spaces, have real diagonalizations with spectra in \([-L_\tau,L_\tau]\). Put \[\kappa:=\frac{4\sqrt{k}L}{\sigma_0}.\] For either signal factorization with coordinate matrix \(R_{\mathrm{coord}}\), the ambient eigenbasis uses the signal coordinates and an orthonormal basis on the orthogonal complement. Hence its condition number is bounded by \[\operatorname{cond}\le \max\{\|R_{\mathrm{coord}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}},1\} \max\{\|R_{\mathrm{coord}}\|_{\mathrm{op}},1\}.\] For the selected lattice tuple, the defining lattice constraints give \[\sigma_k(\widehat R_n)\ge\sigma_0/2, \qquad \|\widehat R_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2\sqrt{k}L, \qquad |\widehat\tau_{n,u}|\le L_\tau .\] Therefore \[\|\widehat R_n^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\frac{2}{\sigma_0}, \qquad \max\{\|\widehat R_n^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}},1\}\le\frac{2}{\sigma_0}, \qquad \max\{\|\widehat R_n\|_{\mathrm{op}},1\}\le2\sqrt{k}L,\] where the last two inequalities use \(0<\sigma_0\le1\), \(2\le k\), and \(1\le L\). Thus the selected diagonalization has condition number at most \(\kappa\). For the population tuple, \[\sigma_k(R_{\mathrm{pop}})\ge\sigma_0, \qquad \|R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{k}L, \qquad |\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}|\le L_\tau,\] so the same calculation, with \[\|R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\frac1{\sigma_0}\le\frac2{\sigma_0}, \qquad \max\{\|R_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}},1\}\le2\sqrt{k}L,\] gives the condition-number bound \(\kappa\) for the population diagonalization as well. For the diagonalizations \[D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}=\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{hat}}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}, \qquad \mathcal D_P=\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{pop}}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1},\] the divided-difference identity gives \[\{\varphi_\omega(\Lambda_{\mathrm{hat}})\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}} -\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}\varphi_\omega(\Lambda_{\mathrm{pop}})\}_{ij} = d_{ij}\{\Lambda_{\mathrm{hat}}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}} -\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{pop}}\}_{ij}, \qquad |d_{ij}|\le1,\] with \(d_{ij}=0\) when the two eigenvalues coincide. The coordinate perturbation in the last display is \[\Lambda_{\mathrm{hat}}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}} -\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{pop}} = \mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1} (D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}-\mathcal D_P) \mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}.\] Since \(|d_{ij}|\le1\), each column of the Schur product has Euclidean norm bounded by the corresponding column norm of this coordinate perturbation. The fixed-dimensional column estimate therefore gives \[\left\| \varphi_\omega(\Lambda_{\mathrm{hat}})\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}} -\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}\varphi_\omega(\Lambda_{\mathrm{pop}}) \right\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \sqrt{d_x}\, \left\| \mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1} (D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}-\mathcal D_P) \mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}} \right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.\] Multiplying by the exterior diagonalizer factors yields \[\begin{aligned} \|\varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n})-\varphi_\omega(\mathcal D_P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} &\le \sqrt{d_x}\, \|\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \|\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{hat}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \|D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}-\mathcal D_P\|_{\mathrm{op}} \|\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \|\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \\ &\le \sqrt{d_x}\,\kappa^2 \|D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}-\mathcal D_P\|_{\mathrm{op}}. \end{aligned}\] Because \(\kappa=4\sqrt{k}L/\sigma_0\), this is \[\|\varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n})-\varphi_\omega(\mathcal D_P)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le K_f\|D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}-\mathcal D_P\|_{\mathrm{op}}, \qquad K_f=\frac{16\sqrt{d_x}\,kL^2}{\sigma_0^2}.\] For the selected law, put \[\chi_{\widehat\vartheta,u}:=(\widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top e_1)_u .\] Since \(\widehat V_n^\top\widehat V_n=I_k\) and \(\widehat R_n\widehat R_n^{-1}=I_k\), the functional calculus of the selected structured operator gives \[\varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n}) = \widehat V_n\widehat R_n^{-1} \operatorname{diag}\{\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u}):1\le u\le k\} \widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top,\] because \(\varphi_\omega(0)=0\) on the orthogonal complement. Therefore \[\begin{aligned} &(\widehat V_n\widehat R_n^\top\widehat p_n)^\top \varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n})e_1 \\ &\quad= \widehat p_n^\top\widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top \widehat V_n\widehat R_n^{-1} \operatorname{diag}\{\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u})\} \widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top e_1 \\ &\quad= \widehat p_n^\top \operatorname{diag}\{\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u})\} \widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top e_1 = \sum_{u=1}^k\widehat p_{n,u}\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u})\chi_{\widehat\vartheta,u}. \end{aligned}\] The labelled integral of \(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega)\) is \[\int\varphi_\omega\,d\widehat\lambda_n(\omega) = \sum_{u=1}^k\widehat p_{n,u}\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u}),\] and hence \[\int\varphi_\omega\,d\widehat\lambda_n(\omega) - (\widehat V_n\widehat R_n^\top\widehat p_n)^\top \varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n})e_1 = \sum_{u=1}^k \widehat p_{n,u}\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u})(1-\chi_{\widehat\vartheta,u}).\] Because \(\|\widehat p_n\|_2\le1\) and \[|\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u})| = |\varphi_\omega(\widehat\tau_{n,u})-\varphi_\omega(0)| \le|\widehat\tau_{n,u}| \le L_\tau,\] Cauchy’s inequality yields \[\left| \int\varphi_\omega\,d\widehat\lambda_n(\omega) - (\widehat V_n\widehat R_n^\top\widehat p_n)^\top \varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n})e_1 \right| \le L_\tau\|\widehat R_n\widehat V_n^\top e_1-\mathbf1_k\|_2 .\] For the population tuple, functional calculus gives \[\varphi_\omega(\mathcal D_P) = V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^{-1} \operatorname{diag}\{\varphi_\omega(\tau_{\mathrm{pop}})\} R_{\mathrm{pop}}V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top ,\] since \(\varphi_\omega(0)=0\) on the orthogonal complement. Using \(m_X(P)=V_{\mathrm{pop}}R_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top p_{\mathrm{pop}}\), \(V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top V_{\mathrm{pop}}=I_k\), and \(R_{\mathrm{pop}}V_{\mathrm{pop}}^\top e_1=\mathbf1_k\), we obtain \[m_X(P)^\top\varphi_\omega(\mathcal D_P)e_1 = \sum_{u=1}^k p_{\mathrm{pop},u}\varphi_\omega(\tau_{\mathrm{pop},u}) = \int\varphi_\omega\,d\nu_P .\] Together with \(\|m_X(P)\|_2\le L\) and \[\|\varphi_\omega(D_{\widehat\vartheta_n})e_1\|_2 \le \kappa L_\tau = K_D,\] the residual bounds from the previous step give, in the small-error case, \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) \le (L_\tau+K_D+LK_f)B_{\mathrm{lat}}(\varepsilon_{\omega,P}+q_n).\] If instead \(\varepsilon_{\omega,P}\ge s_0/4\), both laws are supported on \([-L_\tau,L_\tau]\), so the diameter bound from Definition 19 gives \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) \le2L_\tau \le\frac{8L_\tau}{s_0}\varepsilon_{\omega,P}.\] Finally \(q_n\le n^{-1/2}\). Combining the two cases with the definition of \(C_{\mathrm{lat}}\) proves, for every sample, \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) \le C_{\mathrm{lat}} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))+\frac1{\sqrt n} \right\}.\]

  9. The choice \(C\ge C_\#\) gives the displayed candidate-count and work bounds with the same \(C\). For the tail bound, fix \(0<\eta<1/2\). Since \[C\ge C_0,\qquad C\ge\mathrm e,\qquad C\ge C_{\mathrm{lat}}(C_0L+1),\] we have \[\log(C_0/\eta)\le\log(C/\eta), \qquad 1\le\log(C/\eta), \qquad \frac1{\sqrt n}\le\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}}.\] On the event \[d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) \le C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}},\] the deterministic inequality gives \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P) \le C_{\mathrm{lat}}(C_0L+1) \sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} \le C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}}.\] Therefore \[\left\{ W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P)> C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \subseteq \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P))> C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \right\}.\] Applying Lemma 1 under \(Q_P^{(n)}\) yields \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P)> C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \le\eta .\]

∎
Proof of Theorem 2.

  1. Let \[\mathscr S=\{S(P'):P'\in\mathcal M\}, \qquad \mathcal K=\overline{\mathscr S}\] in the finite-dimensional summary space equipped with \(d_S\). By Lemma 1, choose a simultaneous concentration constant \(C_0\ge 1\). By Proposition 4, choose a positive lattice tail constant \(C_{\mathrm{tail}}\) associated with the structured-lattice estimator. By Theorem 1, choose \(C_{\mathrm{mod}}>0\) and a continuous extension \[\overline F:\mathcal K\to \mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\] such that \[W_1\{\overline F(s),\overline F(s')\} \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\,d_S(s,s'), \qquad s,s'\in\mathcal K,\] and \[\overline F(S(Q))=\nu_Q, \qquad Q\in\mathcal M .\] Set \[C= \max\{2C_{\mathrm{tail}},\, \max(2C_0,\,4C_{\mathrm{mod}}C_0L)\}.\] Then \(C>0\), and \(C\) depends on \((k,d_x,d_z,L,\pi_0,\sigma_0)\).

  2. Fix \(n\ge1\). The empirical summary \(\widehat S_n\) is a Borel sample map: its coordinates are finite sums of Borel coordinate maps, treatment-arm indicators, products, and the total empty-arm normalizers in Definition 35. Applying Proposition 4 at this \(n\), choose the structured-lattice estimator \(\widehat\lambda_n\). Its finite list contains each well-formed candidate once, in lexicographic order, and the returned law is \[\widehat\lambda_n(\omega)=\lambda_{\widehat\vartheta_n(\omega)},\] where \(\widehat\vartheta_n(\omega)\) is the first lexicographic minimizer of \(J_n(\vartheta;\widehat S_n(\omega))\) over the structured lattice in Algorithm 2, with threshold \(\pi_0\sigma_0^2/2\). The same result gives Borel measurability of \(\widehat\lambda_n\) and, for every \(P\in\mathcal M\) and every \(0<\zeta<1/2\), \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P)> C_{\mathrm{tail}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{\mathrm{tail}}/\zeta)}{n}} \right\} \le \zeta .\]

  3. Proposition 2 gives compactness of \(\mathcal K\). If \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\), take the selector \[\Pi(s)=0\] for every ambient summary \(s\), and define the repaired estimator by the zero-law branch in Algorithm 1. If \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\), identify the finite-dimensional summary space with Euclidean space. The loss \((s,q)\mapsto d_S(q,s)\) is continuous by Lemma 23, so Lemma 2 gives a Borel map \(\Pi\) such that \[\Pi(s)\in\mathcal K, \qquad d_S(\Pi(s),s)\le d_S(q,s) \quad\text{for all }q\in\mathcal K .\] With this selector, set \[\widehat\nu_n(\omega) = \begin{cases} \delta_0, & \mathcal K=\varnothing,\\ \overline F\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\omega))\}, & \mathcal K\ne\varnothing . \end{cases}\] Continuity of \(\overline F\), Borel measurability of \(\Pi\), and Borel measurability of \(\widehat S_n\) make \(\widehat\nu_n\) a total Borel estimator into \(\mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\).

  4. Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\). Then \(S(P)\in\mathscr S\subseteq\mathcal K\), so \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\). For every sample \(\omega\), nearest-summary optimality gives \[d_S\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\omega)),\widehat S_n(\omega)\} \le d_S\{S(P),\widehat S_n(\omega)\} = d_S\{\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P)\}.\] By the triangle inequality for \(d_S\), \[\begin{aligned} d_S\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\omega)),S(P)\} &\le d_S\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\omega)),\widehat S_n(\omega)\} +d_S\{\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P)\} \\ &\le 2d_S\{\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P)\}. \end{aligned}\] Since \(\overline F(S(P))=\nu_P\), the Lipschitz extension bound gives \[\begin{aligned} W_1(\widehat\nu_n(\omega),\nu_P) &\le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\, d_S\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\omega)),S(P)\} \\ &\le 2C_{\mathrm{mod}}\,d_S\{\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P)\}. \end{aligned}\]

  5. Fix \(0<\eta<1/2\), and define \[\Theta_\lambda = C_{\mathrm{tail}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{\mathrm{tail}}/(\eta/2))}{n}}, \qquad \Theta_S = C_0L \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/(\eta/2))}{n}},\] \[\Theta_C = C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} .\] The definition of \(C\) implies \[2C_{\mathrm{tail}}\le C, \qquad 2C_0\le C, \qquad 4C_{\mathrm{mod}}C_0L\le C .\] Hence \[\frac{C_{\mathrm{tail}}}{\eta/2} = \frac{2C_{\mathrm{tail}}}{\eta} \le \frac{C}{\eta}, \qquad \frac{C_0}{\eta/2} = \frac{2C_0}{\eta} \le \frac{C}{\eta}.\] Monotonicity of \(\log\) and of the square root gives \[\Theta_\lambda\le\Theta_C\] and \[2C_{\mathrm{mod}}\Theta_S\le\Theta_C .\] Therefore \[\begin{aligned} &\left\{ \max\!\left[ W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P), W_1(\widehat\nu_n,\nu_P) \right]>\Theta_C \right\} \\ &\qquad\subseteq \{W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P)>\Theta_\lambda\} \cup \{d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P))>\Theta_S\}. \end{aligned}\] Indeed, on the complement of the right-hand union, the lattice error is at most \(\Theta_\lambda\le\Theta_C\), while the repaired-summary error is at most \(2C_{\mathrm{mod}}\Theta_S\le\Theta_C\).

  6. Apply the lattice tail bound with \(\zeta=\eta/2\) and Lemma 1 with \(\zeta=\eta/2\). The displayed set inclusion and the union bound yield \[\begin{aligned} &Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ \max\!\left[ W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P), W_1(\widehat\nu_n,\nu_P) \right]> C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \\ &\qquad\le Q_P^{(n)}\{W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P)>\Theta_\lambda\} + Q_P^{(n)}\{d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P))>\Theta_S\} \\ &\qquad\le \eta/2+\eta/2 =\eta . \end{aligned}\] The arm-count conclusion is also supplied by Lemma 1: for each \(t\in\{0,1\}\), \[Q_P^{(n)}\{N_{t,n}=0\}\le (1-k\pi_0)^n .\]

∎
Proof of Theorem 3.

  1. By Lemma 1, choose \(C_0\ge1\) such that, for every \(L\ge1\), \(n\ge1\), \(P\in\mathcal M\), and \(0<\alpha<1/2\), \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P))> C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\alpha)}{n}} \right\} \le \alpha .\] Fix \(L\ge1\), and put \[L_\tau=\frac{4L\sqrt{d_z}}{\sigma_0}.\] Let \[\mathcal K=\overline{\{S(P):P\in\mathcal M\}}\] be the summary closure from Definition 43. By Theorem 1, choose \(C_{\mathrm{mod}}>0\) and a continuous extension \[\overline F:\mathcal K\to \mathcal P_k([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\] such that \[W_1\{\overline F(\chi),\overline F(\chi')\} \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}d_S(\chi,\chi') \qquad(\chi,\chi'\in\mathcal K),\] and \[\overline F(S(P))=\nu_P \qquad(P\in\mathcal M).\] By Proposition 4, the prescribed structured-lattice constant \(C_{\mathrm{lat}}\) is positive. For each \(n\ge1\), Proposition 4 supplies a measurable structured-lattice estimator \(\widehat\lambda_n\), generated by the finite exhaustive duplicate-free lexicographic list of well-formed lattice candidates and by the first minimizer of \(J_n(\vartheta;\widehat S_n)\), such that, for every sample \(\mathsf o\in O_{d_x,d_z}^{n}\) and every \(P\in\mathcal M\), \[W_1\bigl(\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o),\nu_P\bigr) \le C_{\mathrm{lat}} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o),S(P))+(\sqrt n)^{-1} \right\}.\] The same cited result gives, for every sample, \[\operatorname{AtomFloor}\bigl(\pi_0,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\bigr).\] For \(0<\alpha<1/2\), define \[r_{n,\alpha} = C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\alpha)}{n}}, \qquad R_{n,\alpha} = C_{\mathrm{lat}}\left(r_{n,\alpha}+(\sqrt n)^{-1}\right).\] The parameter domain gives \(r_{n,\alpha}>0\), and \(C_{\mathrm{lat}}>0\) gives \(R_{n,\alpha}>0\).

  2. Construct the repair selector \(\Pi\). By Proposition 2, \(\mathcal K\) is compact. If \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\), set \[\Pi(s)=0 \qquad\text{for every ambient summary }s.\] If \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\), then Lemma 23 and Lemma 2 give a Borel map \(\Pi\) such that, for every ambient summary \(s\), \[\Pi(s)\in\mathcal K, \qquad d_S(s,\Pi(s))\le d_S(s,q)\quad(q\in\mathcal K).\] Together with \(\overline F\), this is the repair datum of Algorithm 1; the induced repaired summary-to-law map is measurable, and the displayed Lipschitz bound for \(\overline F\) is retained on \(\mathcal K\).

  3. Fix a sample \(\mathsf o\in O_{d_x,d_z}^{n}\). If \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\), then \(\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o)=\{\delta_0\}\), hence \(\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o)\) is nonempty. If \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\), then \(\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\in\mathcal K\), and \[d_S\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o)),\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\} =0 \le 2r_{n,\alpha}.\] Thus \[\overline F\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\}\in\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o),\] so \(\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o)\) is nonempty. The algorithmic set is nonempty because \[\operatorname{AtomFloor}\bigl(\pi_0,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\bigr), \qquad W_1\{\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o),\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\}=0\le R_{n,\alpha}.\] By Algorithm 3, \(m_\star=\pi_0\) and membership in \(\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}\) is membership in the atom-floor subclass together with \[W_1(\nu,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o))\le R_{n,\alpha}.\] By Definition 19, this last inequality is equivalent to the existence of a finite nonnegative transport plan from \(\nu\) to \(\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\) whose absolute-distance cost is at most \(R_{n,\alpha}\). This gives the asserted finite constrained transport representation.

  4. Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\). Since \(S(P)\in\mathcal K\), the closure \(\mathcal K\) is nonempty. Define the bad event \[\mathsf{Bad}_P = \left\{ \mathsf o\in O_{d_x,d_z}^{n}: r_{n,\alpha}<d_S(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o),S(P)) \right\}.\] The concentration bound gives \[Q_P^{(n)}(\mathsf{Bad}_P)\le \alpha .\] The empirical summary is Borel by Definition 35, and \((s,q)\mapsto d_S(s,q)\) is continuous by Lemma 23; hence \(\mathsf{Bad}_P\) is measurable. Therefore \[Q_P^{(n)}(\mathsf{Bad}_P^{\,c}) = 1-Q_P^{(n)}(\mathsf{Bad}_P) \ge 1-\alpha .\] For every \(\mathsf o\in\mathsf{Bad}_P^{\,c}\), \[d_S(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o),S(P))\le r_{n,\alpha}.\] Nearest-summary optimality gives \[d_S\{\widehat S_n(\mathsf o),\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\} \le d_S\{\widehat S_n(\mathsf o),S(P)\} \le r_{n,\alpha},\] and the triangle inequality gives \[\begin{aligned} d_S\{S(P),\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\} &\le d_S\{S(P),\widehat S_n(\mathsf o)\} + d_S\{\widehat S_n(\mathsf o),\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\} \\ &\le 2r_{n,\alpha}. \end{aligned}\] Since \(\overline F(S(P))=\nu_P\), the summary \(S(P)\) itself witnesses \[\nu_P\in\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o).\]

  5. On the same sample \(\mathsf o\in\mathsf{Bad}_P^{\,c}\), Proposition 4 gives \[W_1\{\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o),\nu_P\} \le C_{\mathrm{lat}}\{r_{n,\alpha}+(\sqrt n)^{-1}\} = R_{n,\alpha}.\] By symmetry of \(W_1\), \[W_1\{\nu_P,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\} \le R_{n,\alpha}.\] It remains to verify the atom floor for \(\nu_P\). For each latent class \(u\), Assumption 9 as included in Definition 32 gives \[P(U=u,T=0)\ge\pi_0, \qquad P(U=u,T=1)\ge\pi_0.\] The two treatment cells partition the latent class, so \[p_u=P(U=u)=P(U=u,T=0)+P(U=u,T=1)\ge2\pi_0\ge\pi_0,\] where \(p_u\) is the latent mass from Definition 21. For the raw latent law \[\sum_{u=1}^{k}p_u\delta_{\tau_u},\] the aggregate mass at any distinct support value \(\zeta\) is \[\sum_{u:\tau_u=\zeta}p_u\ge\pi_0,\] because the displayed sum contains at least one latent class. Passing to the quotient law \(\nu_P\) of Definition 40 only aggregates equal atom locations, so Definition 31 gives \[\operatorname{AtomFloor}(\pi_0,\nu_P).\] Thus \[\nu_P\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o).\] Combining the two memberships on \(\mathsf{Bad}_P^{\,c}\), \[1-\alpha \le Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ \nu_P\in \mathcal C_{n,\alpha}\cap\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha} \right\}.\]

  6. Fix a sample \(\mathsf o\). For the theoretical diameter, take any \[\xi_1,\xi_2\in\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o).\] If \(\mathcal K=\varnothing\), then both elements equal the zero-law fallback, so \[W_1(\xi_1,\xi_2)=0\le4C_{\mathrm{mod}}r_{n,\alpha}.\] If \(\mathcal K\ne\varnothing\), then by Algorithm 3 there are \(\chi_1,\chi_2\in\mathcal K\) such that \[\xi_j=\overline F(\chi_j), \qquad d_S\{\chi_j,\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\}\le2r_{n,\alpha} \quad(j=1,2).\] By symmetry and the triangle inequality, \[\begin{aligned} d_S(\chi_1,\chi_2) &\le d_S\{\chi_1,\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o))\} + d_S\{\Pi(\widehat S_n(\mathsf o)),\chi_2\} \\ &\le4r_{n,\alpha}. \end{aligned}\] The Lipschitz bound for \(\overline F\) gives \[W_1(\xi_1,\xi_2) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}d_S(\chi_1,\chi_2) \le 4C_{\mathrm{mod}}r_{n,\alpha}.\] Taking the supremum over all pairs in the nonempty set \(\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o)\) yields \[\operatorname{diam}_{W_1}(\mathcal C_{n,\alpha}) \le 4C_{\mathrm{mod}}r_{n,\alpha}.\]

  7. For the algorithmic diameter, take any \[\xi_1,\xi_2\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o).\] By Algorithm 3, \[W_1\{\xi_1,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\}\le R_{n,\alpha}, \qquad W_1\{\xi_2,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\}\le R_{n,\alpha}.\] By symmetry and the triangle inequality, \[\begin{aligned} W_1(\xi_1,\xi_2) &\le W_1\{\xi_1,\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o)\} + W_1\{\widehat\lambda_n(\mathsf o),\xi_2\} \\ &\le2R_{n,\alpha}. \end{aligned}\] Taking the supremum over all pairs in the nonempty set \(\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}(\mathsf o)\) gives \[\operatorname{diam}_{W_1}(\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}) \le 2R_{n,\alpha}.\]

∎
Proof of Theorem 4.

  1. By Lemma 1, choose \(C_0\) with \(C_0\ge1\) such that, for every \(L\ge1\), \(n\ge1\), \(P\in\mathcal M\), and \(0<\eta<1/2\), \[Q_P^{(n)} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) > C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \le \eta .\] Fix \(L\ge1\). With the displayed definitions of \(L_\tau,s_0,c_V,K_D,A_D,K_f,c_D,c_m,c_b,c_{\mathrm{grid}},B_{\mathrm{lat}}\), Proposition 4 gives \[C_{\mathrm{lat}}>0\] and, for every \(n\ge1\), a Borel structured-lattice estimator \(\widehat\lambda_n\) obtained from the exhaustive duplicate-free lexicographic list of well-formed candidates and the first minimizer of \(J_n(\vartheta;\widehat S_n)\). It also gives, for every sample \(\omega\), \[\operatorname{AtomFloor}(\pi_0,\widehat\lambda_n(\omega)),\] and, for every \(P\in\mathcal M\), \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) \le C_{\mathrm{lat}} \left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))+\frac1{\sqrt n} \right\}.\] The extension \(\overline F\) on \(\mathcal K\) is supplied by Theorem 1. By Proposition 2, \(\mathcal K\) is compact, and by Lemma 23, \((s,q)\mapsto d_S(s,q)\) is continuous. If \(\mathcal K\ne\emptyset\), Lemma 2 supplies a Borel selector \(\Pi\) satisfying \[\Pi(s)\in\mathcal K, \qquad d_S(\Pi(s),s)\le d_S(q,s)\quad(q\in\mathcal K).\] If \(\mathcal K=\emptyset\), set \(\Pi(s)=0\) for every ambient summary \(s\). Together with \(\overline F\), these maps are repair data of the form specified in Algorithm 1; the induced sample-to-law map is Borel measurable by composition in the nonempty case and by the constant fallback in the empty case.

  2. Fix \(0<\alpha<1/2\), \(P\in\mathcal M\), and \(n\ge1\). Define \[r_{n,\alpha} = C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\alpha)}{n}}, \qquad R_{n,\alpha} = C_{\mathrm{lat}}\left(r_{n,\alpha}+\frac1{\sqrt n}\right), \qquad \rho_{n,\alpha}=\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\pi_0}.\] Since \(C_{\mathrm{lat}}>0\), \(C_0\ge1\), \(L\ge1\), \(n\ge1\), and \(0<\alpha<1/2\), we have \[R_{n,\alpha}>0, \qquad \rho_{n,\alpha}>0.\] The event \[E_P=\left\{\omega:d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))\le r_{n,\alpha}\right\}\] is the complement of the deviation event in the first display with \(\eta=\alpha\), and hence \[Q_P^{(n)}(E_P)\ge1-\alpha .\] For every \(\omega\in E_P\), the deterministic lattice bound gives \[W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) \le C_{\mathrm{lat}} \left(r_{n,\alpha}+\frac1{\sqrt n}\right) = R_{n,\alpha}.\]

  3. The representative law \(\nu_P\) has atom floor \(\pi_0\). Indeed, for each latent class \(u\), \[p_u(P)=P(U=u,T=0)+P(U=u,T=1)\ge \pi_0+\pi_0\ge\pi_0\] by the latent-arm positivity component of \(P\in\mathcal M\). After coincident latent effects are aggregated as in Definition 40, every distinct support atom has aggregate mass at least \(\pi_0\). Since \(W_1\) is symmetric on represented atomic laws, the preceding step gives \[W_1(\nu_P,\widehat\lambda_n(\omega))\le R_{n,\alpha}.\] Thus \[\nu_P\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha},\] because Algorithm 3 defines \[\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha} = \left\{ \xi: \operatorname{AtomFloor}(\pi_0,\xi) \ \text{and}\ W_1(\xi,\widehat\lambda_n(\omega))\le R_{n,\alpha} \right\}.\]

  4. We shall use the following finite-transport implication. If two finite atomic probability laws \(\chi\) and \(\chi'\) have atom floor \(m>0\) and \[W_1(\chi,\chi')\le m\rho ,\] then \[\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}\chi')\le\rho \quad(x\in\operatorname{supp}\chi), \qquad \operatorname{dist}(y,\operatorname{supp}\chi)\le\rho \quad(y\in\operatorname{supp}\chi').\] For the first assertion, put \[d_\chi(x):=\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}\chi').\] If \(d_\chi(x)>\rho\), then every coupling in Definition 19 moves the aggregate mass at \(x\), which is at least \(m\), by distance at least \(d_\chi(x)\). Hence every transport cost is at least \(m d_\chi(x)>m\rho\), contradicting the displayed Wasserstein bound. The second assertion follows by applying the same argument after transposing a coupling. Applying this implication with \[m=\pi_0,\qquad \chi=\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\qquad \chi'=\nu_P,\qquad \rho=\rho_{n,\alpha},\] and using \(\pi_0\rho_{n,\alpha}=R_{n,\alpha}\), gives mutual \(\rho_{n,\alpha}\)-closeness of the two supports.

  5. The components \(\widehat{\mathscr K}_{n,\alpha}\) are the connected components of \(\operatorname{supp}(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega))\) under links of length at most \(4\rho_{n,\alpha}\). For such a component \(C\), \[K_C(P) = \{x\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P):\operatorname{dist}(x,C)\le\rho_{n,\alpha}\}.\] The mutual support closeness from the previous step implies that every \(x\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\) belongs to at least one such set. If \(x\) belonged to two components, choose center atoms in the two components within \(\rho_{n,\alpha}\) of \(x\). These two center atoms are within \(2\rho_{n,\alpha}\le4\rho_{n,\alpha}\) of each other, so they lie in the same connected component. Hence \[x\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \exists!\,C\in\widehat{\mathscr K}_{n,\alpha}\ \text{with}\ x\in K_C(P).\] For every component \(C\), reverse support closeness supplies a true atom within \(\rho_{n,\alpha}\) of any center atom in \(C\), so \(K_C(P)\ne\emptyset\). The inclusion \[K_C(P)\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\] is part of the displayed definition of \(K_C(P)\). If \(x\in K_C(P)\), then some \(y\in C\) satisfies \(|x-y|\le\rho_{n,\alpha}\), and therefore \[\min C-\rho_{n,\alpha}\le x\le \max C+\rho_{n,\alpha},\] which is the reported support interval in Algorithm 4.
