Since \(\nu_P\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}\), \[\nu_P(K_C(P))\in \left\{v:\exists \xi\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha},\ v=\xi(K_C(\xi))\right\}.\] Consequently \[\inf_{\xi\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}}\xi(K_C(\xi)) \le \nu_P(K_C(P)) \le \sup_{\xi\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}}\xi(K_C(\xi)),\] which proves the mass-interval containment.

  6. Fix \(\xi\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}\). Then \[\operatorname{AtomFloor}(\pi_0,\xi), \qquad W_1(\xi,\widehat\lambda_n(\omega))\le R_{n,\alpha},\] and the triangle inequality gives \[W_1(\xi,\nu_P) \le W_1(\xi,\widehat\lambda_n(\omega)) + W_1(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P) \le 2R_{n,\alpha}.\] The transport implication applied to \(\xi\) and \(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega)\) shows that their supports are mutually \(\rho_{n,\alpha}\)-close. For a component \(C\), write \[K_C^\xi:=K_C(\xi), \qquad K_C^P:=K_C(P), \qquad \Delta_C^{\mathrm r}:=(\Delta_C(P))_{\mathrm{real}}\] when \(\Delta_C(P)<+\infty\), where \(\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\) is the finite real external gap.

    If \(\Delta_C(P)=+\infty\), then \[\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\setminus K_C^P=\emptyset .\] The partition just proved makes \(C\) the unique empirical component associated with the true support. The same partition argument for \(\xi\) gives \[\operatorname{supp}(\xi)\subseteq K_C^\xi.\] Thus \[\xi(K_C^\xi)=1=\nu_P(K_C^P).\]

    Now suppose \(\Delta_C(P)<+\infty\). The finite sets \(K_C^P\) and \(\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\setminus K_C^P\) are nonempty in this case, so \[\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}>0.\] Let \(\gamma\) be any transport plan from \(\xi\) to \(\nu_P\). If \[\mathscr A_C^\xi:=\{i:x_i^\xi\in K_C^\xi\}, \qquad \mathscr A_C^P:=\{j:x_j^P\in K_C^P\},\] then marginal balance gives \[\xi(K_C^\xi)-\nu_P(K_C^P) = \sum_{i\in\mathscr A_C^\xi}\sum_{j\notin\mathscr A_C^P}\gamma_{ij} - \sum_{i\notin\mathscr A_C^\xi}\sum_{j\in\mathscr A_C^P}\gamma_{ij}.\] Every positive-mass crossing pair in these two sums is separated by at least \(\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}-2\rho_{n,\alpha}\). For the first crossing case \(i\in\mathscr A_C^\xi\), \(j\notin\mathscr A_C^P\), choose \(z\in C\) with \[|x_i^\xi-z|\le\rho_{n,\alpha}.\] Since \(z\) is a center atom, support closeness from the center to \(\nu_P\) gives \(y\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\) with \[|z-y|\le\rho_{n,\alpha}.\] Then \(y\in K_C^P\). Because \(x_j^P\notin K_C^P\), the definition of \(\Delta_C(P)\) gives \[\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\le |y-x_j^P|.\] Also \[|y-x_i^\xi|\le |y-z|+|z-x_i^\xi|\le2\rho_{n,\alpha},\] and therefore \[\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}-2\rho_{n,\alpha} \le |x_i^\xi-x_j^P|.\]

    For the second crossing case \(i\notin\mathscr A_C^\xi\), \(j\in\mathscr A_C^P\), support closeness from \(\xi\) to the center gives a center atom \(z\) with \[|x_i^\xi-z|\le\rho_{n,\alpha}.\] Let \(C_z\) be the empirical component containing \(z\). Support closeness from the center to \(\nu_P\) gives \(y\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\) with \[|z-y|\le\rho_{n,\alpha},\] so \(y\in K_{C_z}(P)\). If \(y\in K_C(P)\), the uniqueness of the true association would force \(C_z=C\); then \(z\in C\), and \[\operatorname{dist}(x_i^\xi,C)\le |x_i^\xi-z|\le\rho_{n,\alpha},\] contradicting \(i\notin\mathscr A_C^\xi\). Hence \[y\notin K_C(P).\] Since \(x_j^P\in K_C(P)\), the external-gap definition yields \[\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\le |x_j^P-y|.\] Together with \[|y-x_i^\xi|\le |y-z|+|z-x_i^\xi|\le2\rho_{n,\alpha},\] this gives \[\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}-2\rho_{n,\alpha} \le |x_i^\xi-x_j^P|.\] The preceding crossing estimate and the displayed marginal identity imply \[(\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}-2\rho_{n,\alpha}) \left|\xi(K_C^\xi)-\nu_P(K_C^P)\right| \le \operatorname{cost}(\gamma).\] Taking an optimal plan and using \(W_1(\xi,\nu_P)\le2R_{n,\alpha}\), if \(4\rho_{n,\alpha}<\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\), then \[\left|\xi(K_C^\xi)-\nu_P(K_C^P)\right| \le \frac{2R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}-2\rho_{n,\alpha}} \le \frac{4R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}}.\] Since \(4\rho_{n,\alpha}=4R_{n,\alpha}/\pi_0<\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\) and \(\pi_0\le1\), this is bounded by \[\frac{4}{\pi_0} \min\left\{1,\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}}\right\}.\] If \(4\rho_{n,\alpha}\ge\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\), then both cluster masses lie in \([0,1]\), so their difference has absolute value at most \(1\). When \(R_{n,\alpha}/\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\ge1\), this is at most \(4/\pi_0\). When \(R_{n,\alpha}/\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}<1\), the inequality \(\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}\le4R_{n,\alpha}/\pi_0\) gives \[\frac{\pi_0}{4}\le\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}},\] and hence \[1\le \frac{4}{\pi_0}\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C^{\mathrm r}}.\] Thus, in all finite-gap cases, \[\left|\xi(K_C(\xi))-\nu_P(K_C(P))\right| \le \frac{4}{\pi_0} \min\left\{1,\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C(P)}\right\},\] with the top-gap case giving equality of the two masses.

  7. For a fixed component \(C\), let \[\operatorname{Val}(C) = \left\{v:\exists \xi\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha},\ v=\xi(K_C(\xi))\right\}.\] The preceding step bounds every \(v\in\operatorname{Val}(C)\) within \[B_C^{\mathrm{err}} := \frac{4}{\pi_0} \min\left\{1,\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C(P)}\right\}\] of \(\nu_P(K_C(P))\) when \(\Delta_C(P)<+\infty\), and within \(0\) when \(\Delta_C(P)=+\infty\). Since \(\nu_P(K_C(P))\in\operatorname{Val}(C)\), the elementary extremal implication \[\bigl(|v-v_0|\le B\ \text{for all }v\in\operatorname{Val}(C),\ v_0\in\operatorname{Val}(C)\bigr) \Longrightarrow \sup\operatorname{Val}(C)-\inf\operatorname{Val}(C)\le2B\] applies to the endpoints of \(I_C\). Therefore, if \(\Delta_C(P)=+\infty\), then \[\operatorname{length}(I_C)=0,\] and, for every component, \[\operatorname{length}(I_C) \le \frac{8}{\pi_0} \min\left\{1,\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\Delta_C(P)}\right\},\] with the displayed right side read as zero when \(\Delta_C(P)=+\infty\).

  8. If \(K_C(P)=\{x\}\), every center atom \(z\in C\) is within \(\rho_{n,\alpha}\) of a true atom \(y\). That true atom satisfies \(y\in K_C(P)\), and hence \(y=x\). Thus \[|z-x|\le\rho_{n,\alpha}\qquad(z\in C).\] Applying this to the minimum and maximum points of the finite component \(C\) gives \[\max C-\min C\le2\rho_{n,\alpha}.\] The reported support interval therefore has length \[(\max C+\rho_{n,\alpha})-(\min C-\rho_{n,\alpha}) \le4\rho_{n,\alpha}.\]

  9. In the same singleton case, the external gap dominates the global effect gap. If \(\Delta_C(P)<+\infty\), then for every \(y\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu_P)\setminus K_C(P)\), \[\delta(P)\le |x-y|,\] because \(x\) and \(y\) are distinct positive-mass support points of \(\nu_P\). Taking the infimum over such \(y\) gives \[\delta(P)\le \Delta_C(P).\] If \(\Delta_C(P)=+\infty\), the preceding zero-width conclusion applies. Since \(R_{n,\alpha}\ge0\) and \(\pi_0>0\), substituting the finite-gap comparison into the external-gap interval bound gives \[\operatorname{length}(I_C) \le \frac{8}{\pi_0} \min\left\{1,\frac{R_{n,\alpha}}{\delta(P)}\right\}.\]

  10. Finally, Definition 19 defines \(W_1\) as the minimum finite transport cost over couplings with the prescribed marginals. Therefore, for every represented atomic law \(\xi\), \[\xi\in\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \operatorname{AtomFloor}(\pi_0,\xi) \ \text{and}\ \exists\gamma\ \text{with transport cost at most }R_{n,\alpha} \text{ from }\xi\text{ to }\widehat\lambda_n(\omega).\] The forward implication takes an optimal finite transport plan attaining \(W_1\); the reverse implication uses that any feasible plan upper-bounds the minimum. This is the finite constrained representation of \(\mathcal C^{\mathrm{alg}}_{n,\alpha}\).

∎
Proof of Theorem 5.

  1. Let \(C_{\mathrm{quo}}>0\) be the constant supplied by Theorem 2. Define \[\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}:=\max\{C_{\mathrm{quo}},2\},\qquad \beta_{\mathrm{mean}} :=\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\sqrt{\log(2\chi_{\mathrm{tail}})} +\frac{\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}^2\sqrt{\pi}}{2},\] \[\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}}:=8+\frac{16}{\pi_0}, \qquad C:=\max\{2,\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}}\beta_{\mathrm{mean}}\}.\] The hypotheses give \(\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\ge2\), \(\log(2\chi_{\mathrm{tail}})>0\), \(\beta_{\mathrm{mean}}>0\), and \(\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}}>0\), hence \(C>0\). Fix \(n\ge1\). By Theorem 2, choose the measurable nearest-summary quotient-law estimator \(\widehat\nu_n\) from Algorithm 1. Set \[\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}(\omega):= p^{\uparrow}\!\left(\widehat\nu_n(\omega)\right), \qquad \omega\in O_{d_x,d_z}^n ,\] which is exactly the ordered-weight estimator of Algorithm 5. Its measurability follows from the measurability of \(\widehat\nu_n\) and the Borel ordered-mass map; its values lie in the probability simplex by the ordered-mass construction, including its barycentric fallback branch.

  2. Fix \(g\) with \(0<g\le1/4\), and fix \(P\in\mathcal M(g)\). Write \[Z_P(\omega):= W_1\!\left(\widehat\nu_n(\omega),\nu_P\right), \qquad \omega\in O_{d_x,d_z}^n ,\] where \(\nu_P\) is the quotient latent-effect law of Definition 40. The support radius \(L_\tau=4L\sqrt{d_z}/\sigma_0\) gives \[0\le Z_P(\omega)\le 2L_\tau\] for every \(\omega\), so \(Z_P\) is integrable under \(Q_P^{(n)}\). For every \(0<\eta<1/2\), the tail bound in Theorem 2 and the inequality \(C_{\mathrm{quo}}\le\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\) give \[Q_P^{(n)}\!\left\{ \chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}/\eta)}{n}} < Z_P \right\} \le \eta .\] Indeed, the displayed event is contained in the corresponding collision-uniform event because \[C_{\mathrm{quo}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{\mathrm{quo}}/\eta)}{n}} \le \chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}/\eta)}{n}} .\] To integrate this tail, put \[b_{\mathrm{mean}}:= \chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2\chi_{\mathrm{tail}})}{n}}, \qquad \varrho_{\mathrm{mean}}:=\frac{n}{\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}^2}.\] For \(x>b_{\mathrm{mean}}\), define \[\eta_x:=\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp(-\varrho_{\mathrm{mean}}x^2).\] Then \(0<\eta_x<1/2\) and \[\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}/\eta_x)}{n}}=x.\] Thus \[Q_P^{(n)}\{Z_P>x\}\le \chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp(-\varrho_{\mathrm{mean}}x^2), \qquad x>b_{\mathrm{mean}}.\] The layer-cake identity and the Gaussian tail bound yield \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}[Z_P] \le b_{\mathrm{mean}} +\chi_{\mathrm{tail}}\int_{b_{\mathrm{mean}}}^{\infty} \exp(-\varrho_{\mathrm{mean}}x^2)\,dx \le \frac{\beta_{\mathrm{mean}}}{\sqrt n}.\]

  3. Let \[\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}} := \bigl((p_u(P))_{u=1}^k,(\tau_u(P))_{u=1}^k\bigr)\] be the \(k\)-slot representative of \(\nu_P\). By Lemma 17, \[p^{\uparrow}(P)=p^{\uparrow}\!\left(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}}\right),\] because \(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}}\) and \(\nu_P\) represent the same aggregated atomic law. The gap-stratum assumptions give, for every latent class \(u\), \[\pi_0\le P(U=u,T=0)\le P(U=u)=p_u(P),\] and Definition 41 gives distinct latent effects and \[\frac g2\le |\tau_u(P)-\tau_v(P)|\qquad (u\ne v).\] Consequently, if \(\xi=\sum_{a=1}^k \widetilde p_a\delta_{\widetilde\tau_a}\) is any valid \(k\)-slot atomic law and \[W_1\!\left(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}},\xi\right) < \frac{\pi_0(g/2)}{4},\] then \[\sum_{j=1}^k \left|p_j^{\uparrow}(\xi)-p_j^{\uparrow}(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}})\right| \le \frac{8}{g}\, W_1\!\left(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}},\xi\right).\] For this last implication, take an optimal transport plan \(\gamma\). Each true atom \(\tau_u(P)\) must have a positive-mass atom \(\widetilde\tau_{\varphi(u)}\) of \(\xi\) within distance \(g/8\); otherwise the mass \(p_u(P)\ge\pi_0\) transported from that atom alone would cost at least \(\pi_0(g/2)/4\). The map \(\varphi\) is injective, hence bijective, because two \(g/8\)-close images of distinct \(g/2\)-separated true atoms would violate the triangle inequality. It also preserves the increasing order. Therefore \[\sum_{j=1}^k \left|p_j^{\uparrow}(\xi)-p_j^{\uparrow}(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}})\right| = \sum_{u=1}^k \left|\widetilde p_{\varphi(u)}-p_u(P)\right|.\] With \[\operatorname{diag}_{\gamma}:=\sum_{u=1}^k\gamma_{u,\varphi(u)}, \qquad \operatorname{off}_{\gamma}:=1-\operatorname{diag}_{\gamma},\] the row and column marginal identities give \[\sum_{u=1}^k \left|\widetilde p_{\varphi(u)}-p_u(P)\right| \le 2\,\operatorname{off}_{\gamma}.\] All off-matching mass moves at least \(g/4\), so \[\frac g4\,\operatorname{off}_{\gamma} \le W_1\!\left(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}},\xi\right),\] and the displayed local stability bound follows.

  4. Apply the preceding bound to the representative \(\xi_\omega\) of \(\widehat\nu_n(\omega)\). Since \(\xi_\omega\) and \(\widehat\nu_n(\omega)\) represent the same law, \[W_1\!\left(\nu_P^{\mathrm{slot}},\xi_\omega\right)=Z_P(\omega).\] If \[Z_P(\omega)<\frac{\pi_0(g/2)}4,\] then \[\sum_{j=1}^k \left|\widehat p_{n,j}^{\uparrow}(\omega)-p_j^{\uparrow}(P)\right| \le \frac{8 Z_P(\omega)}{g} \le \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}} Z_P(\omega)}{g}.\] If instead \(Z_P(\omega)\ge \pi_0(g/2)/4\), the simplex diameter gives \[\sum_{j=1}^k \left|\widehat p_{n,j}^{\uparrow}(\omega)-p_j^{\uparrow}(P)\right| \le 2 \le \frac{16}{\pi_0}\frac{Z_P(\omega)}{g} \le \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}} Z_P(\omega)}{g}.\] Thus, for every sample \(\omega\), \[\sum_{j=1}^k \left|\widehat p_{n,j}^{\uparrow}(\omega)-p_j^{\uparrow}(P)\right| \le \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}} Z_P(\omega)}{g}.\]

  5. Integrating the samplewise inequality and using the mean bound for \(Z_P\), \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \le \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}}}{g}E_{Q_P^{(n)}}[Z_P] \le \kappa_{\mathrm{ord}}\beta_{\mathrm{mean}}\, (\sqrt n\,g)^{-1}.\] Independently, both \(\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}(\omega)\) and \(p^{\uparrow}(P)\) lie in the simplex, hence \[\left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}(\omega)-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1\le2\] for every \(\omega\), and therefore \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right]\le2 .\]

  6. If \((\sqrt n\,g)^{-1}\le1\), the preceding rate bound and \(\kappa_{\mathrm{ord}}\beta_{\mathrm{mean}}\le C\) give \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \le C(\sqrt n\,g)^{-1} = C\min\{1,(\sqrt n\,g)^{-1}\}.\] If \(1\le(\sqrt n\,g)^{-1}\), the simplex-diameter bound and \(2\le C\) give \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \le C = C\min\{1,(\sqrt n\,g)^{-1}\}.\] The constant \(C\) depends only on \(k,d_x,d_z,L,\pi_0,\sigma_0\), and the construction above supplies the required estimator for every \(n\ge1\).

∎
Proof of Proposition 5.

  1. Fix \(0\le \varepsilon\le 1/8\). For \(\mathfrak b\in\{0,1\}\), write \[\operatorname{Ber}_{\theta}(\mathfrak b) = \begin{cases} \theta,& \mathfrak b=1,\\ 1-\theta,& \mathfrak b=0. \end{cases}\] The law in Definition 55 is the finite law on points \[X=(1,\mathfrak x)^\top,\qquad Z=(1,\mathfrak z)^\top,\qquad Y(0)=\mathfrak y_0,\qquad Y(1)=\mathfrak y_1,\qquad Y=Y(T),\] where \(\mathfrak t,\mathfrak x,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak y_0,\mathfrak y_1\in\{0,1\}\). Its elementary mass at \((u,\mathfrak t,\mathfrak x,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak y_0,\mathfrak y_1)\), for \(u\in\{1,2\}\), is \[\theta^U_u \operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^T_u}(\mathfrak t) \operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^X_u}(\mathfrak x) \operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^Z_{u,\mathfrak t}}(\mathfrak z) \operatorname{Ber}_{1/4}(\mathfrak y_0) \operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^1_u}(\mathfrak y_1),\] with \[(\theta^U_1,\theta^U_2)=\left(\frac25,\frac35\right),\quad (\theta^T_1,\theta^T_2)=\left(\frac25,\frac35\right),\quad (\theta^X_1,\theta^X_2)=\left(\frac15,\frac45\right),\] \[\theta^Z_{1,0}=\frac3{10},\quad \theta^Z_{2,0}=\frac7{10},\quad \theta^Z_{1,1}=\frac7{20},\quad \theta^Z_{2,1}=\frac34,\quad \theta^1_1=\frac12-\varepsilon,\quad \theta^1_2=\frac12+\varepsilon .\] The perturbation range gives \[\frac38\le \frac12-\varepsilon\le \frac12, \qquad \frac12\le \frac12+\varepsilon\le \frac58,\] so every displayed Bernoulli factor is a probability mass function. Summing successively over \(\mathfrak y_1,\mathfrak y_0,\mathfrak z,\mathfrak x,\mathfrak t\) leaves \(\theta^U_u\), and \(\theta^U_1+\theta^U_2=1\). Thus \(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\) is a probability law.

  2. The parameter domain in Definition 32 is satisfied because \[2\le 2,\qquad 2\le 2,\qquad 2\le 2,\qquad 1\le 2,\qquad 0<\frac1{10}\le \frac1{2\cdot2},\qquad 0<\frac1{10}\le 1 .\] The finite product form gives the conditional factorization requirements. For bounded measurable test functions \(\psi_Z,\psi_{XY},\psi_X,\psi_{YT},\psi_{Y_t},\psi_T\), \[E[\psi_Z(Z)\psi_{XY}(X,Y)\mid U=u,T=t] = E[\psi_Z(Z)\mid U=u,T=t]\, E[\psi_{XY}(X,Y)\mid U=u,T=t],\] \[E[\psi_X(X)\psi_{YT}(Y,T)\mid U=u] = E[\psi_X(X)\mid U=u]\, E[\psi_{YT}(Y,T)\mid U=u],\] and \[E[\psi_{Y_t}(Y(t))\psi_T(T)\mid U=u] = E[\psi_{Y_t}(Y(t))\mid U=u]\, E[\psi_T(T)\mid U=u].\] These identities give Assumptions 1, 2, and 4. The construction gives \(Y=Y(T)\) and \(X_1=1\) almost surely, which are Assumptions 3 and 5. Since \(X,Z\in\{(1,0)^\top,(1,1)^\top\}\) and \(Y\in\{0,1\}\), \[\|X\|_2\le \sqrt2\le 2,\qquad \|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le \|Z\|_2\|X\|_2\le 2,\qquad \|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le 2,\] so Assumptions 6, 7, and 8 hold with \(L=2\). The latent-arm masses are \[P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(U=1,T=0)=\frac6{25},\quad P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(U=1,T=1)=\frac4{25},\quad P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(U=2,T=0)=\frac6{25},\quad P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(U=2,T=1)=\frac9{25},\] and each is at least \(1/10\), giving Assumption 9. The feature matrices are \[B=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[2pt]1/5&4/5\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[2pt]3/10&7/10\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_1=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\[2pt]7/20&3/4\end{pmatrix}.\] For every \(\upsilon=(\upsilon_1,\upsilon_2)\in\mathbb R^2\), \[\frac1{100}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\le \|B\upsilon\|_2^2,\qquad \frac1{100}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\le \|A_0\upsilon\|_2^2,\qquad \frac1{100}\|\upsilon\|_2^2\le \|A_1\upsilon\|_2^2.\] Indeed, after multiplying the three differences by \(100,100,400\), respectively, the differences equal \[103\left(\upsilon_1+\frac{116}{103}\upsilon_2\right)^2 +\frac{3333}{103}\upsilon_2^2,\] \[108\left(\upsilon_1+\frac{121}{108}\upsilon_2\right)^2 +\frac{1343}{108}\upsilon_2^2,\] and \[445\left(\upsilon_1+\frac{505}{445}\upsilon_2\right)^2 +\frac{4264}{89}\upsilon_2^2.\] Thus the smallest singular value of each of \(B,A_0,A_1\) is at least \(1/10\), proving Assumption 10. Hence \[P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}\in\mathcal M \quad\text{with}\quad L=2,\quad \pi_0=\frac1{10},\quad \sigma_0=\frac1{10}.\]

  3. Lemma 10 gives \[P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(T=0)=\frac{12}{25}=0.48,\qquad P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(T=1)=\frac{13}{25}=0.52 .\] Also, Lemmas 11 and 12 give \[\det(B)=\frac35=0.6,\qquad \det(A_0)=\det(A_1)=\frac25=0.4 .\] It remains to compute the observable proxy moment determinants. For \(\iota,\jmath\in\{1,2\}\) and \(\mathfrak t\in\{0,1\}\), put \[x_1(\mathfrak x)=1,\qquad x_2(\mathfrak x)=\mathfrak x,\qquad z_1(\mathfrak z)=1,\qquad z_2(\mathfrak z)=\mathfrak z .\] After summing out \(\mathfrak y_0\) and \(\mathfrak y_1\), Definition 28 gives the conditional-normalized finite sum \[M_{\mathfrak t}(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_{\iota\jmath} = \frac{ \sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{\mathfrak x,\mathfrak z\in\{0,1\}} \theta^U_u\operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^T_u}(\mathfrak t) \operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^X_u}(\mathfrak x) \operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^Z_{u,\mathfrak t}}(\mathfrak z) z_\iota(\mathfrak z)x_\jmath(\mathfrak x)} {\sum_{u=1}^{2}\theta^U_u\operatorname{Ber}_{\theta^T_u}(\mathfrak t)} .\] Since the \(\mathfrak x\)- and \(\mathfrak z\)-sums are Bernoulli means, this is \[M_{\mathfrak t}(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_{\iota\jmath} = \sum_{u=1}^{2} P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}(U=u\mid T=\mathfrak t)\, A_{\mathfrak t}(\iota,u)B(\jmath,u).\] For \(\mathfrak t=0\), the conditional latent weights are \((1/2,1/2)\), and for \(\mathfrak t=1\) they are \((4/13,9/13)\). Therefore \[M_0(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}) = \begin{pmatrix} 1&1/2\\[2pt] 1/2&31/100 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad M_1(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}) = \begin{pmatrix} 1&8/13\\[2pt] 163/260&142/325 \end{pmatrix}.\] Taking \(2\times2\) determinants, \[\det(M_0(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})) = \frac{31}{100}-\frac14 = \frac3{50} = 0.06,\] and \[\det(M_1(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})) = \frac{142}{325}-\frac8{13}\frac{163}{260} = \frac{216}{4225} = \frac25\cdot\frac35\cdot\frac{36}{169}.\]

  4. Lemma 8 gives \[p_1(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac25,\qquad p_2(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac35,\] and Lemma 9 gives \[\tau_1(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac14-\varepsilon,\qquad \tau_2(P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac14+\varepsilon .\] Since \(\nu_P\) in Definition 40 places latent mass \(p_u\) at latent effect \(\tau_u\), \[\nu_{P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}}} = \frac25\,\delta_{1/4-\varepsilon} + \frac35\,\delta_{1/4+\varepsilon} = 0.4\,\delta_{0.25-\varepsilon} + 0.6\,\delta_{0.25+\varepsilon}.\]

  5. At \(\varepsilon=0\), the singular-value inequalities in the second step give injectivity, hence nonsingularity, of \(B,A_0,A_1\). The determinant identities in the third step give nonsingularity of \(M_0(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})\) and \(M_1(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})\). The quotient-law identity in the fourth step becomes \[\nu_{P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}} = \frac25\,\delta_{1/4}+\frac35\,\delta_{1/4} = \delta_{1/4} = \delta_{0.25}.\] Finally, Lemma 8 gives \[p_1(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac25,\qquad p_2(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac35,\] and these two latent-class masses are distinct.

∎
Proof of Theorem 6.

  1. Define \[a:=\min\left\{\frac1{3200},\frac{\sqrt{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}}{3200}\right\}, \qquad c:=\frac a8, \qquad C:=16000 .\] Since \(0<c_{\mathrm{loc}}<1\), \[0<a\le \frac1{3200}\le\frac18,\qquad c>0,\qquad C>0,\] and \[16000a^2\le c_{\mathrm{loc}}, \qquad 16000a^2\le \frac14\le\frac25 .\] Fix \(n\ge1\), and put \[\varepsilon_n:=\frac{a}{\sqrt n}.\] Then \(0\le\varepsilon_n\le1/8\), and \[16000\varepsilon_n^2 = \frac{16000a^2}{n} \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}.\]

    Let \[\Omega_{\mathrm{vis}}:=\{0,1\}^4, \qquad \zeta=(t,x,z,y)\in\Omega_{\mathrm{vis}},\] and let \[\phi(t,x,z,y):=(t,(1,x)^\top,(1,z)^\top,y)\in O_{2,2},\] with \(O_{2,2}\) as in Definition 18. For \(q\in[0,1]\) write \[\operatorname{Bern}_q(1)=q,\qquad \operatorname{Bern}_q(0)=1-q .\] For \(0\le\varepsilon\le1/8\), define the finite visible witness cell mass by \[\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{\varepsilon}(t,x,z,y) = \sum_{u=1}^{2} p_u\,\operatorname{Bern}_{\theta_u}(t)\, \operatorname{Bern}_{\beta_u}(x)\, \operatorname{Bern}_{\alpha_{t,u}}(z) \begin{cases} \operatorname{Bern}_{1/4}(y),& t=0,\\ \operatorname{Bern}_{1/2+\operatorname{sgn}_u\varepsilon}(y),& t=1, \end{cases}\] where \[(p_1,p_2)=\left(\frac25,\frac35\right),\quad (\theta_1,\theta_2)=\left(\frac25,\frac35\right),\quad (\beta_1,\beta_2)=\left(\frac15,\frac45\right),\] \[(\alpha_{0,1},\alpha_{0,2})=\left(\frac3{10},\frac7{10}\right),\quad (\alpha_{1,1},\alpha_{1,2})=\left(\frac7{20},\frac34\right),\quad (\operatorname{sgn}_1,\operatorname{sgn}_2)=(-1,1).\] The pushforward of this finite law by \(\phi\) is \((P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O\), the observed-data marginal of Definition 20. At \(\varepsilon=0\), every visible cell is bounded below by the first-class contribution: \[\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{0}(\zeta) \ge \frac25\cdot\frac25\cdot\frac15\cdot\frac14\cdot\frac14 = \frac1{500} \ge \frac1{1000}.\] Only the treated outcome factor varies with \(\varepsilon\), and hence \[\left|\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta) -\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{0}(\zeta)\right| \le \varepsilon .\] For Kullback–Leibler divergence as in Definition 53, the elementary finite-carrier comparison is \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathfrak r\Vert\mathfrak s) \le \sum_{\zeta}\frac{(\mathfrak r(\zeta)-\mathfrak s(\zeta))^2}{\mathfrak s(\zeta)}\] whenever \(\mathfrak r\) and \(\mathfrak s\) are probability vectors on a finite carrier and \(\mathfrak s(\zeta)>0\) for every \(\zeta\); this follows from \(\log x\le x-1\). Therefore \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O\Vert(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})_O\right) \le \sum_{\zeta\in\Omega_{\mathrm{vis}}} \frac{\{\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)-\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{0}(\zeta)\}^2} {\psi^{\mathrm{wit}}_{0}(\zeta)} \le 16\cdot1000\,\varepsilon^2 = 16000\varepsilon^2,\] where the first inequality also uses data processing under \(\phi\). Taking \(\varepsilon=\varepsilon_n\), Proposition 5 gives \(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}},P_{\varepsilon_n}^{\mathrm{wit}}\in\mathcal M\), and the displayed KL bound places both laws in \(\mathcal L_n^\nu\) from Definition 56.

  2. We use the following two-point expected-risk inequality. Let \(\mathsf R_1\ll\mathsf R_0\) be one-observation laws with integrable log-likelihood ratio and suppose \[nD_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathsf R_1\Vert\mathsf R_0)\le\frac25 .\] Tensorization and Pinsker’s inequality give \[\left\|\mathsf R_1^{\otimes n}-\mathsf R_0^{\otimes n}\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \sqrt{\frac12D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathsf R_1^{\otimes n}\Vert\mathsf R_0^{\otimes n})} \le\frac12 .\] For an estimator \(\widehat\theta\) in a metric space with targets \(\Theta_1,\Theta_0\), set \[\Delta:=d(\Theta_1,\Theta_0), \qquad \mathcal B_1:=\left\{d(\widehat\theta,\Theta_1)\ge\frac{\Delta}{2}\right\}, \qquad \mathcal B_0:=\left\{d(\widehat\theta,\Theta_0)\ge\frac{\Delta}{2}\right\}.\] The triangle inequality gives \(\mathcal B_1^{c}\subseteq\mathcal B_0\) when \(\Delta>0\), and the case \(\Delta=0\) is immediate. Thus \[\mathsf R_1^{\otimes n}(\mathcal B_1)+\mathsf R_0^{\otimes n}(\mathcal B_0) \ge 1-\left\|\mathsf R_1^{\otimes n}-\mathsf R_0^{\otimes n}\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \ge\frac12,\] so one of the two probabilities is at least \(1/4\). Since \[\frac{\Delta}{2}\,\Pr\!\left\{d(\widehat\theta,\Theta_i)\ge\frac{\Delta}{2}\right\} \le E\,d(\widehat\theta,\Theta_i),\] one of the two expected risks is at least \(\Delta/8\). For simplex-valued estimators the same argument applied to one coordinate gives \[\frac{|p_1(j)-p_0(j)|}{8} \le E_{\mathsf R_1^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\|\widehat p-p_1\|_1\right] \quad\text{or}\quad \frac{|p_1(j)-p_0(j)|}{8} \le E_{\mathsf R_0^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\|\widehat p-p_0\|_1\right],\] because \(|\widehat p(j)-p_i(j)|\le\|\widehat p-p_i\|_1\). The \(W_1\) loss is bounded on the stated compact support radius, and the simplex \(\ell^1\) loss is bounded by \(2\), so the expectations used below are finite.

  3. Apply the two-point inequality with \[\mathsf R_1=(P_{\varepsilon_n}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O, \qquad \mathsf R_0=(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})_O .\] The KL calculation gives \[nD_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathsf R_1\Vert\mathsf R_0) \le 16000a^2 \le \frac25 .\] By Proposition 5, \[\nu_{P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}}=\delta_{1/4}, \qquad \nu_{P_{\varepsilon_n}^{\mathrm{wit}}} = \frac25\delta_{1/4-\varepsilon_n} + \frac35\delta_{1/4+\varepsilon_n}.\] Every coupling from the point mass at \(1/4\) to the second law transports total mass one over distance \(\varepsilon_n\). Hence, using Definition 19, \[W_1\!\left(\nu_{P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}},\nu_{P_{\varepsilon_n}^{\mathrm{wit}}}\right) = \varepsilon_n = \frac a{\sqrt n}.\] Thus every measurable quotient-law estimator \(\widetilde\nu_n\) has one of the two local witness laws satisfying \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[W_1(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_P)\right] \ge \frac{\varepsilon_n}{8} = \frac{c}{\sqrt n}.\] This proves the quotient-law risk assertion.

  4. Fix \(g\in(0,1/4]\), and define \[h:=h(n,g):=a\min\left\{1,\frac1{\sqrt n\,g}\right\},\] the calibrated displacement of Definition 58. Since \(n\ge1\) and \(g>0\), \[x_{n,g}:=\sqrt n\,g>0, \qquad 0<\min\{1,x_{n,g}^{-1}\}\le1.\] Together with \(a>0\), this gives \[0<h\le a\le\frac1{3200}\le\frac1{100}, \qquad |h|\le\frac1{100}.\] In particular \(h\in[-1,1]\), so \(h\) lies in the tangent-amplitude domain. Moreover, \[x_{n,g}\min\{1,x_{n,g}^{-1}\}\le1,\] and therefore \[n g^2 h^2 = a^2\left(x_{n,g}\min\{1,x_{n,g}^{-1}\}\right)^2 \le a^2 .\] Consequently \[16000g^2h^2\le\frac{c_{\mathrm{loc}}}{n}.\] The hypotheses of Lemma 16 are satisfied for \(P_{g,h}\), and also for \(P_{g,0}\). Hence \[P_{g,h},P_{g,0}\in\mathcal L_{n,g}^{p}.\]

  5. We prove the labelled-path KL and ordered-mass certificates. For \((t,x,z,y)\in\Omega_{\mathrm{vis}}\), define \[\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,h}(t,x,z,y) = \begin{cases} \displaystyle \sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{y_0=0}^{1} P_{g,h}(U=u,T=1,X_2=x,Z_2=z,Y(0)=y_0,Y(1)=y), & t=1,\\[1.2em] \displaystyle \sum_{u=1}^{2}\sum_{y_1=0}^{1} P_{g,h}(U=u,T=0,X_2=x,Z_2=z,Y(0)=y,Y(1)=y_1), & t=0. \end{cases}\] The pushforward of this finite law by \(\phi\) is \((P_{g,h})_O\). At \(h=0\), Definition 50 gives \(P_{g,0}=P_{g/2}^{\mathrm{wit}}\). For \(t=0\), the first latent class contributes at least \[\frac6{25}\cdot\frac15\cdot\frac3{10}\cdot\frac14 = \frac{18}{5000} \ge \frac1{1000}.\] For \(t=1\), because \(0\le g\le1/4\), both treated outcome probabilities lie in \([3/8,5/8]\), and the first latent class contributes at least \[\frac4{25}\cdot\frac15\cdot\frac7{20}\cdot\frac38 = \frac{21}{5000} \ge \frac1{1000}.\] Therefore \[\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,0}(t,x,z,y)\ge\frac1{1000} \qquad \text{for all }(t,x,z,y)\in\Omega_{\mathrm{vis}} .\]

    For \(|h|\le1/100\), all denominators appearing in Definition 50 are bounded away from zero; for example \[2/5+h\ge39/100,\qquad 3/5-h\ge59/100,\qquad 15h+4\ge77/20,\qquad 5h+3\ge59/20 .\] Substituting the displayed path probabilities of Definition 50, summing out the inactive potential outcome, and reducing the four \((x,z)\) cases gives \[\sum_{u=1}^{2}P_{g,h}(U=u,T=1,X_2=x,Z_2=z) = \sum_{u=1}^{2}P_{g,0}(U=u,T=1,X_2=x,Z_2=z),\] and \[\begin{aligned} &\sum_{u=1}^{2}\operatorname{sgn}_u P_{g,h}(U=u,T=1,X_2=x,Z_2=z) - \sum_{u=1}^{2}\operatorname{sgn}_u P_{g,0}(U=u,T=1,X_2=x,Z_2=z) \\ &\hspace{3em}= -2\kappa_{xz}h, \end{aligned}\] where \(\operatorname{sgn}_1=-1\), \(\operatorname{sgn}_2=1\), and \[\kappa_{11}=\frac9{100},\qquad \kappa_{10}=\frac3{100},\qquad \kappa_{01}=\frac9{25},\qquad \kappa_{00}=\frac3{25}.\] Since the treated outcome mass is \[\operatorname{Bern}_{1/2+\operatorname{sgn}_u g/2}(y) = \frac12+(2y-1)\frac{\operatorname{sgn}_u g}{2},\] the visible-cell displacement is \[\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,h}(t,x,z,y)-\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,0}(t,x,z,y) = \mathbf 1\{t=1\}(1-2y)\kappa_{xz}gh .\] Thus \[\left|\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,h}(t,x,z,y)-\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,0}(t,x,z,y)\right| \le g|h|.\] The same finite KL–chi-square bound, now applied with the KL divergence of Definition 53, and data processing under \(\phi\) give \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{g,h})_O\Vert(P_{g,0})_O\right) \le \sum_{\zeta\in\Omega_{\mathrm{vis}}} \frac{\{\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,h}(\zeta)-\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,0}(\zeta)\}^2} {\psi^{\mathrm{path}}_{g,0}(\zeta)} \le 16000g^2h^2 = C g^2h^2.\]

    The latent effects on the path are \[\tau_1(P_{g,h})=\frac14-\frac g2, \qquad \tau_2(P_{g,h})=\frac14+\frac g2,\] so their order is fixed for \(g>0\). Also \(|h|\le1/100\) gives \[\frac25+h>0,\qquad \frac35-h>0.\] Using Definition 30, \[p^{\uparrow}(P_{g,h})=\left(\frac25+h,\frac35-h\right), \qquad p^{\uparrow}(P_{g,0})=\left(\frac25,\frac35\right),\] and hence \[\sum_i\left|p_i^{\uparrow}(P_{g,h})-p_i^{\uparrow}(P_{g,0})\right| = |h|+|-h| = 2|h|.\]

  6. Apply the coordinate form of the two-point inequality to \[\mathsf R_1=(P_{g,h})_O, \qquad \mathsf R_0=(P_{g,0})_O,\] and to the ordered coordinate corresponding to the lower effect. The preceding bounds give \[nD_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathsf R_1\Vert\mathsf R_0) \le 16000\,n g^2h^2 \le 16000a^2 \le \frac25,\] and \[\left|p^{\uparrow}_1(P_{g,h})-p^{\uparrow}_1(P_{g,0})\right|=h .\] Therefore every measurable simplex-valued ordered-weight estimator \(\widetilde p_n\) has one of the two local laws satisfying \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[\|\widetilde p_n-p^{\uparrow}(P)\|_1\right] \ge \frac h8 = \frac a8\min\left\{1,\frac1{\sqrt n\,g}\right\} = c\min\left\{1,\frac1{\sqrt n\,g}\right\}.\] The quotient-law certificate follows from the witness KL calculation with \(\varepsilon=\varepsilon_n\): \[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left((P_{a/\sqrt n}^{\mathrm{wit}})_O\Vert(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})_O\right) \le 16000\frac{a^2}{n} \le \frac{16000}{n} = \frac Cn,\] and from \[\frac c{\sqrt n} \le \frac a{\sqrt n} = W_1\!\left(\nu_{P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}},\nu_{P_{a/\sqrt n}^{\mathrm{wit}}}\right).\] The labelled-path membership, KL bound, tangent-amplitude statement, and ordered-mass identity were established above for every \(g\in(0,1/4]\). All four asserted conclusions hold with the constants \(a,c,C\) fixed at the start.

∎
Proof of Proposition 6.

Apply Theorem 6 with local radius \(1/4\). It gives a constant \(c_{\nu}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge 1\) and every measurable quotient-law estimator \(\widetilde\nu_n\), there is a probability law \(P\) in the local quotient experiment \(\mathcal L_n^\nu\) satisfying \[\frac{c_{\nu}}{\sqrt n} \le E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ W_1\!\left(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_P\right) \right].\] By Definition 56, this law belongs to the fixed model class \(\mathcal M\). Hence \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ W_1\!\left(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_P\right) \right] \ge \frac{c_{\nu}}{\sqrt n}.\] Taking the infimum over the same admissible estimators gives \[\inf_{\widetilde\nu_n} \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ W_1\!\left(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_P\right) \right] \ge \frac{c_{\nu}}{\sqrt n}.\]

  1. For the upper bound, Theorem 2 applies at \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), \(L=2\), \(\pi_0=1/10\), and \(\sigma_0=1/10\). Thus there is a constant \(C_{\mathrm{col}}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge 1\), there is a measurable structured-lattice estimator \[\widehat\lambda_n:O_{2,2}^n\to \mathcal P_{\le 2}([-L_\tau,L_\tau])\] and a measurable repair estimator \(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{rep}}\) such that, for every \(P\in\mathcal M\) and every \(0<\eta<1/2\), \[Q_P^{(n)}\!\left\{ \max\!\left( W_1(\widehat\lambda_n,\nu_P), W_1(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{rep}},\nu_P) \right) > C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{\mathrm{col}}/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \le \eta .\] Set \[\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}:=\max\{C_{\mathrm{col}},2\}.\] For \(0<\eta<1/2\), \[C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{\mathrm{col}}/\eta)}{n}} \le \overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}/\eta)}{n}},\] because \(C_{\mathrm{col}}\le\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\), the logarithm is monotone on positive arguments, and \(n\ge1\). Therefore, with \[Z_P(\omega):= W_1\!\left(\widehat\lambda_n(\omega),\nu_P\right), \qquad \omega\in O_{2,2}^n,\] we have the uniform tail bound \[Q_P^{(n)}\!\left\{ Z_P> \overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}/\eta)}{n}} \right\} \le \eta .\]

  2. The one-Wasserstein loss is nonnegative, so \(Z_P(\omega)\ge0\). Define \[\beta_n:= \overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(2\overline C_{\mathrm{col}})}{n}}, \qquad \chi_n:= \frac{n}{\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}^2}.\] Since \(\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\ge2\), both \(\beta_n\) and \(\chi_n\) are positive. For every \(\zeta>\beta_n\), put \[\eta_\zeta:= \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\exp(-\chi_n\zeta^2).\] Then \(0<\eta_\zeta<1/2\), and \[\overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\log(\overline C_{\mathrm{col}}/\eta_\zeta)}{n}} = \overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \sqrt{\frac{\chi_n\zeta^2}{n}} = \zeta .\] Substitution in the preceding tail bound gives \[Q_P^{(n)}\{Z_P>\zeta\} \le \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\exp(-\chi_n\zeta^2), \qquad \zeta>\beta_n .\] Using the nonnegative-tail identity and the trivial bound \(Q_P^{(n)}\{Z_P>\zeta\}\le1\) for \(0<\zeta\le\beta_n\), \[\begin{aligned} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}[Z_P] &\le \beta_n+ \overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \int_{\beta_n}^{\infty}\exp(-\chi_n\zeta^2)\,d\zeta \\ &\le \beta_n+ \overline C_{\mathrm{col}} \int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-\chi_n\zeta^2)\,d\zeta \\ &= \beta_n+ \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\frac{\sqrt{\pi/\chi_n}}{2} \\ &= \frac{ \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\sqrt{\log(2\overline C_{\mathrm{col}})} + \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}^2\sqrt{\pi}/2 }{\sqrt n}. \end{aligned}\]

  3. Let \[C_{\mathrm{mean},\nu}:= \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}\sqrt{\log(2\overline C_{\mathrm{col}})} + \overline C_{\mathrm{col}}^2\sqrt{\pi}/2, \qquad C_{\nu}:=\max\{C_{\mathrm{mean},\nu},\,2c_{\nu}\}.\] The first term is positive, so \(C_{\nu}>0\), and \(c_{\nu}<C_{\nu}\). The estimator \(\widehat\lambda_n\) is admissible for the infimum, and the preceding display holds uniformly in \(P\in\mathcal M\). Hence, for every \(n\ge1\), \[\inf_{\widetilde\nu_n} \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ W_1\!\left(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_P\right) \right] \le \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}[Z_P] \le \frac{C_{\mathrm{mean},\nu}}{\sqrt n} \le \frac{C_{\nu}}{\sqrt n}.\] Taking \(c=c_{\nu}\) and \(C=C_{\nu}\) gives \(0<c<C\) and both displayed minimax inequalities.

∎
Proof of Proposition 7.

For \(n\ge 1\) and \(0<g\le 1/4\), set \[\beta_{n,g}:=\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt n\,g}\right\}.\] Let \(\mathsf{Est}_n\) be the class of measurable simplex-valued labelled weight estimators based on \(n\) observed records in the specialized model \[k=d_x=d_z=2,\qquad L=2,\qquad \pi_0=\frac1{10},\qquad \sigma_0=\frac1{10},\] and define \[\mathsf{Risk}_{n,g} := \inf_{\widetilde p_n\in\mathsf{Est}_n} \sup_{P\in\mathcal M(g)} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widetilde p_n-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right].\]

  1. Apply Theorem 6 with \(c_{\mathrm{loc}}=1/4\). It gives a constant \(\kappa_{\mathrm{lo}}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), every \(0<g\le1/4\), and every \(\widetilde p_n\in\mathsf{Est}_n\), there is a law \(P\in\mathcal L_{n,g}^{p}\) satisfying \[\kappa_{\mathrm{lo}}\beta_{n,g} \le E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widetilde p_n-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right].\] By Definition 57, \(\mathcal L_{n,g}^{p}\subseteq\mathcal M(g)\). Hence, for every admissible \(\widetilde p_n\), \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M(g)} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widetilde p_n-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \ge \kappa_{\mathrm{lo}}\beta_{n,g},\] and taking the infimum over \(\widetilde p_n\in\mathsf{Est}_n\) yields \[\mathsf{Risk}_{n,g}\ge \kappa_{\mathrm{lo}}\beta_{n,g}.\]

  2. Apply Theorem 5 with \[k=d_x=d_z=2,\qquad L=2,\qquad \pi_0=\frac1{10},\qquad \sigma_0=\frac1{10}.\] It gives a constant \(\kappa_{\mathrm{up}}>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), there is an estimator \[\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}\in\mathsf{Est}_n\] satisfying \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \le \kappa_{\mathrm{up}}\beta_{n,g}\] for every \(0<g\le1/4\) and every \(P\in\mathcal M(g)\). The target is the ordered aggregate mass vector of the quotient law \(\nu_P\) from Definition 40: Definition 30 defines it as \(p^{\uparrow}(P)\), and Lemma 17 makes this ordered vector depend only on the represented probability law. Therefore \[\mathsf{Risk}_{n,g} \le \sup_{P\in\mathcal M(g)} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widehat p_n^{\uparrow}-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \le \kappa_{\mathrm{up}}\beta_{n,g}.\]

  3. Define \[c:=\min\left\{\kappa_{\mathrm{lo}},\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{up}}}{2}\right\}, \qquad C:=\kappa_{\mathrm{up}}.\] Since \(\kappa_{\mathrm{lo}}>0\) and \(\kappa_{\mathrm{up}}>0\), \[0<c \quad\text{and}\quad c\le\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{up}}}{2}<\kappa_{\mathrm{up}}=C,\] so \(0<c<C\). Combining the bounds from the preceding two steps gives, for every \(n\ge1\) and \(0<g\le1/4\), \[c\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt n\,g}\right\} \le \mathsf{Risk}_{n,g} \le C\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt n\,g}\right\}.\] This is the asserted minimax inequality.

∎
Proof of Theorem 7.

  1. Apply Theorem 6 with \[c_{\mathrm{loc}}=\frac14,\] which lies in the domain of Definition 49. This gives constants \(a,c\in\mathbb R\), and an auxiliary positive Kullback–Leibler constant, such that \[0<a\le \frac18, \qquad 0<c,\] and all conclusions of Theorem 6 hold for every \(n\ge1\). In the present specialization, \[L_\tau =\frac{4L\sqrt{d_z}}{\sigma_0} =\frac{4\cdot2\sqrt2}{1/10}.\]

    We record the published-scope implication used for the endpoint laws. Let \(P\in\mathcal M\) for \[k=d_x=d_z=2,\qquad L=2,\qquad \pi_0=\sigma_0=\frac1{10}.\] The restrictions in Definition 32 supply the qualitative proxy separations, causal consistency, armwise latent ignorability, anchor normalization, boundedness, latent-arm positivity, and proxy-rank margin. The proxy-rank margin gives \[\sigma_2(A_0)\ge \frac1{10}, \qquad \sigma_2(A_1)\ge \frac1{10}, \qquad \sigma_2(B)\ge \frac1{10},\] so the three proxy feature matrices have full column rank. Latent-arm positivity gives \[P(U=u,T=t)\ge \frac1{10}>0, \qquad u\in\{1,2\},\quad t\in\{0,1\}.\] Thus \(P\) satisfies the qualitative structural conditions in Definition 60. The cited VMW model-scope correspondence, (Virk et al., 2026), identifies these qualitative conditions with membership in the published VMW model predicate attached to Definition 52.

    It remains to verify VMW Assumption 4 in Definition 3. Put \[M_{\mathrm{stack}}:=\operatorname{stack}(M_0(P),M_1(P)).\] Choose a singular-value decomposition \[M_{\mathrm{stack}}=\sum_j s_j\ell_j r_j^\top, \qquad s_1\ge s_2\ge\cdots .\] By Proposition 1, \[s_2(M_{\mathrm{stack}})=\sigma_2(M_{\mathrm{stack}}) \ge \pi_0\sigma_0^2=\frac1{1000}>0.\] Let \(V=(r_1,r_2)\). Then the columns of \(V\) are orthonormal and satisfy \[M_{\mathrm{stack}}^\top M_{\mathrm{stack}}r_j=s_j^2r_j, \qquad j=1,2,\] so \(V\) is a population top-two right singular basis. Since \(s_j>0\), \[r_j=s_j^{-1}M_{\mathrm{stack}}^\top\ell_j, \qquad j=1,2.\] For \(y=(y_0,y_1)\in\mathbb R^{d_z}\oplus\mathbb R^{d_z}\), \[M_{\mathrm{stack}}^\top y=M_0(P)^\top y_0+M_1(P)^\top y_1.\] Hence each \(r_j\) lies in the signal space generated by the two armwise row spaces. The factorization in Proposition 1 gives rank at most \(2\) for this signal space, while \(s_2(M_{\mathrm{stack}})>0\) gives rank at least \(2\). Therefore the span of \(V\) is exactly the signal space. Applying the compressed-margin conclusion of Proposition 1 to this basis gives \[\sigma_2\{M_t(P)V\}\ge \pi_0\sigma_0^2=\frac1{1000}, \qquad t\in\{0,1\}.\] Together with \[\|X\|_2\le2,\qquad \|ZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2,\qquad \|YZX^\top\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2, \qquad P(T=t)\ge2\pi_0=\frac15,\] these are the finite envelope, treatment-arm, top-right-basis, stacked-margin, and compressed-margin clauses of Definition 3, with \[L_X=L_{ZX}=L_{YZX}=2, \qquad \pi=\frac15, \qquad \sigma=\frac1{1000}.\] Thus every such \(P\in\mathcal M\) satisfies VMW Assumption 4.

  2. Fix \(n\ge1\). The quotient-law certificate in Theorem 6, together with Definition 56, supplies probability laws \[P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}, \qquad P_{a/\sqrt n}^{\mathrm{wit}},\] belonging to \(\mathcal M\) with the fixed parameters above. By the implication from the preceding step, both endpoint laws belong to the published VMW model and satisfy VMW Assumption 4.

    At the collision endpoint, Lemma 9 gives \[\tau_1(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac14, \qquad \tau_2(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}})=\frac14.\] The cited recovery-regime correspondence, (Virk et al., 2026), identifies the separated recovery predicate attached to Definition 52, for \(k=d_x=d_z=2\), with the conjunction in Definition 61, including spectral separation of the two latent effects. The displayed equality violates that spectral-separation clause, so \(P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}\) lies outside the published separated recovery regime.

  3. Let \(\mathcal V_n^\nu\) satisfy the quotient-class hypotheses, and choose \[P_{\nu,0}^{\mathcal V},P_{\nu,1}^{\mathcal V}\in\mathcal V_n^\nu\] with \[P_{\nu,0}^{\mathcal V}=P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}, \qquad P_{\nu,1}^{\mathcal V}=P_{a/\sqrt n}^{\mathrm{wit}}.\] For any estimator \(\widetilde\nu_n\) with support radius \(L_\tau\), the quotient-risk conclusion of Theorem 6 gives an endpoint \[P_{\nu,\star}\in \left\{P_0^{\mathrm{wit}},P_{a/\sqrt n}^{\mathrm{wit}}\right\}\] such that \[\frac{c}{\sqrt n} \le E_{Q_{P_{\nu,\star}}^{(n)}}\!\left[ W_1\!\left(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_{P_{\nu,\star}}\right) \right].\] If \(P_{\nu,\star}=P_0^{\mathrm{wit}}\), use \(P_{\nu,0}^{\mathcal V}\); if \(P_{\nu,\star}=P_{a/\sqrt n}^{\mathrm{wit}}\), use \(P_{\nu,1}^{\mathcal V}\). Equality as full-data laws identifies the observed product law and the quotient law, hence \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal V_n^\nu} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ W_1\!\left(\widetilde\nu_n,\nu_P\right) \right] \ge \frac{c}{\sqrt n}.\] This is the quotient lower bound.

  4. Fix \(g\) in the gap-scale domain, so \[0<g\le\frac14,\] and set \[h(n,g):= a\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt n\,g}\right\}.\] The labeled-path certificate in Theorem 6 supplies the tangent-amplitude condition \[h(n,g)\in[-1,1],\] and places \[P_{g,0}, \qquad P_{g,h(n,g)}\] in the local labeled-weight experiment \(\mathcal L_{n,g}^p\). By Definition 57, both laws belong to the gap stratum \(\mathcal M(g)\), and therefore to \(\mathcal M\) with the fixed parameters. The implication from the first step gives published VMW model membership and VMW Assumption 4 for both endpoint laws.

    Let \(P_{\mathrm{end}}\) be either \(P_{g,0}\) or \(P_{g,h(n,g)}\). Since \(P_{\mathrm{end}}\in\mathcal M(g)\), Definition 41 supplies the distinct-effects condition of Assumption 12. Latent-arm positivity gives \[P_{\mathrm{end}}(U=u) \ge P_{\mathrm{end}}(U=u,T=0) \ge \frac1{10}>0, \qquad u\in\{1,2\}.\] Thus both latent classes have positive mass, and the distinct-effects condition yields \[\tau_1(P_{\mathrm{end}})\ne \tau_2(P_{\mathrm{end}}).\] This is the two-class spectral-separation condition in Definition 61.

  5. Let \(\mathcal V_{n,g}^p\) satisfy the labeled-weight comparator-class hypotheses, and choose \[P_{p,0}^{\mathcal V},P_{p,1}^{\mathcal V}\in\mathcal V_{n,g}^p\] with \[P_{p,0}^{\mathcal V}=P_{g,0}, \qquad P_{p,1}^{\mathcal V}=P_{g,h(n,g)}.\] For any ordered-weight estimator \(\widetilde p_n\), the ordered-weight conclusion of Theorem 6 gives an endpoint \[P_{p,\star}\in \left\{P_{g,0},P_{g,h(n,g)}\right\}\] such that \[c\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt n\,g}\right\} \le E_{Q_{P_{p,\star}}^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widetilde p_n-p^{\uparrow}(P_{p,\star})\right\|_1 \right].\] If \(P_{p,\star}=P_{g,0}\), use \(P_{p,0}^{\mathcal V}\); if \(P_{p,\star}=P_{g,h(n,g)}\), use \(P_{p,1}^{\mathcal V}\). Equality as full-data laws preserves the observed product law and the ordered-mass target, hence \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal V_{n,g}^p} E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left\|\widetilde p_n-p^{\uparrow}(P)\right\|_1 \right] \ge c\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt n\,g}\right\}.\] This proves the labeled-weight lower bound.

∎
Proof of Theorem 8.

  1. Put \[D:=4d_zd_x+d_x,\qquad b:=4\sqrt{d_zd_x}+\sqrt{d_x}.\] By Theorem 1, choose \(C_{\mathrm{mod}}>0\) such that, for any two model laws \(P_1,P_2\in\mathcal M\), \[W_1(\nu_{P_1},\nu_{P_2}) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}\,d_S(S(P_1),S(P_2)).\] Let \(C_{\mathrm{card}}>0\) and \(C_{\mathrm{work}}>0\) be the finite-library cardinality and work constants constructed below, and let \(C_0\ge1\) be the constant supplied by Lemma 1. Define \[C_{\mathrm{tail}} := \max\{C_0,\exp(1),C_{\mathrm{mod}}(2C_0L+1)\}, \qquad C:=\max\{C_{\mathrm{card}},C_{\mathrm{work}},C_{\mathrm{tail}}\}.\] Then \(C>0\), and this single constant dominates the constants used for the library size, work bound, and probability bound.

  2. Fix \(n\ge1\), and set \[\epsilon_n:=n^{-1/2},\qquad \delta_n:=\frac{\epsilon_n}{b}.\] The library is the deterministic half-open coordinate-grid library in the \(D\)-coordinate summary box, with side length \(\delta_n\). Its coordinates are the entries of the four \(d_z\times d_x\) matrix blocks and the \(d_x\) entries of the mean block. For each grid cube meeting the admissible image \(\mathscr S=S(\mathcal M)\), choose one representative \(S_i\in\mathscr S\) and order the representatives by the lexicographic order of the cube lower corners. The coordinate bounds following from Proposition 1 place every admissible summary coordinate in \([-L,L]\). If \(q\in\mathscr S\) and \(S_i\) lie in the same cube, then every scalar coordinate differs by at most \(\delta_n\), so \[\|M_t(S_i)-M_t(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \sqrt{d_zd_x}\,\delta_n,\qquad \|N_t(S_i)-N_t(q)\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \sqrt{d_zd_x}\,\delta_n\] for \(t\in\{0,1\}\), and \[\|m_X(S_i)-m_X(q)\|_2\le \sqrt{d_x}\,\delta_n .\] Using \(d_S\) from Definition 33, \[d_S(S_i,q) \le \bigl(4\sqrt{d_zd_x}+\sqrt{d_x}\bigr)\delta_n =\epsilon_n .\] Thus the stored representatives form an \(n^{-1/2}\)-net of \(\mathscr S\); when \(\mathscr S=\varnothing\), the index set is empty and the program uses the fallback law in Algorithm 7.

  3. Each coordinate lower corner has at most \[\left\lceil \frac{2L}{\delta_n}\right\rceil+1\] possible values. Since \(\delta_n=n^{-1/2}/b\) and \(n^{1/2}\ge1\), \[\left\lceil \frac{2L}{\delta_n}\right\rceil+1 \le (2Lb+2)n^{1/2}.\] The lower-corner code is injective, hence \[|A| \le \bigl((2Lb+2)n^{1/2}\bigr)^D =(2Lb+2)^D n^{D/2} \le C_{\mathrm{card}} n^{(4d_zd_x+d_x)/2}.\] After enlarging \(C\), the asserted cardinality bound follows.

  4. Fix a uniform provider of the exact-real primitives. The estimator is the law component of \(\operatorname{NetProgram}(A,\omega)\) in Algorithm 7. On a nonempty library, enumerate the finite index set as \(\iota_0,\ldots,\iota_{m-1}\) and update the current index by choosing the smaller pair \[\bigl(d_S(S_i,s),\operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(i)\bigr)\] in lexicographic order. For each fixed representative, \(s\mapsto d_S(S_i,s)\) is continuous by Lemma 23; therefore every update is Borel, being defined by finitely many weak and strict Borel inequalities. The empty selection is constant. Since \(\widehat S_n\) is Borel by Definition 35, and the selected finite index is mapped to a stored spectral law, \(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}\) is Borel measurable.

  5. The exact-real trace is assembled from the empirical-summary trace, the finite-library search trace, and the selected spectral run: \[\mathfrak T(\omega) = \mathfrak T_{\mathrm{emp}}(n) \,\Vert\, \mathfrak T_{\mathrm{search}}(A) \,\Vert\, \begin{cases} \mathfrak T_{\mathrm{spec}}(S_i), &\text{if the search selects representative }i,\\ \varnothing, &\text{if the search has empty selection.} \end{cases}\] Thus the returned law and the displayed trace come from the same execution.

  6. The empirical-summary trace has \(nD\) arithmetic operations. Each library comparison uses \(D\) scalar arithmetic operations, four fixed-dimensional singular-value or operator-norm operations, and one comparison. The selected spectral tail has three singular-value operations, one root-isolation operation, and one arithmetic operation. Therefore, for every sample \(\omega\), \[\operatorname{ops}(\omega) \le nD+|A|(D+5)+5 .\] Combining this with the cardinality bound gives \[\operatorname{ops}(\omega) \le C_{\mathrm{work}}\{n+n^{D/2}\} \le C\{n+n^{(4d_zd_x+d_x)/2}\}.\]

  7. If the program selects \(i\) at sample \(\omega\), put \[s_\omega:=\widehat S_n(\omega).\] The exhaustive fold preserves domination, where \(i\) dominates \(j\) at \(s_\omega\) when \[d_S(S_i,s_\omega)\le d_S(S_j,s_\omega)\] and equality implies \[\operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(i) \le \operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(j).\] Induction over the scanned list yields, for every library index \(j\), \[d_S(S_i,\widehat S_n(\omega)) \le d_S(S_j,\widehat S_n(\omega)),\] and \[d_S(S_i,\widehat S_n(\omega)) = d_S(S_j,\widehat S_n(\omega)) \Longrightarrow \operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(i) \le \operatorname{rank}_{\mathrm{lex}}(j).\]

  8. Let \(\mathcal R_i\) be the exact spectral run at the selected representative \(S_i\). By the definition of the program output, \[\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}(\omega)=\operatorname{law}(\mathcal R_i).\]

  9. The same spectral execution supplies a basis \(V_i\), its signal spans, and an eigenvalue list \((\lambda_{i,r})_{r<k}\). Its output fields give, for each \(t\in\{0,1\}\), \[x\longmapsto M_t(S_i)V_i x\] injective, the exact threshold characterization \[\frac{\pi_0\sigma_0^2}{2} \le \sigma_j\!\left(\operatorname{stack}(M_0(S_i),M_1(S_i))\right) \Longleftrightarrow j<k,\] and completeness of the returned eigenvalue list: \[z\in\operatorname{spec}(\Delta_i) \Longrightarrow \exists r<k\ \text{such that }z=\lambda_{i,r},\] where \(\Delta_i\) is the compressed operator formed from \(S_i\), \(V_i\), and the run’s spans.

  10. Fix any stored representative \(S_i\). Since \(S_i\in\mathscr S\), choose \(Q_i\in\mathcal M\) with \[S(Q_i)=S_i.\] Let \(p_{i,u}:=Q_i(U=u)\), write \(p_i:=(p_{i,u})_{u=1}^k\), and set \[\tau_{i,u}:=\mu_{1u}(Q_i)-\mu_{0u}(Q_i).\] The latent-arm positivity condition in Assumption 9 gives \(p_{i,u}>0\) for every \(u\). Let \(\mathcal R_i\) be the exact spectral run at \(S_i\), with signal basis \(V_i\), spans, anchors \(a_i,c_i\), returned eigenvalues \((\lambda_{i,r})_{r<k}\), and compressed operator \[\Delta_i :=(M_1(S_i)V_i)^\dagger N_1(S_i)V_i -(M_0(S_i)V_i)^\dagger N_0(S_i)V_i .\] Set \[\Omega_i:=B(Q_i)^\top V_i\in\mathbb R^{k\times k}, \qquad \Gamma_{i,t} :=\operatorname{diag}\{Q_i(U=u\mid T=t):1\le u\le k\},\] and \[\Phi_{i,t}:=A_t(Q_i)\Gamma_{i,t}\in\mathbb R^{d_z\times k}.\] Lemmas 4 and 18 give, for \(t\in\{0,1\}\), \[M_t(S_i)V_i=\Phi_{i,t}\Omega_i, \qquad N_t(S_i)V_i =\Phi_{i,t} \operatorname{diag}(\mu_{t1}(Q_i),\ldots,\mu_{tk}(Q_i))\Omega_i .\] The run’s armwise full-rank certificate makes \(x\mapsto M_t(S_i)V_ix\) injective for each \(t\). Taking \(t=0\), the first factorization shows that \(\Omega_i\) is injective and hence invertible. The same factorization also gives injectivity of \(\Phi_{i,0}\): if \(\Phi_{i,0}v=\Phi_{i,0}v'\) for \(v,v'\in\mathbb R^k\), then \[M_0(S_i)V_i\Omega_i^{-1}v = \Phi_{i,0}v = \Phi_{i,0}v' = M_0(S_i)V_i\Omega_i^{-1}v',\] so injectivity of \(M_0(S_i)V_i\) gives \(\Omega_i^{-1}v=\Omega_i^{-1}v'\), and hence \(v=v'\). The proxy factorization before postmultiplication by \(V_i\) is \[M_0(S_i)=\Phi_{i,0}B(Q_i)^\top .\] Since \(\Phi_{i,0}\) is injective, its Moore–Penrose inverse is a left inverse on its range, and therefore \[\Phi_{i,0}^\dagger M_0(S_i)=B(Q_i)^\top .\] Transposing gives \[B(Q_i)=M_0(S_i)^\top(\Phi_{i,0}^\dagger)^\top .\] By construction of the run’s span certificate, \[\operatorname{range}(V_i) = \operatorname{range}(M_0(S_i)^\top)+\operatorname{range}(M_1(S_i)^\top) \subseteq\mathbb R^{d_x},\] and consequently \[\operatorname{range}(B(Q_i)) \subseteq \operatorname{range}(M_0(S_i)^\top) \subseteq \operatorname{range}(V_i).\] The second factorization can therefore be rewritten as \[N_t(S_i)V_i = M_t(S_i)V_i \{\Omega_i^{-1} \operatorname{diag}(\mu_{t1}(Q_i),\ldots,\mu_{tk}(Q_i))\Omega_i\}.\] Since the Moore–Penrose inverse is a left inverse on the range of an injective linear map, \[(M_t(S_i)V_i)^\dagger N_t(S_i)V_i = \Omega_i^{-1} \operatorname{diag}(\mu_{t1}(Q_i),\ldots,\mu_{tk}(Q_i))\Omega_i .\] Subtracting the two treatment-arm identities gives \[\Delta_i = \Omega_i^{-1} \operatorname{diag}(\tau_{i,1},\ldots,\tau_{i,k}) \Omega_i .\] Finally, the law of total expectation gives \[m_X(S_i)=B(Q_i)p_i .\] The column-space inclusion just proved implies that the orthogonal projector \(V_iV_i^\top\) fixes \(B(Q_i)\). Since \(\Omega_i^\top=V_i^\top B(Q_i)\), \[B(Q_i)=V_iV_i^\top B(Q_i)=V_i\Omega_i^\top .\] Thus \[a_i=V_i^\top m_X(S_i)=\Omega_i^\top p_i .\] The target-proxy anchor condition gives \(B(Q_i)^\top e_1=\mathbf 1_k\), while \(c_i=V_i^\top e_1\) and \(B(Q_i)^\top V_iV_i^\top=B(Q_i)^\top\), so \[\Omega_i c_i=\mathbf 1_k .\] For the polynomial projector attached to a returned slot \(r\), \[\mathcal P_{i,r} := \prod_{\lambda_{i,\ell}\ne\lambda_{i,r}} (\lambda_{i,r}-\lambda_{i,\ell})^{-1} (\Delta_i-\lambda_{i,\ell}I_k),\] the diagonalization gives \[\mathcal P_{i,r} = \begin{cases} \Omega_i^{-1} \operatorname{diag}\{\mathbf 1(\tau_{i,u}=\lambda_{i,r})\}_{u=1}^k \Omega_i, &\lambda_{i,r}\in\{\tau_{i,u}:1\le u\le k\},\\ 0,&\lambda_{i,r}\notin\{\tau_{i,u}:1\le u\le k\}. \end{cases}\] Hence the returned slot weight \[w_{i,r}:=a_i^\top\mathcal P_{i,r}c_i\] satisfies \[w_{i,r} = \sum_{u:\tau_{i,u}=\lambda_{i,r}}p_{i,u}\] for a spectral value and \(w_{i,r}=0\) for a listed value outside the spectrum. The output validity of \(\mathcal R_i\) gives \(w_{i,r}\ge0\) and \(\sum_{r<k}w_{i,r}=1\). Completeness gives at least one returned slot for each latent-effect value, and the positivity of the corresponding cluster mass \[m_i(\lambda):=\sum_{u:\tau_{i,u}=\lambda}p_{i,u}\] forces exactly one positive returned slot for each distinct latent-effect value; otherwise the total \(\sum_{r<k}w_{i,r}\) would exceed \(\sum_{\lambda}m_i(\lambda)=1\). Therefore, for every bounded test function \(\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\), \[\sum_{r<k}w_{i,r}\varphi(\lambda_{i,r}) = \sum_{u=1}^k p_{i,u}\varphi(\tau_{i,u}).\] Thus the induced atomic measures agree: \[\sum_{r<k}w_{i,r}\delta_{\lambda_{i,r}} = \sum_{u=1}^kp_{i,u}\delta_{\tau_{i,u}} = \nu_{Q_i},\] with coincident effects aggregated as in Definition 40. The exact spectral run at \(S_i\) returns \(\nu_{Q_i}\): \[\operatorname{law}(\mathcal R_i)=\nu_{Q_i}.\]

  11. The validity certificate of the same result-bearing spectral execution states \[w_{i,r}\ge0,\qquad \sum_{r<k}w_{i,r}=1,\qquad |\lambda_{i,r}|\le L_\tau \quad(r<k).\] Consequently the returned weights form a probability vector and every returned atom lies in \([-L_\tau,L_\tau]\), so the represented finite atomic measure is a probability law supported on that interval.

  12. For a stored representative \(S_i=S(Q_i)\), put \[\mathcal G_i:=\operatorname{stack}(M_0(S_i),M_1(S_i)), \qquad s_0:=\pi_0\sigma_0^2 .\] Lemma 25 supplies the rank identity, and Lemma 24 supplies the singular-value margin: \[\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal G_i)=k, \qquad s_0\le \sigma_{k-1}(\mathcal G_i).\] If \(H\in\mathbb R^{2d_z\times d_x}\) satisfies \[\|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}<s_0/2,\] Weyl’s singular-value inequality gives, for every \(r\), \[|\sigma_r(\mathcal G_i+H)-\sigma_r(\mathcal G_i)| \le \|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}.\] Thus \[\sigma_r(\mathcal G_i+H)>s_0/2\quad(r<k),\] while \(\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal G_i)=k\) gives \(\sigma_r(\mathcal G_i)=0\) for \(r\ge k\), and hence \[\sigma_r(\mathcal G_i+H)<s_0/2\quad(r\ge k).\] Consequently the perturbed stacked moment satisfies the two threshold clauses \[\frac{s_0}{2}\le \sigma_r(\mathcal G_i+H)\quad(r<k), \qquad \sigma_r(\mathcal G_i+H)<\frac{s_0}{2}\quad(r\ge k).\] Equivalently, exactly \(k\) singular values of \(\mathcal G_i+H\) meet the threshold \(s_0/2=\pi_0\sigma_0^2/2\).

  13. Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\) and a sample \(\omega\). If \(P\in\mathcal M\), then \(\mathscr S\) is nonempty, so the library has a selected nearest index \(i\). By the covering property, choose \(j\) such that \[d_S(S_j,S(P))\le n^{-1/2}.\] Nearest-library optimality and the triangle inequality yield \[\begin{aligned} d_S(S_i,S(P)) &\le d_S(S_i,\widehat S_n(\omega)) +d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))\\ &\le d_S(S_j,\widehat S_n(\omega)) +d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))\\ &\le d_S(S_j,S(P)) +2d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))\\ &\le n^{-1/2}+2d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P)). \end{aligned}\] By Equations 3 and 4, \[\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}(\omega)=\nu_{Q_i}, \qquad S(Q_i)=S_i.\] Applying the modulus from Theorem 1 gives \[W_1\!\left(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}}(\omega),\nu_P\right) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}} \left\{ 2d_S(\widehat S_n(\omega),S(P))+n^{-1/2} \right\}.\]

  14. Fix \(0<\eta<1/2\). Since \(C\ge C_0\), \(C\ge\exp(1)\), and \(\eta\le1\), \[1\le \log(C/\eta), \qquad \log(C_0/\eta)\le \log(C/\eta).\] Therefore \[n^{-1/2}\le \sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}}, \qquad \sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \le \sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}}.\] On the event \[d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) \le C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}},\] the deterministic inequality gives \[W_1(\widehat\nu_n^{\mathrm{net}},\nu_P) \le C_{\mathrm{mod}}(2C_0L+1) \sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}} \le C\sqrt{\frac{\log(C/\eta)}{n}}.\] Thus the bad event in the theorem is contained in \[\left\{ d_S(\widehat S_n,S(P)) > C_0L\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_0/\eta)}{n}} \right\}.\] By Lemma 1, its \(Q_P^{(n)}\)-probability is at most \(\eta\).

∎
